“盖斯潘可不是那种能攒下钱的人，”他说，“女人和赌博早就把他所有的工钱榨得精光。就在上周，‘商业咖啡馆’的老板还跑来大吵大闹讨要欠账，甚至扬言要去向伯爵告状呢。”
察觉到自己这番话的分量，男仆似乎为了挽回几分，连忙补充道：
“我跟盖斯潘并没有什么私仇；在今天之前，我一直觉得他是个挺聪明利落的家伙，虽说过分爱搞恶作剧；而且考虑到他的出身，他未免有些傲气——”
“你可以退下了，”法官打断了弗朗索瓦先生的夸夸其谈；男仆恭敬地退了出去。
在这番盘问过程中，盖斯潘逐渐回过了神。预审法官、普朗塔和镇长目不转睛地审视着他脸上的每一丝微表情，他此刻根本没有冷静的心力去伪装掩饰，而医生则按着他的脉搏，默数着跳动的次数。
“这是良心受谴，害怕惩罚，”镇长低声嘀咕道。
“是蒙冤无辜，却无法自证清白，”普朗塔低声回应道。
多米尼先生听到了这两句低语，却仿佛未曾听见一般。他尚未形成定论，也不希望任何人能从他的一言一行中揣测出他的倾向。
“你觉得好些了吗，朋友？”让德龙医生关切地问盖斯潘。
可怜的人点了点头。接着，他环顾四周，目光惶恐得宛如一个人在丈量自己刚刚跌落的万丈深渊，他用手抹了抹眼睛，结结巴巴地说：
“给口水喝吧！”
有人端来了一杯水，他一饮而尽，脸上流露出极其舒畅的神情。随后他挣扎着站了起来。

===Original===

“Are you now in a fit state to answer me?” asked the judge.
Guespin staggered a little, then drew himself up. He continued erect before the judge, supporting himself against a table. The nervous trembling of his hands diminished, the blood returned to his cheeks, and as he listened, he arranged the disorder of his clothes.
“You know the events of this night, don’t you?” commenced the judge; “the Count and Countess de Trémorel have been murdered. You went away yesterday with all the servants of the château; you left them at the Lyons station about nine o’clock; you have just returned, alone. Where have you passed the night?”
Guespin hung his head and remained silent.
“That is not all,” continued M. Domini; “yesterday you had no money, the fact is well known; one of your fellow-servants has just proved it. Today, one hundred and sixty-seven francs are found in your wallet. Where did you get this money?”
The unhappy creature’s lip moved as if he wished to answer; a sudden thought seemed to check him, for he did not speak.
“More yet. What is this card of a hardware establishment that has been found in your pocket?”
Guespin made a sign of desperation, and stammered:
“I am innocent.”
“I have not as yet accused you,” said the judge of instruction, quickly. “You knew, perhaps, that the count received a considerable sum yesterday?”
A bitter smile parted Guespin’s lips as he answered:
“I know well enough that everything is against me.”
There was a profound silence. The doctor, the mayor, and Plantat, seized with a keen curiosity, dared not move. Perhaps nothing in the world is more thrilling than one of these merciless duels between justice and a man suspected of a crime. The questions may seem insignificant, the answers irrelevant; both questions and answers envelop terrible, hidden meanings. The smallest gesture, the most rapid movement of physiognomy may acquire deep significance, a fugitive light in the eye betray an advantage gained; an imperceptible change in the voice may be confession.
The coolness of M. Domini was disheartening.
“Let us see,” said he after a pause: “where did you pass the night? How did you get this money? And what does this address mean?”
“Eh!” cried Guespin, with the rage of powerlessness, “I should tell you what you would not believe.”
The judge was about to ask another question, but Guespin cut him short.

===Chinese===

“你现在能清醒地回答我的问题了吗？”法官问。
盖斯潘踉跄了一下，随即挺直了身子。他倚靠着桌子，笔直地站在法官面前。双手神经质的颤抖渐渐平复，血色重新回到了双颊，他一边听着，一边整理着凌乱不堪的衣服。
“你清楚昨晚发生的事，对吧？”法官开门见山地说，“特雷莫雷尔伯爵和伯爵夫人遇害了。你昨天同城堡里的所有仆人一起出发；晚上9点左右在里昂火车站离开了他们；而你刚刚才独自返回。昨晚你究竟在哪儿过的夜？”
盖斯潘垂着头，默不作声。
“这还不是全部，”多米尼先生继续追问，“昨天你身无分文，这是人尽皆知的事实；你的一位同事刚才已经证实了这一点。而今天，在你的皮夹里搜出了167法郎。这笔钱你是从哪儿弄来的？”
这个不幸的人嘴唇蠕动着，仿佛想要辩白；但某种突如其来的念头似乎阻拦了他，他终究没有吭声。
“还有。从你口袋里翻出的这张五金店的名片，又是怎么回事？”
盖斯潘绝望地挥了一下手，结结巴巴地说：
“我是无辜的。”
“我还没有指控你，”预审法官语气平缓却敏锐地说，“你或许事先知道伯爵昨天收到了一大笔巨款吧？”
一抹苦涩的冷笑掠过盖斯潘的唇角，他答道：
“我心里很清楚，现在一切证据都对我极其不利。”
四周陷入了一片死寂。医生、镇长和普朗塔怀着强烈的好奇心，连大气都不敢喘。世间或许再也没有什么比司法与嫌疑人之间这种毫不留情的殊死决斗更令人屏息凝神了。提问看似漫不经心，回答看似答非所问；然而这一问一答之间，却包藏着可怕而隐秘的杀机。最微不足道的手势，最转瞬即逝的面部抽动，都可能蕴含着深意；眼中掠过的一抹亮光可能泄露了一记致命破绽；嗓音中一丝难以察觉的颤抖可能就是无声的招供。
多米尼先生的冷酷令人不寒而栗。
“我们接着说吧，”稍作停顿后他说，“昨晚你究竟在哪儿过的夜？这笔钱你是从哪儿来的？这张地址又代表什么？”
“唉！”盖斯潘带着无能为力的狂怒失声喊道，“就算我说了，你们也绝不会相信的。”
法官刚要继续发问，盖斯潘却粗暴地打断了他。

===Original===

“No; you wouldn’t believe me,” he repeated, his eyes glistening with anger. “Do men like you believe men like me? I have a past, you know, of antecedents, as you would say. The past! They throw that in my face, as if, the future depended on the past. Well, yes; it’s true, I’m a debauchee, a gambler, a drunkard, an idler, but what of it? It’s true I have been before the police court, and condemned for night poaching﻿—what does that prove? I have wasted my life, but whom have I wronged if not myself? My past! Have I not sufficiently expiated it?”
Guespin was self-possessed, and finding in himself sensations which awoke a sort of eloquence, he expressed himself with a savage energy well calculated to strike his hearers.
“I have not always served others,” he continued; “my father was in easy circumstances﻿—almost rich. He had large gardens, near Saumur, and he passed for one of the best gardeners of that region. I was educated, and when sixteen years old, began to study law. Four years later they thought me a talented youth. Unhappily for me, my father died. He left me a landed property worth a hundred thousand francs: I sold it out for sixty thousand and went to Paris. I was a fool then. I had the fever of pleasure-seeking, a thirst for all sorts of pastimes, perfect health, plenty of money. I found Paris a narrow limit for my vices; it seemed to me that the objects of my desires were wanting. I thought my sixty thousand francs would last forever.”
Guespin paused; a thousand memories of those times rushed into his thoughts and he muttered:
“Those were good times.”
“My sixty thousand francs,” he resumed, “held out eight years. Then I hadn’t a sou, yet I longed to continue my way of living. You understand, don’t you? About this time, the police, one night, arrested me. I was ‘detained’ six months. You will find the records of the affair at the prefecture. Do you know what it will tell you? It will tell you that on leaving prison I fell into that shameful and abominable misery which exists in Paris. It will tell you that I have lived among the worst and lowest outcasts of Paris﻿—and it is the truth.”
The worthy mayor was filled with consternation.
“Good Heaven!” thought he, “what an audacious and cynical rascal! and to think that one is liable at any time to admit such servants into his house!”
The judge held his tongue.

===Chinese===

“不；你们绝不会相信我的，”他重复道，眼中闪烁着愤怒的火焰，“像你们这样的人，何曾相信过像我们这样的人？你们知道我有前科，照你们的话说，有劣迹。过去！人们总是把过去狠狠摔在我脸上，仿佛未来完全由过去决定似的。好吧，没错；这是事实，我是个浪荡子、赌徒、酒鬼、游手好闲之徒，可那又怎么样？没错，我曾被送上过轻罪法庭，因夜间非法偷猎被判过刑——可这能证明什么？我虚度了自己的一生，但除了我自己，我又伤害过谁？我的过去！难道我赎的罪还不够深重吗？”
盖斯潘恢复了自制力，内心涌动的激烈情感赋予了他某种雄辩的气势，他以一种极具野性力量的口吻倾诉着，深深震撼了在座的每一个人。
“我并不总是在伺候别人，”他继续说道，“我的父亲曾经家境宽裕——几乎称得上富有。他在索米尔（Saumur）附近拥有大片苗圃，被公认为那片地区最出色的园艺家之一。我受过良好的教育，16岁时就开始攻读法律。四年后，大家都夸我是个才华横溢的年轻人。不幸的是，我父亲去世了。他给我留下了价值10万法郎的田产：我以60000法郎贱卖了产业，动身前往巴黎。那时的我真是个蠢货。我发了疯似地追逐享乐，对各种消遣欲壑难填，身强力壮，手里又有大把钞票。我甚至觉得巴黎的天地太小，容不下我的放纵；在我看来，能满足我欲望的花样还远远不够。我以为自己的60000法郎能挥霍一辈子。”
盖斯潘顿了顿；对往昔岁月的无数回忆涌上心头，他喃喃自语道：
“那真是一段快活的日子。”
“我的60000法郎，”他接着说，“撑了整整8年。后来我一个子儿都不剩了，却依然巴望着维持以往的排场。你们懂的，不是吗？大约就在那个时候，一天夜里，警察逮捕了我。我被‘拘禁’了6个月。你们去警察厅能查到案卷记录。你们知道卷宗上会怎么写吗？上面会写着，我一走出监狱大门，就堕入了巴黎最下流、最可憎的穷困潦倒之中。上面会写着，我整日同巴黎最卑劣、最底层的无赖渣滓混在一起——而这全都是千真万确的。”
可敬的镇长大惊失色。
“天哪！”他暗自思忖，“多么肆无忌惮、恬不知耻的恶棍！想想看，人们随时都可能把这样的下人招进自家宅邸！”
预审法官始终不动声色。

===Original===

He knew that Guespin was in such a state that, under the irresistible impulse of passion, he might betray his innermost thoughts.
“But there is one thing,” continued the suspected man, “that the record will not tell you; that, disgusted with this abject life, I was tempted to suicide. It will not tell you anything of my desperate attempts, my repentance, my relapses. At last, I was able in part to reform. I got work; and after being in four situations, engaged myself here. I found myself well off. I always spent my month’s wages in advance, it’s true﻿—but what would you have? And ask if anyone has ever had to complain of me.”
It is well known that among the most intelligent criminals, those who have had a certain degree of education, and enjoyed some good fortune, are the most redoubtable. According to this, Guespin was decidedly dangerous. So thought those who heard him. Meanwhile, exhausted by his excitement, he paused and wiped his face, covered with perspiration.
M. Domini had not lost sight of his plan of attack.
“All that is very well,” said he, “we will return to your confession at the proper time and place. But just now the question is, how you spent your night, and where you got this money.”
This persistency seemed to exasperate Guespin.
“Eh!” cried he, “how do you want me to answer? The truth? You wouldn’t credit it. As well keep silent. It is a fatality.”
“I warn you for your own sake,” resumed the judge, “that if you persist in refusing to answer, the charges which weigh upon you are such that I will have you arrested as suspected of this murder.”
This menace seemed to have a remarkable effect on Guespin. Great tears filled his eyes, up to that time dry and flashing, and silently rolled down his cheeks. His energy was exhausted; he fell on his knees, crying:
“Mercy! I beg you, Monsieur, not to arrest me; I swear I am innocent, I swear it!”
“Speak, then.”
“You wish it,” said Guespin, rising. Then he suddenly changed his tone. “No, I will not speak, I cannot! One man alone could save me; it is the count; and he is dead. I am innocent; yet if the guilty are not found, I am lost. Everything is against me. I know it too well. Now, do with me as you please; I will not say another word.”
Guespin’s determination, confirmed by his look, did not surprise the judge.

===Chinese===

他心知肚明，盖斯潘此刻正处于一种极度激动的状态，在无法遏制的情感冲动下，很可能会失口吐露心底最深处的隐秘。
“但有一件事，”嫌疑人继续说道，“卷宗上绝不会告诉你们；那就是我对那种下贱的生活厌恶透顶，甚至一度企图自杀。卷宗绝不会记载我曾经做出过怎样的绝望挣扎、我的悔恨以及我的重蹈覆辙。最后，我终于设法改邪归正了一部分。我找到了营生；在换了四处雇主之后，我受雇来到了这里。我觉得在这里过得很称心。确实，我总是提前把当月的工钱预支花光——可这又能怪谁呢？你们大可去打听打听，有谁曾对我挑出过半点不是。”
众所周知，在形形色色的聪明罪犯中，那些受过一定教育、曾经过惯优渥生活的人往往是最难对付的。照此推论，盖斯潘无疑是一个极度危险的人物。在场的所有人心中都闪过这样的念头。与此同时，由于情绪激动耗尽了体力，他停了下来，擦拭着汗流浃背的脸庞。
多米尼先生丝毫没有偏离自己的审讯方略。
“这一切都很好，”他说，“关于你的自白，我们稍后在合适的场合再作讨论。但眼下最关键的问题是，昨晚你究竟是如何度过的，这笔钱你又是从何而来的。”
这种步步紧逼的追问似乎彻底激怒了盖斯潘。
“哎！”他失声喊道，“你们到底想让我怎么回答？说真话吗？你们根本就不会信。还不如闭嘴不说。这就是命数。”
“为了你自己的前途着想，我不得不警告你，”法官严厉地说，“如果你继续负隅顽抗拒绝交代，凭着压在你身上的重重嫌疑，我将不得不以谋杀嫌疑人的罪名将你依法逮捕。”
这番威吓似乎对盖斯潘产生了异乎寻常的震动。大颗大颗的泪水涌上了那双原本干涸而喷射着怒火的眼眶，无声地顺着双颊滚落。他的强硬意志瓦解了；他扑通一声双膝跪倒在地，痛哭哀求道：
“开恩啊！我求求您，先生，千万别逮捕我；我发誓我是无辜的，我指天发誓！”
“那就如实招来。”
“这可是你们逼我的，”盖斯潘站起身来说。然而他的语气突然骤变：“不，我绝不会说的，我不能说！世上只有一个人能救我；那就是伯爵；可他已经死了。我是无辜的；但要是真凶抓不到，我就全完了。一切都对我极其不利。这一点我心知肚明。现在，你们想怎么处置我就怎么处置吧；我半个字也不会再吐了。”
盖斯潘决绝的神色和眼神，并未让预审法官感到意外。

===Original===

“You will reflect,” said he, quietly, “only, when you have reflected, I shall not have the same confidence in what you say as I should have now. Possibly,” and the judge spoke slowly and with emphasis, “you have only had an indirect part in this crime; if so﻿—”
“Neither indirect nor direct,” interrupted Guespin; and he added, violently, “what misery! To be innocent, and not able to defend myself.”
“Since it is so,” resumed M. Domini, “you should not object to be placed before Mme. de Trémorel’s body?”
The accused did not seem affected by this menace. He was conducted into the hall whither they had fetched the countess. There, he examined the body with a cold and calm eye. He said, simply:
“She is happier than I; she is dead, she suffers no longer; and I, who am not guilty, am accused of her death.”
M. Domini made one more effort.
“Come, Guespin; if in any way you know of this crime, I conjure you, tell me. If you know the murderers, name them. Try to merit some indulgence for your frankness and repentance.”
Guespin made a gesture as if resigned to persecution. “By all that is most sacred,” he answered, “I am innocent. Yet I see clearly that if the murderer is not found, I am lost.”
Little by little M. Domini’s conviction was formed and confirmed. An inquest of this sort is not so difficult as may be imagined. The difficulty is to seize at the beginning; in the entangled skein, the main thread, which must lead to the truth through all the mazes, the ruses, silence, falsehoods of the guilty. M. Domini was certain that he held this precious thread. Having one of the assassins, he knew well that he would secure the others. Our prisons, where good soup is eaten, and good beds are provided, have tongues, as well as the dungeons of the medieval ages.
The judge ordered the brigadier to arrest Guespin, and told him not to lose sight of him. He then sent for old Bertaud. This worthy personage was not one of the people who worry themselves. He had had so many affairs with the men of law, that one inquisition the more disturbed him little.
“This man has a bad reputation in my commune,” whispered the mayor to M. Domini.
Bertaud heard it, however, and smiled.
Questioned by the judge of instruction, he recounted very clearly and exactly what had happened in the morning, his resistance, and his son’s determination.

===Chinese===

“你会想明白的，”法官平静地说，“不过，等你反复权衡之后，我对你供词的信任，可就不会像现在这么充分了。也许，”法官语气缓慢而加重分量，“你在这起罪案中仅仅扮演了从犯的角色；如果是这样的话——”
“既非从犯，也非主犯，”盖斯潘粗暴地打断了他，狂怒地补充道，“真是造化弄人！明明是无辜的，却连自辩都做不到。”
“既然如此，”多米尼先生说，“那么把你带到特雷莫雷尔夫人的遗体前辨认，你总该不会有异议吧？”
被告似乎对这种威慑无动于衷。他被押解到了安放伯爵夫人的大厅。在那里，他用冷漠而平静的目光注视着遗体。他只是淡淡地说了句：
“她比我幸运；她死了，再也不用受苦受难了；而我，明明问心无愧，却要背上谋杀她的罪名。”
多米尼先生做了最后一次努力。
“好了，盖斯潘；如果你对这起罪案有任何耳闻目睹，我恳求你，如实告诉我。如果你知道凶手是谁，把名字说出来。争取以你的坦白和悔罪换取从宽处理。”
盖斯潘做出一个甘受迫害的无奈手势。“凭着人间最神圣的一切，”他回答道，“我是清白无辜的。但我心里雪亮，要是抓不到真凶，我就死路一条。”
渐渐地，多米尼先生心中的确信形成了，并且坚不可摧。这种刑事侦讯并不像外界想象的那样高深莫测。难点在于能否在纷乱如麻的线索开头抓住那一根主线，凭借它穿透罪犯设下的所有迷宫、诡计、沉默与谎言，直抵真相。多米尼先生深信自己已经紧紧攥住了这根珍贵的线头。既然抓获了一名凶犯，他深知其余的党羽也插翅难飞。我们那些提供热汤软床的现代监牢，其撬开犯人口风的能耐绝不下于中世纪阴森的地牢。
法官命令宪兵班长正式逮捕盖斯潘，并严令片刻不得让他脱离视线。随后他传唤了老贝尔托。这位体面的人物可不是那种遇事发愁的主儿。他同官府打交道的次数实在太多了，多一次盘问根本激不起他半点波澜。
“这人在我们公社名声极坏，”镇长凑近多米尼先生耳语道。
贝尔托却听了个真切，咧嘴笑了笑。
面对预审法官的盘问，他极为清晰而准确地讲述了今早发生的一切，自己的百般阻拦，以及儿子的执拗坚持。

===Original===

He explained the reason for the falsehood they told; and here again the chapter of antecedents came up.
“Look here; I’m better than my reputation, after all,” said he. “There are many folks who can’t say as much. You see many things when you go about at night﻿—enough.”
He was urged to explain his allusions, but in vain.
When he was asked where and how he had passed the night, he answered, that having left the cabaret at ten o’clock, he went to put down some traps in Mauprévoir wood; and had gone home and to bed about one o’clock.
“By the by,” added he, “there ought to be some game in those traps by this time.”
“Can you bring a witness to prove that you went home at one?” asked the mayor, who bethought him of the count’s clock, stopped at twenty minutes past three.
“Don’t know, I’m sure,” carelessly responded the poacher, “it’s quite likely that my son didn’t wake up when I went to bed.”
He added, seeing the judge reflect:
“I suspect that you are going to imprison me until the murderers are discovered. If it was winter, I wouldn’t complain much; a fellow is well off in prison then, for it’s warm there. But just at the time for hunting, it’s provoking. It will be a good lesson for that Philippe; it’ll teach him what it costs to render a service to gentlefolks.”
“Enough!” interrupted M. Domini, sternly. “Do you know Guespin?”
This name suddenly subdued the careless insolence of the marauder; his little gray eyes experienced a singular restlessness.
“Certainly,” he answered in an embarrassed tone, “we have often made a party at cards, you understand, while sipping our ‘gloria.’ ”1
The man’s inquietude struck the four who heard him. Plantat, especially, betrayed profound surprise. The old vagabond was too shrewd not to perceive the effect which he produced.
“Faith, so much the worse!” cried he: “I’ll tell you everything. Every man for himself, isn’t it? If Guespin has done the deed, it will not blacken him any more, nor make him any the worse off. I know him, simply because he used to sell me the grapes and strawberries from the count’s conservatories; I *suppose* he stole them; we divided the money, and I left.”
Plantat could not refrain from an exclamation of satisfaction, as if to say, “Good luck! I knew it well enough!”
When he said he would be sent to prison, Bertaud was not wrong. The judge ordered his arrest.
It was now Philippe’s turn.

===Chinese===

他解释了他们为何要撒谎的缘由；而在这里，前科劣迹的老账又被翻了出来。
“瞧瞧；归根结底，我这人可比我的名声强多了，”他说，“世上许多人连这点都做不到呢。夜里四处走动的时候，你能瞧见不少事情呢——多着呢。”
法官极力追问他话里有何所指，但他却守口如瓶。
当被问及昨夜他究竟在何处、如何度过时，他回答说，晚上10点离开小酒馆后，他去莫普雷瓦尔（Mauprévoir）树林里下了几个套子；大约半夜1点左右才回家睡觉。
“说起来，”他补充道，“这当口那些套子里肯定已经套住猎物了。”
“你能找出证人证明你是1点回家的吗？”镇长问道，他猛然想起了伯爵房里停在3点20分的座钟。
“说不准，我真没把握，”偷猎者满不在乎地答道，“很可能我上床睡觉的时候，我儿子压根就没醒。”
看到法官陷入沉思，他接着说：
“我猜你们是要把我关进大牢，直到抓到真凶为止了。要是换在冬天，我倒也没多少怨言；大冬天蹲大牢挺舒坦的，里头暖和。可眼下正是打猎的好时节，这可真叫人扫兴。这对那个菲利普倒是一堂生动的教训；好让他长长记性，知道给贵人们行善帮忙得付出多大代价。”
“住口！”多米尼先生严厉地打断了他，“你认得盖斯潘吗？”
这个名字骤然压制住了偷猎者那副玩世不恭的狂妄；他那双灰色的小眼睛里掠过一丝异样的慌乱。
“当然认得，”他语带局促地回答，“我们常凑在一块儿打牌，您明白的，一边抿两口‘格洛利亚’1咖啡加白兰地。”
这人的慌乱立刻引起了在座四人的警觉。尤其是普朗塔，脸上流露出极度的诧异。老流氓精明过人，立刻察觉到了自己失态造成的后果。
“嗨，管他呢，爱咋咋地吧！”他嚷道，“我都招了。人不为己，天诛地灭，对吧？要是案子真是盖斯潘干的，多招这一桩也不会让他抹得更黑，境遇更惨。我认得他，纯粹是因为他以前常把伯爵温室里的葡萄和草莓偷拿出来卖给我；我猜是他偷的；我们分了赃钱，我就走人。”
普朗塔忍不住发出一声得意的惊叹，仿佛在说：“太好了！我早就料准了！”
说到自己会被关进大牢，贝尔托倒真没料错。法官当即下令将他收押。
现在轮到菲利普了。

===Original===

The poor fellow was in a pitiable state; he was crying bitterly.
“To accuse me of such a crime, *me*!” he kept repeating.
On being questioned he told the pure and simple truth, excusing himself, however, for having dared to penetrate into the park. When he was asked at what hour his father reached home, he said he knew nothing about it; he had gone to bed about nine, and had not awoke until morning. He knew Guespin, from having seen him at his father’s several times. He knew that the old man had some transactions with the gardener, but he was ignorant as to what they were. He had never spoken four times to Guespin. The judge ordered Philippe to be set at liberty, not that he was wholly convinced of his innocence, but because if the crime had been committed by several persons, it was well to have one of them free; he could be watched, and he would betray the whereabouts of the rest.
Meanwhile the count’s body was nowhere to be found. The park had been rigidly searched, but in vain. The mayor suggested that he had been thrown into the river, which was also M. Domini’s opinion; and some fishermen were sent to drag the Seine, commencing their search a little above the place where the countess was found.
It was then nearly three o’clock. M. Plantat remarked that probably no one had eaten anything during the day. Would it not be wise to take something, he suggested, if the investigations were to be pursued till night? This appeal to the trivial necessities of our frail humanity highly displeased the worthy mayor; but the rest readily assented to the suggestion, and M. Courtois, though not in the least hungry, followed the general example. Around the table which was yet wet with the wine spilt by the assassins, the judge, M. Plantat, the mayor, and the doctor sat down, and partook of an improvised collation.

===Chinese===

可怜的年轻人陷入了万分凄惨的境地；他哭得撕心裂肺。
“指控我犯下这等弥天大罪，指控我！”他不住地重复着。
接受盘问时，他一五一十吐露了纯粹的事实真相，唯独为自己擅闯庄园公园的鲁莽再三告罪。当被问及父亲昨夜究竟几点到家时，他说自己一无所知；他大约9点就上床睡下了，一觉睡到大天亮。他认得盖斯潘，只因为在他父亲家见过几次面。他知道父亲同那个园丁有些见不得光的来往，但他完全不知道具体是什么勾当。他跟盖斯潘说过的话加起来都不到四句。法官下令将菲利普当场释放，并非完全确信他是清白的，而是考虑到如果这起罪案是由数人合谋犯下的，留一个在外面反而是件好事；可以派人暗中盯梢，顺藤摸瓜引出其余的同党。
与此同时，伯爵的遗体依然杳无音信。庄园公园已被地毯式搜查了一遍，却一无所获。镇长推测尸体被抛入了河中，多米尼先生也深表赞同；于是几名渔民奉命下塞纳河撒网打捞，搜寻从发现伯爵夫人遗体处略微偏上的水域展开。
此时时间已将近3点。普朗塔先生提醒大家，恐怕一整天谁都还没顾得上吃上一口东西。他提议道，如果要将调查一直持续到入夜，先吃点东西垫垫肚子难道不是明智之举吗？这种对凡夫俗子琐碎生理需求的关注，让可敬的镇长颇感不快；但其余几人欣然采纳了这个建议，于是库尔图瓦先生虽然毫无饥意，也顺应大流照办了。在这张尚且残留着凶手泼洒葡萄酒痕迹的餐桌旁，法官、普朗塔先生、镇长以及医生围坐下来，享用了一顿匆忙拼凑的便餐。

## IX / 第九章
===Original===

Despite the haste they made, it was nearly ten o’clock when M. Plantat and his guests quitted the château of Valfeuillu. Instead of taking the high road, they cut across a pathway which ran along beside Mme. de Lanascol’s park, and led diagonally to the wire bridge; this was the shortest way to the inn where M. Lecoq had left his slight baggage. As they went along, M. Plantat grew anxious about his good friend, M. Courtois.
“What misfortune can have happened to him?” said he to Dr. Gendron.
“Thanks to the stupidity of that rascal of a servant, we learned nothing at all. This letter from Mademoiselle Laurence has caused the trouble, somehow.”
They had now reached the Faithful Grenadier.
A big red-faced fellow was smoking a long pipe at the door, his back against the house. He was talking with a railway employee. It was the landlord.
“Well, Monsieur Plantat,” he cried, “what a horrible affair this is! Come in, come in; there are several folks in the hall who saw the assassins. What a villain old Bertaud is! And that Guespin; ah, I would willingly trudge to Corbeil to see them put up the scaffold!”
“A little charity, Master Lenfant; you forget that both these men were among your best customers.”
Master Lenfant was confused by this reply; but his native impudence soon regained the mastery.
“Fine customers, *Parbleu!*” he answered, “this thief of a Guespin has got thirty francs of mine which I’ll never see again.”
“Who knows?” said Plantat, ironically. “Besides, you are going to make more than that tonight, there’s so much company at the Orcival festival.”
During this brief conversation, M. Lecoq entered the inn for his nightgown. His office being no longer a secret, he was not now welcomed as when he was taken for a simple retired haberdasher. Mme. Lenfant, a lady who had no need of her husband’s aid to show penniless sots the door, scarcely deigned to answer him. When he asked how much he owed, she responded, with a contemptuous gesture, “Nothing.” When he returned to the door, his nightgown in hand, M. Plantat said:
“Let’s hurry, for I want to get news of our poor mayor.”
The three hastened their steps, and the old justice of the peace, oppressed with sad presentiments, and trying to combat them, continued:
“If anything had happened at the mayor’s, I should certainly have been informed of it by this time.

===Chinese===

尽管大家脚步匆匆，普朗塔先生和他的客人们离开瓦尔菲伊城堡时，时间也已经将近10点了。他们没有走大路，而是抄了一条沿着拉纳斯科尔夫人庄园公园延伸、斜穿直通铁索桥的小径；这是前往勒科克先生（M. Lecoq）存放简易行囊的客栈最近的路。一路上，普朗塔先生始终为他的好友库尔图瓦先生悬着心。
“到底能出什么祸事呢？”他对让德龙医生说。
“多亏了那个浑蛋仆役的愚蠢，我们什么都没能打听出来。横竖都是洛朗斯小姐（Mademoiselle Laurence）的那封信惹出来的乱子。”
此时他们已经走到了“忠诚近卫兵”客栈门前。
一个满面红光的胖汉正背靠着客栈墙壁，吧嗒吧嗒抽着长烟斗。他正同一个铁路员工闲聊。这人便是店老板。
“哎呀，普朗塔先生，”他高声嚷道，“这案子可太骇人听闻了！快请进，快请进；大堂里有好几位客人都看见凶手了呢。老贝尔托可真是个彻头彻尾的恶棍！还有那个盖斯潘；哼，我真巴不得大老远赶到科尔贝伊，亲眼瞧着绞刑架搭起来！”
“积点口德吧，朗方掌柜（Master Lenfant）；你可别忘了，他们俩以往可都是你的座上宾呢。”
朗方掌柜被这话噎得好一阵尴尬；但他骨子里的厚颜无耻很快又占了上风。
“什么狗屁贵客，真见鬼！”他反唇相讥道，“盖斯潘那个贼胚还欠着我30法郎呢，我看是打水漂再也拿不回来了。”
“谁知道呢？”普朗塔语带讥讽地应道，“再说了，今晚奥尔西瓦尔过节来了这么多客人，你挣的早就远不止这个数了。”
在这短短的几句交谈中，勒科克先生走进客栈去取他的睡袍。既然他的官方身份已不再是秘密，他所受到的待遇自然无法同被当作退休小布商时同日而语。朗方老板娘——一位无需丈夫搭手就能轻而易举把身无分文的酒鬼轰出大门的悍妇——甚至懒得正眼瞧他。当他询问该付多少钱时，她只是轻蔑地一挥手：“免了。”当他手里拿着睡袍重新走出门外时，普朗塔先生说道：
“咱们得快点，我急着去打听咱们可怜的镇长究竟怎么样了。”
三个人加快了脚步，老治安法官被不祥的预感压得喘不过气来，竭力克制着内心的慌乱，接着说道：
“要是镇长家真出了什么大祸，按理说这会儿我早就该收到信儿了。

===Original===

Perhaps Laurence has written that she is ill, or a little indisposed. Madame Courtois, who is the best woman in the world, gets excited about nothing; she probably wanted to send her husband for Laurence at once. You’ll see that it’s some false alarm.”
No; some catastrophe had happened. A number of the village women were standing before the mayor’s gate. Baptiste, in the midst of the group, was ranting and gesticulating. But at M. Plantat’s approach, the women fled like a troop of frightened gulls. The old man’s unexpected appearance annoyed the placid Baptiste not a little, for he was interrupted, by the sudden departure of his audience, in the midst of a superb oratorical flight. As he had a great fear of M. Plantat, however, he dissimulated his chagrin with his habitual smile.
“Ah, sir,” cried he, when M. Plantat was three steps off, “ah, what an affair! I was going for you﻿—”
“Does your master wish me?”
“More than you can think. He ran so fast from Valfeuillu here, that I could scarcely keep up with him. He’s not usually fast, you know; but you ought to have seen him this time, fat as he is!”
M. Plantat stamped impatiently.
“Well, we got here at last,” resumed the man, “and monsieur rushed into the drawing-room, where he found madame sobbing like a Magdalene. He was so out of breath he could scarcely speak. His eyes stuck out of his head, and he stuttered like this﻿—‘What’s-the-matter? What’s the-matter?’ Madame, who couldn’t speak either, held out mademoiselle’s letter, which she had in her hand.”
The three auditors were on coals of fire; the rogue perceived it, and spoke more and more slowly.
“Then monsieur took the letter, went to the window, and at a glance read it through. He cried out hoarsely, thus: ‘Oh!’ then he went to beating the air with his hands, like a swimming dog; then he walked up and down and fell, pouf! like a bag, his face on the floor. That was all.”
“Is he dead?” cried all three in the same breath.
“Oh, no; you shall see,” responded Baptiste, with a placid smile.
M. Lecoq was a patient man, but not so patient as you might think. Irritated by the manner of Baptiste’s recital, he put down his bundle, seized the man’s arm with his right hand, while with the left he whisked a light flexible cane, and said:
“Look here, fellow, I want you to hurry up, you know.”

===Chinese===

也许是洛朗斯来信说她病了，或者有些身体不适。库尔图瓦夫人是天底下最心软善良的女人，芝麻大点的事都能把她急得团团转；她八成是想让丈夫立刻动身去把洛朗斯接回来。你们瞧着吧，多半是虚惊一场。”
不；大祸确实降临了。几个村妇正聚在镇长家大门前交头接耳。巴蒂斯特站在人群中间，正手舞足蹈、口沫横飞地大发宏论。但在普朗塔先生走近时，这帮妇人吓得像一群受惊的海鸥般四散奔逃。老人的突如其来让神气活现的巴蒂斯特大为扫兴，因为听众的骤然作鸟兽散，硬生生打断了他那精彩绝伦的雄辩演说。不过，由于他对普朗塔先生畏之如虎，还是用惯常的谄笑掩饰住了内心的悻悻然。
“哎呀，先生，”当普朗塔先生离他只有三步远时，他大喊道，“哎呀，出了天大的事了！我正打算去找您呢——”
“你家主人找我？”
“急得不能再急了。他从瓦尔菲伊一路狂奔回这儿，我都差点儿没跟上他的步子。您知道的，他平日里走路从不着急；可这回您真该瞧瞧他那副模样，凭他那副胖墩墩的身板！”
普朗塔先生急躁地跺了跺脚。
“好吧，我们好不容易赶到了家，”男仆接着说道，“先生一头撞进客厅，只见太太在那儿哭得跟抹大拉的玛利亚似的。先生喘得上气不接下气，连话都说不利索了。两颗眼珠子都快从眼眶里瞪出来了，结结巴巴地直嚷嚷：‘出……出什么事了？出……出什么事了？’太太也哽咽得说不出话，只是把手里攥着的小姐的信递了过去。”
三位听众急得如同热锅上的蚂蚁；这个刁奴看在眼里，说话的语调反而越发拖泥带水、慢条斯理。
“接着先生接过了信，走到窗前，一眼从头扫到了底。他嗓子里发出一声嘶哑的惨叫，就像这样：‘噢！’然后就像狗刨水一样在半空中胡乱抓挠；接着他在屋里跌跌撞撞地转了几圈，扑通一声！像个麻袋似的直挺挺栽倒在地上，脸贴着地。就这么回事。”
“他死了吗？”三个人异口同声地失声喊道。
“噢，那倒没有；您待会儿就知道了，”巴蒂斯特带着平静的微笑答道。
勒科克先生是个有耐心的人，但他的耐心可没常人想象的那么宽厚。被巴蒂斯特那副慢腾腾的叙述作派惹得心头火起，他放下手中的包袱，右手一把扭住了男仆的胳膊，左手则轻巧地挥舞着一根柔韧的藤杖，冷冷地说道：
“听着，伙计，我劝你最好快点招，明白吗？”

===Original===

That was all he said; the servant was terribly afraid of this little blond man, with a strange voice, and a fist harder than a vice. He went on very rapidly this time, his eye fixed on M. Lecoq’s rattan.
“Monsieur had an attack of vertigo. All the house was in confusion; everybody except I, lost their heads; it occurred to me to go for a doctor, and I started off for one﻿—for Doctor Gendron, whom I knew to be at the château, or the doctor near by, or the apothecary﻿—it mattered not who. By good luck, at the street corner, I came upon Robelot, the bonesetter﻿—‘Come, follow me,’ said I. He did so; sent away those who were tending monsieur, and bled him in both arms. Shortly after, he breathed, then he opened his eyes, and then he spoke. Now he is quite restored, and is lying on one of the drawing-room lounges, crying with all his might. He told me he wanted to see Monsieur Plantat, and I﻿—”
“And﻿—Mademoiselle Laurence?” asked M. Plantat, with a trembling voice. Baptiste assumed a tragic pose.
“Ah, gentlemen,” said he, “don’t ask me about her﻿—’tis heartrending!”
The doctor and M. Plantat heard no more, but hurried in; M. Lecoq followed, having confided his nightgown to Baptiste, with, “Carry that to M. Plantat’s﻿—quick!”
Misfortune, when it enters a house, seems to leave its fatal imprint on the very threshold. Perhaps it is not really so, but it is the feeling which those who are summoned to it experience. As the physician and the justice of the peace traversed the courtyard, this house, usually so gay and hospitable, presented a mournful aspect. Lights were seen coming and going in the upper story. Mlle. Lucile, the mayor’s youngest daughter, had had a nervous attack, and was being tended. A young girl, who served as Laurence’s maid, was seated in the vestibule, on the lower stair, weeping bitterly. Several domestics were there also, frightened, motionless, not knowing what to do in all this fright. The drawing-room door was wide open; the room was dimly lighted by two candles; Mme. Courtois lay rather than sat in a large armchair near the fireplace. Her husband was reclining on a lounge near the windows at the rear of the apartment. They had taken off his coat and had torn away his shirtsleeves and flannel vest, when he was to be bled. There were strips of cotton wrapped about his naked arms.

===Chinese===

他就说了这么一句；男仆吓得魂飞魄散，对这个留着浅色头发、嗓音奇特、拳头硬得像铁钳一般的矮个子敬畏交加。这回他的语速变得飞快，两只眼睛死死盯着勒科克手中的藤杖。
“先生那是中了眩晕风瘫。整座宅邸全乱套了；除了我，所有人都吓破了胆；我想着得去请个大夫，就拔腿往外跑去请——去找我知道人在城堡里的让德龙医生，或是附近的大夫，或是药剂师——横竖是谁都成。走大运的是，在街拐角我迎面撞上了接骨郎中罗伯洛（Robelot）——‘快，跟我走，’我说。他就跟着来了；他把照顾先生的人全打发开，给先生的两条胳膊都放了血。没过多久，先生缓过气来了，接着睁开了眼，随后能开口说话了。眼下他算是缓过来了，正躺在客厅里的一张长榻上，哭得天昏地暗呢。他跟我说要见普朗塔先生，我这就——”
“那么——洛朗斯小姐呢？”普朗塔先生用颤抖的声音问。巴蒂斯特立刻摆出了一副悲天悯人的架势。
“哎呀，先生们，”他说，“千万别跟我打听她——那真是叫人肝肠寸断啊！”
医生和普朗塔先生再也听不下去了，快步冲进了宅门；勒科克先生紧随其后，顺手把睡袍塞给了巴蒂斯特，吩咐道：“把这送去普朗塔先生府上——麻利点！”
祸患降临宅邸时，似乎连门槛上都留下了致命的阴惨印记。或许客观上并非如此，但这正是每一个应召赶来的人内心油然而生的感受。当医生与治安法官穿过院落时，这栋平日里欢声笑语、热情好客的府邸呈现出一片凄凉肃杀的景象。只见楼上烛火摇曳，人影憧憧。镇长的小女儿吕西勒小姐（Mademoiselle Lucile）犯了神经抽搐，眼下正有人在照料。一名服侍洛朗斯的小女仆坐在门厅底层的楼梯台阶上，哭得死去活来。几个仆人也聚在那里，神色惊恐，呆立原地，在这一片慌乱之中手足无措。客厅的大门大敞着；两支蜡烛散发着昏暗摇曳的微光；库尔图瓦夫人几乎是瘫在壁炉旁的一张大扶手椅里，与其说是坐着，不如说是瘫倒。她的丈夫则斜倚在房间深处靠窗的长榻上。他们替他脱掉了外套，在放血时撕碎了他的衬衫衣袖和法兰绒背心。他赤裸的手臂上紧紧缠着几条棉布绷带。

===Original===

A small man, habited like a well-to-do Parisian artisan, stood near the door, with an embarrassed expression of countenance. It was Robelot, who had remained, lest any new exigency for his services should arise.
The entrance of his friend startled M. Courtois from the sad stupor into which he had been plunged. He got up and staggered into the arms of the worthy Plantat, saying, in a broken voice:
“Ah, my friend, I am most miserable﻿—most wretched!”
The poor mayor was so changed as scarcely to be recognizable. He was no longer the happy man of the world, with smiling face, firm look, the pride of which betrayed plainly his self-importance and prosperity. In a few hours he had grown twenty years older. He was broken, overwhelmed; his thoughts wandered in a sea of bitterness. He could only repeat, vacantly, again and again:
“Wretched! most wretched!”
M. Plantat was the right sort of a friend for such a time. He led M. Courtois back to the sofa and sat down beside him, and taking his hand in his own, forced him to calm his grief. He recalled to him that his wife, the companion of his life, remained to him, to mourn the dear departed with him. Had he not another daughter to cherish? But the poor man was in no state to listen to all this.
“Ah, my friend,” said he shuddering, “you do not know all! If she had died here, in the midst of us, comforted by our tender care, my despair would be great; but nothing compared with that which now tortures me. If you only knew﻿—”
M. Plantat rose, as if terrified by what he was about to hear.
“But who can tell,” pursued the wretched man, “where or how she died? Oh, my Laurence, was there no one to hear your last agony and save you? What has become of you, so young and happy?”
He rose, shaking with anguish and cried:
“Let us go, Plantat, and look for her at the Morgue.” Then he fell back again, muttering the lugubrious word, “the Morgue.”
The witnesses of this scene remained, mute, motionless, rigid, holding their breath. The stifled sobs and groans of Mme. Courtois and the little maid alone broke the silence.
“You know that I am your friend﻿—your best friend,” said M. Plantat, softly; “confide in me﻿—tell me all.”
“Well,” commenced M. Courtois, “know”﻿—but his tears choked his utterance, and he could not go on. Holding out a crumpled letter, wet with tears, he stammered:
“Here, read﻿—it is her last letter.”
M.

===Chinese===

一个身材矮小、衣着如同体面的巴黎手艺人的男子，正站在门边，神色颇显局促。此人正是罗伯洛，他之所以留下来，是防备万一还需要他的医道手段。
挚友的到来，将库尔图瓦先生从方才沉浸的凄凉恍惚中惊醒。他挣扎着站起身，跌跌撞撞地扑进可敬的普朗塔怀中，用断断续续的悲声哭诉道：
“啊，我的朋友，我是天底下最不幸、最痛苦的人啊！”
可怜的镇长模样大变，几乎让人不敢辨认。他再也不是那个志得意满、面带微笑、目光坚毅的世俗幸运儿了，往日那份矜持无不彰显着他的显赫地位与荣华富贵。在短短几个小时里，他仿佛一下子苍老了20岁。他被彻底摧垮了，万念俱灰；心智沉溺在一片苦海之中。他只能茫然失神地一遍又一遍重复着：
“不幸啊！真是万劫不复啊！”
在这样的关头，普朗塔先生展现了真正挚友的分量。他扶着库尔图瓦先生重新坐回沙发上，在他身旁坐下，紧紧握住他的手，强令他平复心中的悲痛。他提醒他，他的妻子——相伴一生的伴侣——依然健在，能够同他分担哀思。难道他没有另一个女儿需要关爱呵护吗？然而这个可怜的男人此刻根本听不进任何劝慰。
“啊，我的朋友，”他浑身颤抖着说道，“你根本不知道事情的原委！如果她是死在家里，死在我们膝下，在亲人无微不至的呵护中合上双眼，我的悲伤纵使万分沉重，也绝无法同此刻撕扯我五脏六腑的痛苦相提并论。如果你知道真相——”
普朗塔先生霍然站起身来，仿佛被自己即将听到的噩耗吓呆了。
“可有谁能告诉我，”这个绝望的父亲接着哀号道，“她究竟死在何处，死于何人之手？噢，我的洛朗斯，难道你临终前弥留之际，竟没有一个人听见你的哀鸣来救救你吗？曾经那么年轻快活的你，如今究竟落到了何种田地？”
他挣扎着站起来，痛不欲生得浑身发抖，大声哭喊道：
“走吧，普朗塔，咱们去停尸房找她。”随后他又颓然瘫倒下去，喃喃念叨着那个阴森凄厉的字眼：“停尸房。”
在场目睹这一切的人全都目瞪口呆，肃立原地，屏息凝神。唯有库尔图瓦夫人和小女仆压抑的啜泣与呜咽打破了这片死寂。
“你知道我是你的朋友——你最要好的朋友，”普朗塔先生轻声说道，“信任我吧——把一切都告诉我。”
“好吧，”库尔图瓦先生开口道，“你得知道”——然而泪水哽住了他的喉咙，他再也无法继续说下去。他颤巍巍地递出一封被泪水打湿、揉得皱巴巴的信，结结巴巴地说：
“拿着，看吧——这是她留下的绝笔信。”

===Original===

Plantat approached the table, and, not without difficulty, read:

> “Dearly Beloved Parents﻿—
> “Forgive, forgive, I beseech you, your unhappy daughter, the distress she is about to cause you. Alas! I have been very guilty, but the punishment is terrible! In a day of wandering, I forgot all﻿—the example and advice of my dear, sainted mother, my most sacred duty, and your tenderness. I could not, no, I could not resist him who wept before me in swearing for me an eternal love﻿—and who has abandoned me. Now, all is over; I am lost, lost. I cannot long conceal my dreadful sin. Oh, dear parents, do not curse me. I am your daughter﻿—I cannot bear to face contempt, I will not survive my dishonor.
> “When this letter reaches you, I shall have ceased to live; I shall have quitted my aunt’s, and shall have gone far away, where no one will find me. There I shall end my misery and despair. Adieu, then, oh, beloved parents, adieu! I would that I could, for the last time, beg your forgiveness on my knees. My dear mother, my good father, have pity on a poor wanderer; pardon me, forgive me. Never let my sister Lucile know. Once more, adieu﻿—I have courage﻿—honor commands! For you is the last prayer and supreme thought of your poor
> Laurence.”

===Chinese===

普朗塔先生走近桌旁，万分艰难地辨认着信上的字迹：

> “至亲至爱的父母亲：
> “我恳求你们，宽恕、宽恕你们不幸的女儿即将给你们带来的无尽痛苦吧。唉！我铸成了弥天大罪，而惩罚又是这般惨烈！在迷失心智的那一天，我把一切都抛到了脑后——我亲爱圣洁的母亲的训诫与榜样、我最神圣的操守，以及你们对我的满腔深情。在那个当着我的面痛哭流涕、赌咒发誓对我永矢弗谖——而今却将我无情抛弃的男人面前，我无力抗拒，是的，我彻底沦陷了。如今，万事皆休；我全毁了，彻底毁了。我无法再长久遮掩我那可怕的罪愆。噢，亲爱的父母，千万不要咒骂我。我是你们的骨肉女儿——我无法面对世人的鄙夷白眼，我也绝不愿苟活于羞辱之中。
> “当这封信送到你们手中时，我已不在人世；我已离开了姑妈家，去往一个谁也找不到我的遥远天涯。在那里，我将了结我的凄苦与绝望。永别了，噢，至爱的父母亲，永别了！我多么渴望能最后一次跪倒在你们膝前乞求宽恕啊。我亲爱的母亲，我善良的父亲，怜悯怜悯一个迷途的可怜人吧；宽恕我，赦免我。千万不要让妹妹吕西勒知晓这一切。再次永别了——我有勇气面对——这是名誉的召唤！你们不幸的女儿将把最后的祈祷与至高无上的思念留给你们。
> 洛朗斯”

===Original===

Great tears rolled silently down the old man’s cheeks as he deciphered this sad letter. A cold, mute, terrible anger shrivelled the muscles of his face. When he had finished, he said, in a hoarse voice:
“Wretch!”
M. Courtois heard this exclamation.
“Ah, yes, wretch indeed,” he cried, “this vile villain who has crept in in the dark, and stolen my dearest treasure, my darling child! Alas, she knew nothing of life. He whispered into her ear those fond words which make the hearts of all young girls throb; she had faith in him; and now he abandons her. Oh, if I knew who he was﻿—if I knew﻿—”
He suddenly interrupted himself. A ray of intelligence had just illumined the abyss of despair into which he had fallen.
“No,” said he, “a young girl is not thus abandoned, when she has a dowry of a million, unless for some good reason. Love passes away; avarice remains. The infamous wretch was not free﻿—he was married. He could only be the Count de Trémorel. It is he who has killed my child.”
The profound silence which succeeded proved to him that his conjecture was shared by those around him.
“I was blind, blind!” cried he. “For I received him at my house, and called him my friend. Oh, have I not a right to a terrible vengeance?”
But the crime at Valfeuillu occurred to him; and it was with a tone of deep disappointment that he resumed:
“And not to be able to revenge myself! I could riot, then, kill him with my own hands, see him suffer for hours, hear him beg for mercy! He is dead. He has fallen under the blows of assassins, less vile than himself.”
The doctor and M. Plantat strove to comfort the unhappy man; but he went on, excited more and more by the sound of his own voice.
“Oh, Laurence, my beloved, why did you not confide in me? You feared my anger, as if a father would ever cease to love his child. Lost, degraded, fallen to the ranks of the vilest, I would still love thee. Were you not my own? Alas! you knew not a father’s heart. A father does not pardon; he forgets. You might still have been happy, my lost love.”
He wept; a thousand memories of the time when Laurence was a child and played about his knees recurred to his mind; it seemed as though it were but yesterday.
“Oh, my daughter, was it that you feared the world﻿—the wicked, hypocritical world? But we should have gone away. I should have left Orcival, resigned my office.

===Chinese===

老人在辨认这封凄惨的绝笔信时，大颗大颗的泪珠无声地顺着双颊滚落。一股冰冷、沉默而可怕的狂怒扭曲了他脸上的每一寸筋肉。读罢信函，他嗓音嘶哑地吐出一句：
“浑蛋！”
库尔图瓦先生听到了这声咒骂。
“啊，没错，真真是一个浑蛋！”他号啕大哭道，“这个在暗处潜行、偷走我最心爱的无价之宝、偷走我心肝宝贝的卑鄙恶徒！唉，她对人世间的事一无所知啊。他在她耳边吐露那些足以令所有年轻姑娘心旌摇曳的甜言蜜语；她全心全意信任他；而如今他却把她弃如敝屣。噢，要是我知道他是谁——要是我知道——”
他冷不丁刹住了话头。一道智慧的灵光骤然照亮了他方才坠入的绝望深渊。
“不，”他说，“一个坐拥100万嫁妆的年轻姑娘，绝不会无缘无故遭到这般遗弃，除非事出有因。情爱会消逝；贪婪却亘古长存。那个卑鄙无耻的恶徒必定身不由己——他已经成了家。他只能是特雷莫雷尔伯爵！是他害死了我的孩子！”
随之而来的死寂向他证明，在场的众人也抱有同样的推测。
“我真是瞎了眼，瞎了狗眼啊！”他捶胸顿足地喊道，“我竟然奉他为座上宾，还口口声声唤他是挚友。噢，难道我没有权利施以最酷烈的复仇吗？”
然而瓦尔菲伊的那桩罪案蓦地闪过他的脑海；他用满是万念俱灰的口吻接着说道：
“可我竟然连报仇雪恨的机会都没有了！我再也不能亲手手刃这个恶贼，再也不能看着他受尽千刀万剐、听他苦苦求饶了！他已经死了。他倒在了比他自己还少几分下作的凶手刀下。”
医生和普朗塔先生极力劝慰这个不幸的男人；但他被自己悲愤的呐喊声刺激得越发癫狂。
“噢，洛朗斯，我心爱的宝贝，你为什么不早点对我敞开心扉呢？你害怕我的怒火，可天底下哪有做父亲的会不爱自己的骨肉？哪怕你迷失堕落，沦为世间最下贱的浪妇，我依然会深爱着你。难道你不是我的亲生骨肉吗？唉！你根本不懂得一个父亲的心肠啊。父亲从不奢谈宽恕，父亲只会选择遗忘。你本该依然能够获得幸福的，我那命苦的心肝宝贝啊。”
他号啕痛哭；洛朗斯童年时代在他膝头嬉戏打闹的无数画面浮现在眼前；一切宛如就发生在昨日。
“噢，我的女儿，难道你是害怕世俗的流言蜚语——那虚伪恶毒的世道吗？可我们本可以远走高飞的啊。我本可以离开奥尔西瓦尔，辞掉我的官职。

===Original===

We should have settled down far away, in the remotest corner of France, in Germany, in Italy. With money all is possible. All? No! I have millions, and yet my daughter has killed herself.”
He concealed his face in his hands; his sobs choked him.
“And not to know what has become of her!” he continued. “Is it not frightful? What death did she choose? You remember, Doctor, and you, Plantat, her beautiful curls about her pure forehead, her great, trembling eyes, her long curved lashes? Her smile﻿—do you know, it was the sun’s ray of my life. I so loved her voice, and her mouth so fresh, which gave me such warm, loving kisses. Dead! Lost! And not to know what has become of her sweet form﻿—perhaps abandoned in the mire of some river. Do you recall the countess’s body this morning? It will kill me! Oh, my child﻿—that I might see her one hour﻿—one minute﻿—that I might give her cold lips one last kiss!”
M. Lecoq strove in vain to prevent a warm tear which ran from his eyes, from falling. M. Lecoq was a stoic on principle, and by profession. But the desolate words of the poor father overcame him. Forgetting that his emotion would be seen, he came out from the shadow where he had stood, and spoke to M. Courtois:
“I, Monsieur Lecoq, of the detectives, give you my honor that I will find Mademoiselle Laurence’s body.”
The poor mayor grasped desperately at this promise, as a drowning man to a straw.
“Oh, yes, we will find her, won’t we? You will help me. They say that to the police nothing is impossible﻿—that they see and know everything. We will see what has become of my child.”
He went toward M. Lecoq, and taking him by the hand:
“Thank you,” added he, “you are a good man. I received you ill a while ago, and judged you with foolish pride: forgive me. We will succeed﻿—you will see, we will aid each other, we will put all the police on the scent, we will search through France, money will do it﻿—I have it﻿—I have millions﻿—take them﻿—”
His energies were exhausted: he staggered and fell heavily on the lounge.
“He must not remain here long,” muttered the doctor in Plantat’s ear, “he must get to bed. A brain fever, after such excitement, would not surprise me.”
The old justice of the peace at once approached Mme. Courtois, who still reclined in the armchair, apparently having seen or heard nothing of what had passed, and oblivious in her grief.
“Madame!” said he, “Madame!”

===Chinese===

我们本可以在法兰西最偏僻的角落，在德意志，在意大利落脚安家。有了钱，天下何事不可为？何事？不！我坐拥几百万家财，却连自己的女儿自尽都救不回来。”
他用双手捂住面孔；抽泣声几乎让他窒息。
“而我甚至连她如今身在何处都不知道啊！”他接着哭喊道，“这难道不是最可怕的折磨吗？她到底选了怎样一种死法？你还记得吗，医生，还有你，普朗塔，她光洁额头前那些美丽的发卷，她那双楚楚动人的大眼睛，她那长长的弯曲睫毛？她的微笑——你们知道吗，那是我生命中唯一的阳光。我是那么依恋她的声音，还有她那清新柔嫩的小嘴，曾给过我多少温存体贴的亲吻。死了！全毁了！而我甚至不知道她那娇柔的身躯落到了何方——也许正遗弃在哪条荒凉河流的烂泥滩里。你们还记得今早伯爵夫人的遗体吗？这念头会要了我的命啊！噢，我的孩子——哪怕让我再看她一眼——哪怕一分钟——哪怕让我亲一亲她冰冷的嘴唇啊！”
勒科克先生竭力想要忍住，却依然止不住一滴滚烫的泪珠从眼眶滑落。按做人的原则和职业的操守，勒科克先生向来是个铁石心肠的冷静之人。然而这位可怜父亲凄凉绝望的字字句句，深深刺痛了他的心灵。普朗塔老爹痛苦地闭上了双眼。唯有让德龙医生的面庞显得冷峻严厉。他走到库尔图瓦夫人身旁，她宛如一尊雕塑般僵坐在那儿，已在悲痛中神智模糊。
“夫人！”医生唤道，“夫人！”

===Original===

She shuddered and rose, with a wandering air.
“It is my fault,” said she, “my miserable fault! A mother should read her daughter’s heart as in a book. I did not suspect Laurence’s secret; I am a most unhappy mother.”
The doctor also came to her.
“Madame,” said he, in an imperious tone, “your husband must be persuaded to go to bed at once. His condition is very serious, and a little sleep is absolutely necessary. I will have a potion prepared﻿—”
“Oh, my God!” cried the poor lady, wringing her hands, in the fear of a new misfortune, as bitter as the first; which, however, restored her to her presence of mind. She called the servants, who assisted the mayor to regain his chamber. Mme. Courtois also retired, followed by the doctor. Three persons only remained in the drawing-room﻿—Plantat, Lecoq, and Robelot, who still stood near the door.
“Poor Laurence!” murmured Plantat. “Poor girl!”
“It seems to me that her father is most to be pitied,” remarked M. Lecoq. “Such a blow, at his age, may be more than he can bear. Even should he recover, his life is broken.”
“I had a sort of presentiment,” said the other, “that this misfortune would come. I had guessed Laurence’s secret, but I guessed it too late.”
“And you did not try﻿—”
“What? In a delicate case like this, when the honor of a family depends on a word, one must be circumspect. What could I do? Put Courtois on his guard? Clearly not. He would have refused to believe me. He is one of those men who will listen to nothing, and whom the brutal fact alone can undeceive.”
“You might have dealt with the Count de Trémorel.”
“The count would have denied all. He would have asked what right I had to interfere in his affairs.”
“But the girl?”
M. Plantat sighed heavily.
“Though I detest mixing up with what does not concern me, I did try one day to talk with her. With infinite precaution and delicacy, and without letting her see that I knew all, I tried to show her the abyss near which she was drawing.”
“And what did she reply?”
“Nothing. She laughed and joked, as women who have a secret which they wish to conceal, do. Besides, I could not get a quarter of an hour alone with her, and it was necessary to act, I knew﻿—for I was her best friend﻿—before committing this imprudence of speaking to her.

===Chinese===

她浑身一颤站起身来，目光空洞迷离。
“这是我的罪过，”她说，“全是我这罪孽深重的母亲的错！做母亲的理应像读一本书那样洞悉女儿的内心。我竟然丝毫没有察觉出洛朗斯的隐秘；我是天底下最失职的母亲。”
医生也走到了她面前。
“夫人，”他用不容置疑的命令口吻说道，“必须立刻劝您丈夫上床歇息。他的病情极其危重，必须立即合眼睡上一觉。我去配一剂安神药汤——”
“噢，老天爷啊！”可怜的贵妇十指紧扣痛哭失声，生怕再添一桩同方才那般惨绝人寰的新祸；然而这阵恐惧反而唤回了她的理智。她唤来仆人们，七手八脚扶着镇长回卧室歇息。库尔图瓦夫人也退了出去，医生紧随其后。客厅里如今只剩下三个人——普朗塔、勒科克，以及依然恭立在门边的罗伯洛。
“可怜的洛朗斯！”普朗塔喃喃自语道，“可怜的姑娘！”
“依我看，她父亲才是最值得怜悯的人，”勒科克先生评价道，“在他这个岁数遭受如此沉重的打击，恐怕远非他的身板所能承受。即便他能捡回一条命，他的后半生也彻底毁了。”
“我隐隐早有某种预感，”普朗塔说，“这场大祸迟早要降临。我早已猜出了洛朗斯的秘密，只可惜我猜中得太迟了。”
“那您就没设法——”
“设法做什么？在这种家族名誉系于只言片语的微妙关头，一个人必须谨小慎微。我能怎么做？提醒库尔图瓦提高警惕吗？显然行不通。他绝不可能相信我的话。他是那种油盐不进的人，唯有残酷无情的事实当头棒喝才能让他醒悟。”
“您本可以去找特雷莫雷尔伯爵当面交涉。”
“伯爵只会矢口否认一切。他会质问我凭什么插手干涉他的私事。”
“那那个姑娘呢？”
普朗塔先生长长地叹了口气。
“尽管我深恶痛绝掺和与己无关的闲事，但有一天我确实曾试图同她谈谈。我极尽审慎与含蓄，在丝毫没有表露出我洞悉一切的前提下，试图向她点明她正步步逼近的万丈深渊。”
“她怎么回答的？”
“什么都没说。她只是嬉皮笑脸打着哈哈，就像那些怀有想要极力隐瞒秘密的女人一贯的做法那样。况且，我根本找不出单独同她待上一刻钟的机会，而我知道——作为她最好的朋友——必须在冒失直言之前有所作为。

===Original===

Not a day passed that she did not come to my garden and cull my rarest flowers﻿—and I would not, look you, give one of my flowers to the Pope himself. She had instituted me her florist in ordinary. For her sake I collected my briars of the Cape﻿—”
He was talking on so wide of his subject that M. Lecoq could not repress a roguish smile. The old man was about to proceed when he heard a noise in the hall, and looking up he observed Robelot for the first time. His face at once betrayed his great annoyance.
“*You* were there, were you?” he said.
The bonesetter smiled obsequiously.
“Yes, Monsieur, quite at your service.”
“You have been listening, eh?”
“Oh, as to that, I was waiting to see if Madame Courtois had any commands for me.”
A sudden reflection occurred to M. Plantat; the expression of his eye changed. He winked at M. Lecoq to call his attention, and addressing the bonesetter in a milder tone, said: “Come here, Master Robelot.”
M. Lecoq had read the man at a glance. Robelot was a small, insignificant-looking man, but really of herculean strength. His hair, cut short behind, fell over his large, intelligent forehead. His eyes shone with the fire of covetousness, and expressed, when he forgot to guard them, a cynical boldness. A sly smile was always playing about his thin lips, beneath which there was no beard. A little way off, with his slight figure and his beardless face, he looked like a Paris gamin﻿—one of those little wretches who are the essence of all corruption, whose imagination is more soiled than the gutters where they search for lost pennies.
Robelot advanced several steps, smiling and bowing. “Perhaps,” said he, “Monsieur has, by chance, need of me?”
“None whatever, Master Robelot, I only wish to congratulate you on happening in so apropos, to bleed Monsieur Courtois. Your lancet has, doubtless, saved his life.”
“It’s quite possible.”
“Monsieur Courtois is generous﻿—he will amply recompense this great service.”
“Oh, I shall ask him nothing. Thank God, I want nobody’s help. If I am paid my due, I am content.”
“I know that well enough; you are prosperous﻿—you ought to be satisfied.”
M. Plantat’s tone was friendly, almost paternal. He was deeply interested, evidently, in Robelot’s prosperity.
“Satisfied!” resumed the bonesetter. “Not so much as you might think. Life is very dear for poor people.”

===Chinese===

以往哪天她不到我的花园里来采摘我最名贵的花儿呢——而你们得知道，我的花哪怕教皇亲自登门，我也舍不得送上一朵。她甚至封我为她的御用花匠。为了她，我特意培育了那些开普敦黑莓刺花——”
他扯得离正题实在太远，以至于勒科克先生忍不住露出一抹狡黠的微笑。老人正打算继续往下说，忽然听见门厅里有动静，抬起头来，这才头一次注意到了罗伯洛的存在。他的脸色顿时流露出极度的不悦。
“你小子竟然一直在这儿？”他说。
接骨郎中谄媚地弓腰笑了笑。
“是的，先生，随时听候差遣。”
“你一直在这儿偷听，是吧？”
“哎哟，瞧您说的，我不过是在这儿候着，看看库尔图瓦夫人是否还有用得着我的地方。”
普朗塔先生脑海中灵光一闪；眼神陡然一变。他朝勒科克先生使了个眼色示意他注意，随后用温和得多的语气对罗伯洛说：“过来，罗伯洛掌柜。”
勒科克先生一眼就把这人看穿了。罗伯洛是个貌不惊人的矮个子，骨子里却蕴藏着力拔千钧的蛮力。头发在脑后剃得很短，垂在他那宽大而精明的前额上。他的双眼中燃烧着贪婪的火焰，一旦忘记伪装，便流露出一种放肆狂妄的痞气。一丝狡黠的冷笑始终挂在他那毫无胡须的薄唇边。从远处看，凭着他那纤瘦的身板和光溜溜的面孔，他活像个巴黎小阿飞——那种汇聚了一切下流堕落特质、脑子里的肮脏念头比他们淘摸落水铜板的水沟还要污浊的可怜虫。
罗伯洛满面堆笑、躬身向前迈了几步。“不知先生莫非正巧有用得着小人的地方？”
“半点用不着，罗伯洛师傅，我只是想恭喜你今天赶得这般巧，及时替库尔图瓦先生放了血。你的柳叶刀想必救了他的性命。”
“这倒极有可能。”
“库尔图瓦先生出手阔绰——他定会重重酬谢你这桩天大的救命之恩。”
“噢，我可不敢向他讨要分文。托老天爷的福，我不求任何人接济。只要照数拿到我该得的，我就心满意足了。”
“这点我再清楚不过了；你近来财源广进——理该知足常乐了。”
普朗塔先生的语气和蔼亲切，甚至近乎慈父般的关怀。显然，他对罗伯洛的发迹史表现出了极其浓厚的兴趣。
“知足！”接骨郎中接话道，“哪有您想得那么宽裕啊。穷人的日子可艰难着呢。”

===Original===

“But, haven’t you just purchased an estate near d’Evry?”
“Yes.”
“And a nice place, too, though a trifle damp. Happily you have stone to fill it in with, on the land that you bought of the widow Frapesle.”
Robelot had never seen the old justice of the peace so talkative, so familiar; he seemed a little surprised.
“Three wretched pieces of land!” said he.
“Not so bad as you talk about. Then you’ve also bought something in the way of mines, at auction, haven’t you?”
“Just a bunch of nothing at all.”
“True, but it pays well. It isn’t so bad, you see, to be a doctor without a diploma.”
Robelot had been several times prosecuted for illegal practicing; so he thought he ought to protest against this.
“If I cure people,” said he, “I’m not paid for it.”
“Then your trade in herbs isn’t what has enriched you.”
The conversation was becoming a cross-examination. The bonesetter was beginning to be restless.
“Oh, I make something out of the herbs,” he answered.
“And as you are thrifty, you buy land.”
“I’ve also got some cattle and horses, which bring in something. I raise horses, cows, and sheep.”
“Also without diploma?”
Robelot waxed disdainful.
“A piece of parchment does not make science. I don’t fear the men of the schools. I study animals in the fields and the stable, without bragging. I haven’t my equal for raising them, nor for knowing their diseases.”
M. Plantat’s tone became more and more winning.
“I know that you are a bright fellow, full of experience. Doctor Gendron, with whom you served, was praising your cleverness a moment ago.”
The bonesetter shuddered, not so imperceptibly as to escape Plantat, who continued: “Yes, the good doctor said he never had so intelligent an assistant. ‘Robelot,’ said he, ‘has such an aptitude for chemistry, and so much taste for it besides, that he understands as well as I many of the most delicate operations.’ ”
“*Parbleu!* I did my best, for I was well paid, and I was always fond of learning.”
“And you were an apt scholar at Doctor Gendron’s, Master Robelot; he makes some very curious studies. His work and experience on poisons are above all remarkable.”
Robelot’s uneasiness became apparent; his look wavered.
“Yes;” returned he, “I have seen some strange experiments.”

===Chinese===

“可是，你不是刚在埃夫里（d’Evry）附近置办了一处产业吗？”
“是的。”
“而且还是个挺不错的地方呢，虽说稍嫌潮湿了点。好在你从弗拉佩勒寡妇（widow Frapesle）手里买下的那块地里有的是石料，正好拿去填地基。”
罗伯洛平生从未见过这位老治安法官这般健谈、这般亲热；他显得有些诧异。
“就那么三块巴掌大的荒地！”他说。
“可没你嘴里说的那么寒碜。接着你还在拍卖会上盘下了一些矿场产权，不是吗？”
“全都是些不值钱的破玩意儿。”
“话虽如此，来钱可快得很呢。你看，当个没有文凭的野郎中，日子过得倒也滋润嘛。”
罗伯洛曾因非法行医被官府起诉过好几回；所以他觉得有必要对此提出抗辩。
“要是真治好了人，”他说，“我也没收人家的诊费。”
“这么说来，你靠倒腾草药可发不了这么大的财。”
这番交谈不知不觉演变成了一场审讯。接骨郎中开始感到坐立不安起来。
“噢，靠贩草药多少也能挣上几个，”他答道。
“再加上你勤俭持家，就把攒下的钱拿去置办田产。”
“手头也养了些牲口和马匹，多少能带来点进项。我倒腾马匹、母牛和羊群。”
“这也是无证经营咯？”
罗伯洛脸上浮现出一抹傲慢的不屑。
“一张羊皮纸文凭可成就不了真本事。我可不怕那些正规学堂里出来的书呆子。我在田间地头和牲口棚里摸爬滚打琢磨牲口，毫不夸张地说，论起喂养牲口、瞧牲口毛病，这方圆之内还没人比得过我。”
普朗塔先生的口吻变得越发和蔼可亲、引人入胜。
“我知道你是个精明过人、见多识广的能人。你曾经跟随过的让德龙医生，刚才还在夸奖你的过人聪慧呢。”
接骨郎中浑身猛地一颤，这细微的动作丝毫没有逃过普朗塔的眼睛，老人继续说道：“没错，好心肠的医生夸你是他见过的最聪明的助手。‘罗伯洛，’他说，‘在化学方面有着极高的悟性，而且钻研起来兴趣浓厚，以至于在许多极其精细复杂的实验操作上，他的手段绝不下于我。’ ”
“可不是嘛！我那会儿可是使出了吃奶的劲儿，因为东家给的工钱丰厚，而且我向来酷爱钻研这门道。”
“而且你在让德龙医生府上真称得上是个得意门生呢，罗伯洛师傅；他钻研的学问可稀奇得很。尤其是关于毒物学的著述和实验，更是举世瞩目。”
罗伯洛的不安越发明显；他的目光开始游移不定。
“是的；”他应道，“我确实见识过一些稀奇古怪的实验。”

===Original===

“Well, you see, you may think yourself lucky﻿—for the doctor is going to have a splendid chance to study this sort of thing, and he will undoubtedly want you to assist him.”
But Robelot was too shrewd not to have already guessed that this cross-examination had a purpose. What was M. Plantat after? he asked himself, not without a vague terror. And, going over in his mind the questions which had been asked, and the answers he had given, and to what these questions led, he trembled. He thought to escape further questioning by saying:
“I am always at my old master’s orders when he needs me.”
“He’ll need you, be assured,” said M. Plantat, who added, in a careless tone, which his rapid glance at Robelot belied, “The interest attaching to this case will be intense, and the task difficult. Monsieur Sauvresy’s body is to be disinterred.”
Robelot was certainly prepared for something strange, and he was armed with all his audacity. But the name of Sauvresy fell upon his head like the stroke of a club, and he stammered, in a choked voice:
“Sauvresy!”
M. Plantat had already turned his head, and continued in an indifferent tone:
“Yes, Sauvresy is to be exhumed. It is suspected that his death was not wholly a natural one. You see, justice always has its suspicions.”
Robelot leaned against the wall so as not to fall. M. Plantat proceeded:
“So Doctor Gendron has been applied to. He has, as you know, found reactive drugs which betray the presence of an alkaloid, whatever it may be, in the substances submitted to him for analysis. He has spoken to me of a certain sensitive paper﻿—”
Appealing to all his energy, Robelot forced himself to stand up and resume a calm countenance.
“I know Doctor Gendron’s process,” said he, “but I don’t see who could be capable of the suspicions of which you speak.”
“I think there are more than suspicions,” resumed M. Plantat. “Madame de Trémorel, you know, has been murdered: her papers have, of course, been examined; letters have been found, with very damaging revelations, receipts, and so on.”
Robelot, apparently, was once more self-possessed; he forced himself to answer:
“*Bast!* let us hope that justice is in the wrong.”

===Chinese===

“那么你看，你尽可以庆幸自己的运气——因为医生马上就会迎来一个千载难逢的大好机会来深入钻研这门学问，毫无疑问，他一定会找你来当他的得力助手。”
然而罗伯洛精明过人，早已猜出这场刨根问底的盘问背后必有深意。普朗塔到底想干什么？他在心底暗自盘算着，不免泛起一阵莫名的恐惧。当他在脑海中重温方才的所有提问、自己的应答，以及这些提问暗藏的矛头所向时，他忍不住浑身战栗。他试图用一句话搪塞过去，避开进一步的逼问：
“只要老东家用得着，小人随时听候差遣。”
“他肯定用得着你，你大可放宽心，”普朗塔先生说，接着用一种满不在乎的口吻补充道，然而他投向罗伯洛的那记锐利如鹰隼的目光却出卖了内心的真实意图，“这个案子牵扯出的利害非同小可，差事也极其艰巨。索夫勒齐先生（Monsieur Sauvresy）的遗体即将被开棺验尸。”
罗伯洛心里固然料到会有怪事发生，也做好了孤注一掷的狂妄准备。然而“索夫勒齐”这三个字落在他头上，却犹如一记当头闷棍，他嗓音干瘪窒息，结结巴巴地吐出一句：
“索夫勒齐！”
普朗塔先生早已把头偏向一旁，继续用事不关己的冷淡口吻说道：
“没错，索夫勒齐要被掘棺验尸了。人们怀疑他的暴毙并非纯属自然天寿。你明白的，官府向来多疑。”
罗伯洛不得不死死倚靠住墙壁，才不至于当场瘫软在地。普朗塔先生继续往下说：
“所以官府已经正式求助于让德龙医生。正如你所熟知的那样，他已经研制出了某种显色反应试剂，送去化验的检材中无论含有何种生物碱毒物，都难逃试剂的法眼。他曾向我提起过某种极其灵敏的特制试纸——”
罗伯洛调动起全身所有的精力，强撑着站直身子，竭力恢复从容的神色。
“我知道让德龙医生的独门秘法，”他说，“但我实在想不通，有谁会起你刚才说的那种疑心。”
“我想这恐怕远不止是捕风捉影的怀疑了，”普朗塔先生接着说，“你知道的，特雷莫雷尔夫人遇害了：她的私密文件自然已经被官府逐一搜查过了；里面翻出了好些极具杀伤力的揭发密信、收条账单，诸如此类。”
罗伯洛表面上似乎重新稳住了阵脚；他勉强应付道：
“嗨！但愿官府是白忙活一场冤枉了好人吧。”

===Original===

Then, such was this man’s self-control, despite a nervous trembling which shook his whole body as the wind does the leaves, that he added, constraining his thin lips to form a smile:
“Madame Courtois does not come down; I am waited for at home, and will drop in again tomorrow. Good evening, gentlemen.”
He walked away, and soon the sand in the court was heard creaking with his steps. As he went, he staggered like a drunken man.
M. Lecoq went up to M. Plantat, and taking off his hat:
“I surrender,” said he, “and bow to you; you are great, like my master, the great Tabaret.”
The detective’s amour-propre was clearly aroused; his professional zeal was inspired; he found himself before a great crime﻿—one of those crimes which triple the sale of the *Gazette of the Courts*. Doubtless many of its details escaped him: he was ignorant of the starting-point; but he saw the way clearing before him. He had surprised Plantat’s theory, and had followed the train of his thought step by step; thus he discovered the complications of the crime which seemed so simple to M. Domini. His subtle mind had connected together all the circumstances which had been disclosed to him during the day, and now he sincerely admired the old justice of the peace. As he gazed at his beloved portrait, he thought, “Between the two of us﻿—this old fox and I﻿—we will unravel the whole web.” He would not, however, show himself to be inferior to his companion.
“Monsieur,” said he, “while you were questioning this rogue, who will be very useful to us, I did not lose any time. I’ve been looking about, under the furniture and so on, and have found this slip of paper.”
“Let’s see.”
“It is the envelope of the young lady’s letter. Do you know where her aunt, whom she was visiting, lives?”
“At Fontainebleau, I believe.”
“Ah; well, this envelope is stamped ‘Paris,’ Saint-Lazare branch post-office. I know this stamp proves nothing﻿—”
“It is, of course, an indication.”
“That is not all; I have read the letter itself﻿—it was here on the table.”
M. Plantat frowned involuntarily.
“It was, perhaps, a liberty,” resumed M. Lecoq, “but the end justifies the means. Well, you have read this letter; but have you studied it, examined the handwriting, weighed the words, remarked the context of the sentences?”
“Ah,” cried Plantat, “I was not mistaken then﻿—you had the same idea strike you that occurred to me!”

===Chinese===

接着，尽管神经质的剧烈颤抖像狂风摇曳树叶般撕扯着他的周身，这人依然凭借惊人的意志力控制住了自己，他强扯着薄薄的嘴唇挤出一丝惨笑，补充道：
“库尔图瓦夫人一时半会儿怕是下不来了；家里还有人候着我呢，我明儿个再登门拜访。诸位先生，小的告退了。”
他快步离去，很快院子里的碎石路上便响起了他沉重的脚步声。走动时，他摇摇晃晃得宛如一个烂醉如泥的醉汉。
勒科克先生走近普朗塔先生，摘下帽子：
“我甘拜下风，”他说，“向您致敬；您真伟大，就像我的恩师、大名鼎鼎的塔巴雷（Tabaret）一样伟大。”
这位大侦探的自尊心显然被彻底激发了；他的职业雄心在熊熊燃烧；他发觉自己正面对着一桩惊天大案——那种能让《法庭公报》（Gazette of the Courts）销量暴涨三倍的离奇大案。毫无疑问，许多案件细节他尚未掌握：他尚不知道谜局的发端；但他已经看清了通往真相的康庄大道。他窥破了普朗塔的推论假说，步步紧跟老人的缜密思绪；从而敏锐地洞察到了这桩在多米尼先生看来如此一目了然的命案背后错综复杂的深潭。他那敏锐深邃的心智，将白日里展现在他面前的所有线索丝丝入扣地串联在了一起，此刻他对这位老治安法官由衷地佩服得五体投地。凝视着老人那副令他敬仰的神态，他暗想：“凭我们俩联手——这只老狐狸和我——我们必定能把整张罗网抽丝剥茧彻底拆解开来。”然而，他也不愿在同伴面前显得相形见绌。
“先生，”他说，“当您在盘问这个将来对我们大有用处的无赖时，我可也没闲着。我一直在四周仔细搜寻，在家具底下摸索，结果找到了这张小纸条。”
“拿来看看。”
“这是那位年轻小姐绝笔信的信封。您知道她在外省投奔的姑妈住在何处吗？”
“我想是在枫丹白露（Fontainebleau）。”
“啊；可是，这个信封上的邮戳赫然盖着‘巴黎’圣拉扎尔（Saint-Lazare）区分局的印记。我知道单凭一个邮戳证明不了什么——”
“这当然是一条极具价值的线索。”
“这还不是全部；那封绝笔信我也一字不落拜读过了——信当时就摊在桌上。”
普朗塔先生不由自主地皱起了眉头。
“这或许有些冒失，”勒科克先生接着说，“但为了查清大案不拘小节嘛。那么，您也读过这封信了；但您可曾仔细研读推敲过它，检验过笔迹，称量过措辞，留意过字里行间的句式脉络吗？”
“啊，”普朗塔失声叫道，“看来我没看走眼——你想到的正是我脑海中闪过的念头！”

===Original===

And, in the energy of his excitement he seized the detective’s hands and pressed them as if he were an old friend. They were about to resume talking when a step was heard on the staircase; and presently Dr. Gendron appeared.
“Courtois is better,” said he, “he is in a doze, and will recover.”
“We have nothing more, then, to keep us here,” returned M. Plantat. “Let’s be off. Monsieur Lecoq must be half dead with hunger.”
As they went away, M. Lecoq slipped Laurence’s letter, with the envelope, into his pocket.

===Chinese===

在极度激动的亢奋之中，他一把紧紧攥住大侦探的双手，像对待多年的老友般用力握了握。他们正准备进一步深谈，楼梯上突然传来了脚步声；紧接着让德龙医生现身了。
“库尔图瓦好多了，”他说，“他已经合眼打了个盹，性命无碍了。”
“既然如此，我们再留在这里也没什么意义了，”普朗塔先生说，“咱们走吧。勒科克先生想必早就饿得前胸贴后背了。”
临出门时，勒科克先生悄悄把洛朗斯的绝笔信连同信封一起塞进了自己的大衣口袋。

## V / 第五章
===Original===

The staircase had been put under guard, but the vestibule had remained free. People were heard coming and going, tramping and coughing; then rising above this continuous noise, the oaths of the gendarmes trying to keep back the crowd. From time to time, a scared face passed by the dining-room door, which was ajar. These were curious folks who, more daring than the rest, wished to see the “men of justice” eating, and tried to hear a word or two, to report them, and so become important in the eyes of the others. But the “men of justice”﻿—as they said at Orcival﻿—took care to say nothing of moment while the doors were open, and while a servant was passing to and fro. Greatly moved by this frightful crime, disturbed by the mystery which surrounded it, they hid their impressions. Each, on his part, studied the probability of his suspicions, and kept his opinion to himself.
M. Domini, as he ate, put his notes in order, numbering the leaves, marking certain peculiarly significant answers of the suspected persons with a cross. He was, perhaps, the least tormented of the four companions at this funereal repast. The crime did not seem to him one of those which keep judges of instruction sleepless through the night; he saw clearly the motive of it; and he had Bertaud and Guespin, two of the assassins, or at least accomplices, secure.
M. Plantat and Dr. Gendron, seated next each other, were talking of the illness which carried off Sauvresy. M. Courtois listened to the hubbub without.
The news of the double murder was soon noised about the neighborhood, and the crowd increased every minute. It filled the court, and became bolder and bolder; the gendarmes were overwhelmed. Then or never was the time for the mayor to show his authority. “I am going to make these people listen to reason,” said he, “and make them retire.” And at once, wiping his mouth, he threw his tumbled napkin on the table, and went out.
It was time. The brigadier’s injunctions were no longer heeded. Some curious people, more eager than the rest, had flanked the position and were forcing an entrance through the gate leading to the garden. The mayor’s presence did not perhaps intimidate the crowd much, but it redoubled the energy of the gendarmes; the vestibule was cleared, amid murmurings against the arm of the law. What a chance for a speech! M. Courtois was not wanting to the occasion.

===Chinese===

楼梯虽然派了人看守，门厅里却依旧人来人往。只听得脚步声、走动声与咳嗽声此起彼伏；接着压过这连绵嘈杂声浪的，是宪兵们竭力阻挡人群的怒骂声。不时有一张张惊慌失措的面孔从半掩着的餐厅门边晃过。这些都是比常人胆子更大的好事之徒，他们巴望着瞧一瞧“官府的老爷们”是用膳的，并千方百计想偷听一两句话，好跑出去向其他人炫耀，从而在众人眼中显得举足轻重。但“官府的老爷们”——正如奥尔西瓦尔村民所称呼的那样——在门虚掩着、且不时有仆人端茶倒水穿梭其间的时候，自然十分谨慎，绝不轻吐半句要紧话。他们被这起惨绝人寰的凶案深深触动，又为笼罩在案情上的重重迷雾所困扰，各自掩饰着内心的真实想法。每个人都在暗自推敲各自怀疑的合理性，对案情秘而不宣。
多米尼先生一边用餐，一边整理着卷宗笔录，给各页编上号码，并在嫌疑人某些格外意味深长的供词旁打上叉号。在这顿气氛阴森的便餐中，他或许是四位同伴中心情最轻松的一个。在他看来，这起罪案绝非那种会让预审法官彻夜难眠的疑难悬案；作案动机昭然若揭；而且贝尔托和盖斯潘这两个凶手、或至少是从犯，已经被他稳稳收押在大牢里了。
挨坐在一起的普朗塔先生和让德龙医生，正在低声探讨当年夺走索夫勒齐性命的那场怪病。库尔图瓦先生则心神不宁地侧耳倾听着门外的喧哗。
双重谋杀的惨剧很快在四邻八乡传得沸沸扬扬，聚集的人群每分每秒都在激增。院子里挤得水泄不通，人群的胆子也越来越大；宪兵们渐渐招架不住了。此情此景，正是镇长大展官威的绝佳时机。“我得去让这帮乡民听听公理，”他说，“好叫他们趁早散去。”说罢，他擦了擦嘴，把揉成一团的餐巾往桌上一扔，快步走了出去。
来得正是时候。班长声嘶力竭的喝令已经没人理睬了。几个格外急切的好奇之徒抄了近道，正强行硬闯通往花园的小铁门。镇长的现身或许未能对人群产生多大的震慑，却让宪兵们精神大振；伴随着对抗拒执法者的厉声斥责，门厅总算被清空了。这可是发表宏论的天赐良机！库尔图瓦先生自然绝不肯错失良机。

===Original===

He believed that his eloquence, endowed with the virtues of a cold showerbath, would calm this unwonted effervescence of his constituency. He stepped forward upon the steps, his left hand resting in the opening of his vest, gesturing with his right in the proud and impassible attitude which the sculptor lends to great orators. It was thus that he posed before his council when, finding unexpected opposition, he undertook to impose his will upon them, and recall the recalcitrant members to their duty.
His speech, in fragments, penetrated to the dining-room. According as he turned to the right or to the left, his voice was clear and distinct, or was lost in space. He said:
“Fellow-citizens, an atrocious crime, unheard of before in our commune, has shocked our peaceable and honest neighborhood. I understand and excuse your feverish emotion, your natural indignation. As well as you, my friends, more than you﻿—I cherished and esteemed the noble Count de Trémorel, and his virtuous wife. We mourn them together﻿—”
“I assure you,” said Dr. Gendron to M. Plantat, “that the symptoms you describe are not uncommon after pleurisy. From the acute state, the inflammation passes to the chronic state, and becomes complicated with pneumonia.”
“But nothing,” pursued the mayor, “can justify a curiosity, which by its importunate attempts to be satisfied, embarrasses the investigation, and is, at all events, a punishable interference with the cause of justice. Why this unwonted gathering? Why these rumors and noises? These premature conjectures?”
“There were several consultations,” said M. Plantat, “which did not have favorable results. Sauvresy suffered altogether strange and unaccountable tortures. He complained of troubles so unwonted, so absurd, if you’ll excuse the word, that he discouraged all the conjectures of the most experienced physicians.”
“Was it not R﻿—﻿—, of Paris, who attended him?”
“Exactly. He came daily, and often remained overnight. Many times I have seen him ascending the principal street of the village, with troubled countenance, as he went to give his prescription to the apothecary.”
“Be wise enough,” cried M. Courtois, “to moderate your just anger; be calm; be dignified.”
“Surely,” continued Dr.

===Chinese===

他深信自己的雄辩口才犹如一盆兜头浇下的冷水，足以平息选民们这阵罕见的狂热骚动。他昂首阔步登上台阶，左手插在马甲前襟，右手挥舞着做出雕塑家赋予大演说家的那种高傲而威严的姿态。平日里在市政议会上，一旦遭遇意外的阻力，他便是以这副架势力压群雄，强令桀骜不驯的议员们服从大局的。
他的演讲片断断续续地飘进餐厅。随着他身体左右转动，他的嗓音时而清晰洪亮，时而消散在空旷的风中。只听他说道：
“同胞们，一桩在我们公社闻所未闻的骇人罪案，惊扰了我们安宁祥和的邻里。我理解并体谅你们焦躁的情绪与出于义愤的激荡。我和你们一样，我的朋友们，甚至比你们更甚——我深切爱戴并敬重高贵的特雷莫雷尔伯爵及其贤良淑德的夫人。我们共同为他们哀悼——”
“我敢向您保证，”让德龙医生对普朗塔先生说，“您方才描述的那些病症在胸膜炎之后并不罕见。炎症从急性转入慢性，并并发了严重的肺炎。”
“然而任何理由，”镇长继续高声宣布，“都不能为这种无理取闹的窥探欲开脱，这种纠缠不休的窥探只会阻碍案情侦查，归根结底是对司法大局的违法干扰。何必聚众滋事？何必谣言四起、喧哗吵闹？何必妄加揣测？”
“当年曾组织过好几次专家会诊，”普朗塔先生说，“但都没有任何良效。索夫勒齐承受的折磨极其诡异离奇、不可名状。他所抱怨的那些痛苦是如此匪夷所思，原谅我用这个词，甚至荒诞不经，以至于让经验最老到的名医的所有推断都彻底落空。”
“当年不是巴黎的R——名医亲自为他诊治的吗？”
“一点不错。他每天都赶来，甚至常常留宿过夜。多少次我亲眼瞧见他神色凝重地走在村里的主街上，前去药房给药剂师递药方。”
“拿出你们的理智来吧，”库尔图瓦先生大喊道，“克制你们正当的怒火；保持冷静；保持体面！”
“平心而论，”让德龙医生继续说道。

===Original===

Gendron, “your apothecary is an intelligent man; but you have at Orcival a fellow who quite outdoes him, a fellow who knows how to make money; one Robelot﻿—”
“Robelot, the bonesetter?”
“That’s the man. I suspect him of giving consultations, and prescribing sub rosa. He is very clever. In fact I educated him. Five or six years ago, he was my laboratory boy, and even now I employ him when I have a delicate operation on hand﻿—”
The doctor stopped, struck by the alteration in the impassible Plantat’s features.
“What is the matter, my friend?” he asked. “Are you ill?”
The judge left his notes, to look at him. “Why,” said he, “Monsieur Plantat is very pale﻿—”
But M. Plantat speedily resumed his habitual expression.
“ ’Tis nothing,” he answered, “really nothing. With my abominable stomach, as soon as I change my hour of eating﻿—”
Having reached his peroration, M. Courtois raised his voice.
“Return,” said he, “to your peaceable homes, your quiet avocations. Rest assured the law protects you. Already justice has begun its work; two of the criminals are in its power, and we are on the track of their accomplices.”
“Of all the servants of the château,” remarked M. Plantat, “there remains not one who knew Sauvresy. The domestics have one by one been replaced.”
“No doubt,” answered the doctor, “the sight of the old servants would be disagreeable to Monsieur de Trémorel.”
He was interrupted by the mayor, who reentered, his eyes glowing, his face animated, wiping his forehead.
“I have let the people know,” said he, “the indecency of their curiosity. They have all gone away. They were anxious to get at Philippe Bertaud, the brigadier says; public opinion has a sharp scent.”
Hearing the door open, he turned, and found himself face to face with a man whose features were scarcely visible, so profoundly did he bow, his hat pressed against his breast.
“What do you wish?” sternly asked M. Courtois. “By what right have you come in here?﻿—Who are you?”
The man drew himself up.
“I am Monsieur Lecoq,” he replied, with a gracious smile. “Monsieur Lecoq of the detective force, sent by the prefect of police in reply to a telegram, for this affair.”
This declaration clearly surprised all present, even the judge of instruction.
In France, each profession has its special externals, as it were, insignia, which betray it at first view.

===Chinese===

“你们村里的药剂师确实是个明白人；但你们奥尔西瓦尔却有一个家伙手段远胜于他，那是个极擅长敛财的精明人；就是那个罗伯洛——”
“接骨郎中罗伯洛？”
“正是此人。我怀疑他私下里给人问诊开方。他脑子极灵光。事实上他是我一手带出来的。五六年前，他在我的实验室里当杂工，即便现在，遇上精细复杂的实验操作我也常常差遣他——”
医生戛然而止，被冷峻的普朗塔脸上掠过的一抹异样神色惊呆了。
“怎么了，我的朋友？”他关切地问，“身子不舒服吗？”
预审法官也从卷宗笔记中抬起头来端详着他。“哎呀，”法官说，“普朗塔先生脸色好苍白——”
然而普朗塔先生迅速恢复了惯常的神色。
“没什么，”他答道，“真的没什么。我这糟糕透顶的胃，只要一换吃饭的时辰就闹毛病——”
演说进入了尾声，库尔图瓦先生提高了嗓门。
“回到你们安宁的家中去吧，”他喊道，“回到你们日常的本职营生中去吧。请诸位放心，法律必将护佑大家。正义之剑已然出鞘；两名罪犯已落入法网，我们正在追捕其余同谋。”
“在城堡里的所有下人中，”普朗塔先生低声说道，“已经没有一个当年伺候过索夫勒齐的老人了。仆人们被陆陆续续全换了个遍。”
“这是自然的，”医生回答，“眼见那些昔日的老仆，难免会让特雷莫雷尔先生触景生情、心生不快。”
镇长推门而入，打断了他们的交谈；只见他两眼放光，面色红润，一边擦着满头大汗。
“我已经让这帮乡民明白了，”他说，“他们那份好奇心有多么不知大体。他们全都散去了。班长说，大伙儿刚才甚至恨不得把菲利普·贝尔托生吞活剥了；民意这东西嗅觉可真够灵敏的。”
听到门轴转动声，他转过身来，迎面撞见一个男子；那人身子鞠得极低，帽子紧紧捂在胸口，面容几乎看不真切。
“你想干什么？”库尔图瓦先生严厉地喝问道，“你凭什么擅闯进来？——你究竟是什么人？”
那名男子直起身来。
“在下勒科克先生，”他带着优雅得体的微笑应道，“巴黎警察厅刑侦处勒科克，奉警察厅长电令，特为本案前来效力。”
这番自报家门显然让在场的所有人吃了一惊，就连预审法官也不例外。
在法兰西，每一个行当都有其独特的外部行头，可以说是一望便知的标志性徽记。

===Original===

Each profession has its conventional type, and when public opinion has adopted a type, it does not admit it possible that the type should be departed from. What is a doctor? A grave man, all in black, with a white cravat. A gentleman with a capacious stomach, adorned with heavy gold seals, can only be a banker. Everybody knows that the artist is a merry liver, with a peaked hat, a velvet vest, and enormous ruffles. By virtue of this rule, the detective of the prefecture ought to have an eye full of mystery, something suspicious about him, a negligence of dress, and imitation jewelry. The most obtuse shopkeeper is sure that he can scent a detective at twenty paces; a big man with mustaches, and a shining felt hat, his throat imprisoned by a collar of hair, dressed in a black, threadbare surtout, carefully buttoned up on account of the entire absence of linen. Such is the type. But, according to this, M. Lecoq, as he entered the dining-room at Valfeuillu, had by no means the air of a detective. True, M. Lecoq can assume whatever air he pleases. His friends declare that he has a physiognomy peculiar to himself, which he resumes when he enters his own house, and which he retains by his own fireside, with his slippers on; but the fact is not well proved. What is certain, is that his mobile face lends itself to strange metamorphoses; that he moulds his features according to his will, as the sculptor moulds clay for modelling. He changes everything, even his look.
“So,” said the judge of instruction, “the prefect has sent you to me, in case certain investigations become necessary.”
“Yes, Monsieur, quite at your service.”
M. Lecoq had on this day assumed a handsome wig of lank hair, of that vague color called Paris blonde, parted on the side by a line pretentiously fanciful; whiskers of the same color puffed out with bad pomade, encircled a pallid face. His big eyes seemed congealed within their red border, an open smile rested on his thick lips, which, in parting, discovered a range of long yellow teeth. His face, otherwise, expressed nothing in particular. It was a nearly equal mixture of timidity, self-sufficiency, and contentment. It was quite impossible to concede the least intelligence to the possessor of such a phiz. One involuntarily looked for a goitre.

===Chinese===

每个行当都有世俗公认的刻板脸谱，而一旦公众接纳了这种脸谱，便绝不承认有任何偏离常规的可能。大夫是什么模样？必定是个一身黑衣、系着白领结的严肃绅士。而一个大腹便便、挂着沉甸甸金表链的体面人，除了银行家绝无第二种可能。人尽皆知，艺术家向来是放浪形骸的快活汉，头戴尖顶帽，身着天鹅绒背心，胸前缀着夸张的荷叶边褶饰。照这套规矩，巴黎警察厅的秘密刑警眼中必定写满了诡秘与阴鸷，举止带着见不得光的鬼祟，衣衫邋遢，佩戴着假金首饰。哪怕最愚钝的铺子掌柜，也笃信自己在20步之外就能嗅出便衣暗探的气味：一个留着大胡子、戴着锃亮毡帽的魁梧大汉，脖子被一圈硬领死死勒住，身穿一件因里面没穿衬衫而扣得严严实实的褪色黑大衣。这就是人们心目中的刑警脸谱。然而按照这种标准，走进瓦尔菲伊餐厅的勒科克先生，可没有半点刑警的影子。当然，勒科克先生能够随意换上任何他需要的伪装面孔。他的朋友们断言他拥有一张唯独属于他自己的本真面孔，唯有踏进自家家门、换上拖鞋坐在自家壁炉前时才会展露；但这传说至今未能得到证实。可以确定的是，他那张变幻莫测的脸孔极擅长令人叹为观止的化装易容；他能随心所欲地捏塑自己的五官面貌，宛如雕塑家揉捏黏土塑像一般。他能改头换面改变一切，甚至连目光都能换一个人。
“这么说，”预审法官说，“厅长派你前来协助我，以防案情需要展开某些特定的侦查。”
“是的，法官先生，随时听候差遣。”
勒科克先生今天特意戴了一顶浅色直发的体面假发，那颜色正是所谓含混不清的“巴黎浅亚麻色”，发缝刻意偏向一侧，显得颇有几分造作滑稽；同样颜色的连鬓络腮胡涂抹着劣质发膏，蓬松地簇拥着一张苍白无血色的面孔。那双大眼睛在泛红的眼眶里仿佛凝固了一般，厚厚的嘴唇上挂着开朗的笑容，一笑便露出一排焦黄的长牙。除此以外，他的脸上看不出任何鲜明的情绪。那几乎是怯懦、自满与乐天知足的奇特混合体。任何人看着这样一张尊容，都绝不可能承认其主人拥有半点过人的心智。人们甚至会不由自主地怀疑他脖子上是不是长着大脖子病。

===Original===

The retail haberdashers, who, having cheated for thirty years in their threads and needles, retire with large incomes, should have such heads as this. His apparel was as dull as his person. His coat resembled all coats, his trousers all trousers. A hair chain, the same color as his whiskers, was attached to a large silver watch, which bulged out his left waistcoat pocket. While speaking, he fumbled with a confection-box made of transparent horn, full of little square lozenges, and adorned by a portrait of a very homely, well-dressed woman﻿—“the defunct,” no doubt. As the conversation proceeded, according as he was satisfied or disturbed, M. Lecoq munched a lozenge, or directed glances toward the portrait which were quite a poem in themselves.
Having examined the man a long time, the judge of instruction shrugged his shoulders. “Well,” said M. Domini, finally, “now that you are here, we will explain to you what has occurred.”
“Oh, that’s quite useless,” responded Lecoq, with a satisfied air, “perfectly useless, sir.”
“Nevertheless, it is necessary that you should know﻿—”
“What? that which monsieur the judge knows?” interrupted the detective, “for that I already know. Let us agree there has been a murder, with theft as its motive; and start from that point. The countess’s body has been found﻿—not so that of the count. What else? Bertaud, an acknowledged rogue, is arrested; he merits a little punishment, doubtless. Guespin came back drunk; ah, there are sad charges against this Guespin! His past is deplorable; it is not known where he passed the night, he refuses to answer, he brings no alibi﻿—this is indeed grave!”
M. Plantat gazed at the detective with visible pleasure.
“Who has told you about these things?” asked M. Domini.
“Well﻿—everybody has told me a little.”
“But where?”
“Here: I’ve already been here two hours, and even heard the mayor’s speech.”
And, satisfied with the effect he had produced, M. Lecoq munched a lozenge.
“You were not aware, then,” resumed the judge, “that I was waiting for you?”
“Pardon me,” said the detective; “I hope you will be kind enough to hear me. You see, it is indispensable to study the ground; one must look about, establish his batteries. I am anxious to catch the general rumor﻿—public opinion, as they say, so as to distrust it.”
“All this,” answered M. Domini, severely, “does not justify your delay.”
M.

===Chinese===

那些在布匹针线生意里倒腾克扣了30年、退休后坐拥丰厚年金的零售布商，大体就是顶着这么一颗脑袋。他的衣着同他的人一样平庸无奇。外套同世间所有的外套别无二致，裤子同世间所有的裤子毫无区别。一条与胡须同色的毛发编织表链，系在一块沉甸甸的银质怀表上，把左侧马甲口袋撑得鼓鼓囊囊。说话间，他手里不停把玩着一个透明牛角糖果盒，里面装满了方块小含片，盒盖上镶嵌着一位穿着讲究却奇丑无比的妇人画像——毫无疑问正是“先室”。在交谈的过程中，随着心情的舒畅或受挫，勒科克先生要么吧唧嚼上一枚含片，要么含情脉脉地朝画像投去一瞥，那一瞥的神韵本身就堪称一首绝妙的抒情诗。
预审法官将这人从头到脚打量了良久，终于耸了耸肩。“好吧，”多米尼先生终于开口道，“既然你已经到了，我们就给你讲讲案发经过。”
“噢，那大可不必，”勒科克满脸得意地应道，“完全没有必要，先生。”
“然而，有些情况你必须知晓——”
“知晓什么？法官大人所知晓的那些情况吗？”刑警打断道，“因为那些事在下早已一清二楚了。咱们不妨先认定这是一起图财害命的凶杀案，并以此为出发点。伯爵夫人的遗体已经找到了——伯爵的尸体尚未寻获。还有什么？贝尔托，一个众所皆知的无赖，已经被捕归案；无疑理该受到些惩罚。盖斯潘喝得烂醉如泥折返回来；哎呀，压在盖斯潘头上的罪证可真叫人不寒而栗！他底细糟糕透顶；昨夜究竟在何处过夜下落不明，他拒不交代，又拿不出不在场证明——这可是要掉脑袋的大罪啊！”
普朗塔先生眼中流露出显而易见的赞许之色，凝视着大侦探。
“这些情况是谁透露给你的？”多米尼先生惊奇地问。
“这个嘛——每个人都告诉了我一点儿。”
“在什么地方？”
“就在这儿啊：我已经在这儿转悠了两个小时了，甚至连镇长大人的精彩演说我都一字不落听全了。”
对自己制造的震撼效果深感满意，勒科克先生嚼了一颗含片。
“这么说，你明明知道我一直在等候你，却故意拖延？”法官追问道。
“请恕在下冒昧，”刑警说，“还望法官大人宽宏大量听我解释。您瞧，勘查现场熟悉地形是必不可少的功夫；一个人必须四下观察，架设好自己的火力炮台。我渴望探听街谈巷议——也就是人们常说的民意舆情，好在办案中对它们保持警惕。”
“这一切，”多米尼先生严厉地回答道，“都不能为你姗姗来迟开脱。”

===Original===

Lecoq glanced tenderly at the portrait.
“Monsieur the judge,” said he, “has only to inquire at the prefecture, and he will learn that I know my profession. The great thing requisite, in order to make an effective search, is to remain unknown. The police are not popular. Now, if they knew who I was, and why I was here, I might go out, but nobody would tell me anything; I might ask questions﻿—they’d serve me a hundred lies; they would distrust me, and hold their tongues.”
“Quite true﻿—quite true,” murmured Plantat, coming to the support of the detective.
M. Lecoq went on:
“So that when I was told that I was going into the country, I put on my country face and clothes. I arrive here and everybody, on seeing me, says to himself, ‘Here’s a curious bumpkin, but not a bad fellow.’ Then I slip about, listen, talk, make the rest talk! I ask this question and that, and am answered frankly; I inform myself, gather hints, no one troubles himself about me. These Orcival folks are positively charming; why, I’ve already made several friends, and am invited to dine this very evening.”
M. Domini did not like the police, and scarcely concealed it. He rather submitted to their cooperation than accepted it, solely because he could not do without them. While listening to M. Lecoq, he could not but approve of what he said; yet he looked at him with an eye by no means friendly.
“Since you know so much about the matter,” observed he, dryly, “we will proceed to examine the scene of the crime.”
“I am quite at Monsieur the judge’s orders,” returned the detective, laconically. As everyone was getting up, he took the opportunity to offer M. Plantat his lozenge-box.
“Monsieur perhaps uses them?”
Plantat, unwilling to decline, appropriated a lozenge, and the detective’s face became again serene. Public sympathy was necessary to him, as it is to all great comedians.

===Chinese===

勒科克深情款款地瞥了一眼盒盖上的画像。
“法官大人，”他说，“只需向警察厅随便打听打听，便会知晓我向来深谙本职之道。要想展开真正卓有成效的侦查，最关键的秘诀在于隐姓埋名。警察向来不受老百姓待见。若是他们知晓我是何许人也、为何而来，我走在大街上，绝不会有人吐露半句真言；我四处打听——他们只会拿100句谎话来敷衍我；他们会提防我，把嘴巴闭得严严实实。”
“千真万确——一点不错，”普朗塔低声附和道，替大侦探帮腔。
勒科克先生接着说道：
“因此，当我奉命要下乡办案时，我立刻换上了乡巴佬的面孔和行头。我一来到这里，所有人一瞧见我，心里都会琢磨：‘这准是个爱管闲事的乡下土包子，不过心眼倒不坏。’于是我四处钻营，侧耳倾听，搭讪攀谈，引逗旁人竹筒倒豆子！我东问一句西扯一把，大家全都推心置腹如实相告；我刺探情报，收集线索，谁也不会对我起半点疑心。奥尔西瓦尔的乡亲们可真叫人如沐春风；嗨，我已经交上了好几个朋友，今晚甚至还应邀赴宴呢。”
多米尼先生向来瞧不上秘密刑警，而且几乎不加掩饰。他对刑警的协助更多是迫于无奈的容忍而非由衷的接纳，纯粹是因为案情离不开这帮人的手段。听着勒科克先生的高论，他虽然挑不出半点毛病，投向对方的目光却毫无善意。
“既然你对案情已经了如指掌，”法官冷冰冰地指出，“那我们就去勘验案发现场吧。”
“悉听法官大人差遣，”刑警言简意赅地应承道。趁着众人起身的当口，他借机向普朗塔先生递上了自己的含片盒。
“先生也许想来上一颗？”
普朗塔不便推辞，便拈起了一颗含片，刑警的脸上顿时重新洋溢起宁静祥和的神色。博得公众的好感对他来说至关重要，正如所有伟大的喜剧大师皆是如此。

## VI / 第六章
===Original===

M. Lecoq was the first to reach the staircase, and the spots of blood at once caught his eye.
“Oh,” cried he, at each spot he saw, “oh, oh, the wretches!”
M. Courtois was much moved to find so much sensibility in a detective. The latter, as he continued to ascend, went on:
“The wretches! They don’t often leave traces like this everywhere﻿—or at least they wipe them out.”
On gaining the first landing, and the door of the boudoir which led into the chamber, he stopped, eagerly scanning, before he entered, the position of the rooms.
Then he entered the boudoir, saying:
“Come; I don’t see my way clear yet.”
“But it seems to me,” remarked the judge, “that we have already important materials to aid your task. It is clear that Guespin, if he is not an accomplice, at least knew something about the crime.”
M. Lecoq had recourse to the portrait in the lozenge-box. It was more than a glance, it was a confidence. He evidently said something to the dear defunct, which he dared not say aloud.
“I see that Guespin is seriously compromised,” resumed he. “Why didn’t he want to tell where he passed the night? But, then, public opinion is against him, and I naturally distrust *that*.”
The detective stood alone in the middle of the room, the rest, at his request, remained at the threshold, and looking keenly about him, searched for some explanation of the frightful disorder of the apartment.
“Fools!” cried he, in an irritated tone, “double brutes! Because they murder people so as to rob them, is no reason why they should break everything in the house. Sharp folks don’t smash up furniture; they carry pretty picklocks, which work well and make no noise. Idiots! one would say﻿—”
He stopped with his mouth wide open.
“Eh! Not so bungling, after all, perhaps.”
The witnesses of this scene remained motionless at the door, following, with an interest mingled with surprise, the detective’s movements.
Kneeling down, he passed his flat palm over the thick carpet, among the broken porcelain.
“It’s damp; very damp. The tea was not all drunk, it seems, when the cups were broken.”
“Some tea might have remained in the teapot,” suggested Plantat.
“I know it,” answered M. Lecoq, “just what I was going to say. So that this dampness cannot tell us the exact moment when the crime was committed.”
“But the clock does, and very exactly,” interrupted the mayor.
“The mayor,” said M.

勒科克先生第一个走上楼梯，台阶上的点点血迹立刻吸引了他的目光。
“哎呀，”每看到一处血迹，他都忍不住惊呼，“哎呀，哎呀，这帮天杀的恶棍！”
库尔图瓦先生见一名刑警竟然拥有如此细腻的悲悯情怀，不由得大受感动。大侦探一边继续拾级而上，一边自言自语道：
“这帮恶棍！他们可极少到处留下这样的血痕——即便留下了，也总会擦洗得干干净净。”
一登上二楼平台，来到通往卧室的那扇小闺房门前，他停下了脚步，在进门前急切而敏锐地扫视着各个房间的格局分布。
随后他迈步跨入小闺房，嘴里嘟囔着：
“得啦；我现在眼前还是一团漆黑。”
“但依我看，”预审法官指出，“我们手头已经掌握了许多重要线索，足以推进你的差事了。显而易见，盖斯潘就算不是从犯，至少也对这桩罪案内情心知肚明。”
勒科克先生又求助于牛角含片盒盖上的画像。那不仅仅是深情的一瞥，更像是一场无声的交心倾诉。他显然是在对亲爱的先室吐露某些不敢当众大声讲出的话语。
“我看得出盖斯潘身上的嫌疑极其严重，”刑警接着说道，“他为什么不肯交代昨夜究竟在哪儿过的夜？然而，既然众口一词把矛头对准了他，我本能地就对‘公论’怀有戒心。”
刑警独自伫立在房间中央，其余人按照他的要求留在门槛之外；他目光如炬地打量着四周，试图为这间屋子里令人触目惊心的狼藉寻找合理的解释。
“蠢货！”他语气恼怒地骂道，“彻头彻尾的蠢驴！哪怕杀人图财，也没必要把屋里的东西砸个稀巴烂吧。精明老道的高手绝不会砸烂家具；他们随身揣着精巧的万能撬锁工具，干起活来又利索又悄无声息。真是一群白痴！任谁都会说——”
他猛地张大嘴巴刹住了话头。
“咦！或许，他们也并没那么笨拙蠢笨呢。”
目睹这幕情景的人依然纹丝不动地立在门口，怀着混杂着好奇与惊叹的目光，注视着大侦探的一举一动。
只见他单膝跪地，将平展的手掌按在厚厚的地毯上，在满地摔碎的瓷片中摸索。
“地毯是湿的；湿得很厉害。看来茶杯被砸碎的时候，杯里的茶水还没喝光呢。”
“茶壶里也可能残留着茶水啊，”普朗塔提醒道。
“我知道，”勒科克先生答道，“这正是在下刚想说的。因此单凭这滩湿迹，还无法断定案发的准确钟点。”
“但那座钟能告诉我们，而且分毫不差，”镇长插话道。
“镇长，”多米尼先生说。

===Original===

Domini, “in his notes, well explains that the movements of the clock stopped when it fell.”
“But see here,” said M. Plantat, “it was the odd hour marked by that clock that struck me. The hands point to twenty minutes past three; yet we know that the countess was fully dressed, when she was struck. Was she up taking tea at three in the morning? It’s hardly probable.”
“I, too, was struck with that circumstance,” returned M. Lecoq, “and that’s why I said, ‘not so stupid!’ Well, let’s see.”
He lifted the clock with great care, and replaced it on the mantel, being cautious to set it exactly upright. The hands continued to point to twenty minutes past three.
