“Twenty past three!” muttered he, while slipping a little wedge under the stand. “People don’t take tea at that hour. Still less common is it that people are murdered at daylight.”
He opened the clock-case with some difficulty, and pushed the longer hand to the figure of half-past three.
The clock struck eleven!
“Good,” cried M. Lecoq, triumphantly. “That is the truth!” and drawing the lozenge-box from his pocket, he excitedly crushed a lozenge between his teeth.
The simplicity of this discovery surprised the spectators; the idea of trying the clock in this way had occurred to no one. M. Courtois, especially, was bewildered.
“There’s a fellow,” whispered he to the doctor, “who knows what he’s about.”
“Ergo,” resumed M. Lecoq (who knew Latin), “we have here, not brutes, as I thought at first, but rascals who looked beyond the end of their knife. They intended to put us off the scent, by deceiving us as to the hour.”
“I don’t see their object very clearly,” said M. Courtois, timidly.
“Yet it is easy to see it,” answered M. Domini. “Was it not for their interest to make it appear that the crime was committed after the last train for Paris had left? Guespin, leaving his companions at the Lyons station at nine, might have reached here at ten, murdered the count and countess, seized the money which he knew to be in the count’s possession, and returned to Paris by the last train.”
“These conjectures are very shrewd,” interposed M. Plantat; “but how is it that Guespin did not rejoin his comrades in the Batignolles? For in that way, to a certain degree, he might have provided a kind of alibi.”
Dr.

===Chinese===

“在他的勘验笔录里解释得很透彻，座钟是在摔落到地上的一瞬间停摆的。”
“不过诸位瞧瞧，”普朗塔先生插言道，“那座钟所指示的古怪时辰一直让我百思不得其解。指针赫然停在3点20分；然而我们心知肚明，伯爵夫人遇害时身上穿戴得整整齐齐。难道她在凌晨三点还会穿戴齐整地喝茶吗？这未免太不合常理了。”
“在下也同样被这个疑点给击中了，”勒科克先生接话道，“这正是在下刚才为何要说‘没那么蠢笨’的缘由！好吧，咱们来瞧个究竟。”
他小心翼翼地捧起座钟，将它重新安放在壁炉台上，万分谨慎地把它摆正扶直。表盘上的指针依然指着3点20分。
“3点20分！”他一边在底座下塞入一小块木楔一边低声自语，“谁也不会挑这个钟点喝茶。更罕见的是大清早天色破晓时分还会有人行凶杀人。”
他颇费了一番周折才扳开座钟的外壳，将分针拨到了3点半的位置。
当！当！当……座钟竟然足足敲了11下！
“漂亮！”勒科克先生喜不自胜地失声喊道，“这才是真相！”说着，他从口袋里摸出含片盒，激动万分地在齿间咬碎了一颗含片。
这项发现是如此简单明了，令在场的所有旁观者目瞪口呆；谁也没想到居然能用这种法子去查验那座钟。尤其是库尔图瓦先生，更是惊得目瞪口呆。
“瞧瞧这位，”他凑到医生耳边窃窃私语道，“真真是一个行家里手啊。”
“因此（Ergo），”懂得拉丁文的勒科克先生总结道，“我们眼下面对的，绝非我最初认定的粗鲁莽汉，而是一伙深谋远虑、眼光远超刀尖的狡黠恶徒。他们妄图通过在时间上做手脚，把我们引入歧途。”
“我不太明白他们这么做的企图何在，”库尔图瓦先生怯生生地问。
“这企图再一目了然不过了，”多米尼先生回答道，“让他们伪造出作案时间是在开往巴黎的末班火车开动之后，难道不正符合他们的利益吗？盖斯潘9点钟在里昂火车站离开同伴，完全可能在10点赶到这里，残杀伯爵夫妇，卷走他明知藏在伯爵书房里的巨款，然后搭乘末班火车潜回巴黎。”
“这些推测确实极其缜密入微，”普朗塔先生插话道；“但盖斯潘为什么没去巴蒂尼奥勒同他的同伴会合呢？因为只有那样，他才能在某种程度上为自己提供一份坚不可摧的不在场证明啊。”
让德龙医生一直坐在房间里唯一完好无损的一张椅子上。

===Original===

Gendron had been sitting on the only unbroken chair in the chamber, reflecting on Plantat’s sudden embarrassment, when he had spoken of Robelot the bonesetter. The remarks of the judge drew him from his revery; he got up, and said:
“There is another point; putting forward the time was perhaps useful to Guespin, but it would greatly damage Bertaud, his accomplice.”
“But,” answered M. Domini, “it might be that Bertaud was not consulted. As to Guespin, he had no doubt good reasons for not returning to the wedding. His restlessness, after such a deed, would possibly have betrayed him.”
M. Lecoq had not thought fit to speak as yet. Like a doctor at a sick bedside, he wanted to be sure of his diagnosis. He had returned to the mantel, and again pushed forward the hands of the clock. It sounded, successively, half-past eleven, then twelve, then half-past twelve, then one.
As he moved the hands, he kept muttering:
“Apprentices﻿—chance brigands! You are malicious, parbleu, but you don’t think of everything. You give a push to the hands, but don’t remember to put the striking in harmony with them. Then comes along a detective, an old rat who knows things, and the dodge is discovered.”
M. Domini and Plantat held their tongues. M. Lecoq walked up to them.
“Monsieur the Judge,” said he, “is perhaps now convinced that the deed was done at half-past ten.”
“Unless,” interrupted M. Plantat, “the machinery of the clock has been out of order.”
“That often happens,” added M. Courtois. “The clock in my drawing-room is in such a state that I never know the time of day.”
M. Lecoq reflected.
“It is possible,” said he, “that Monsieur Plantat is right. The probability is in favor of my theory; but probability, in such an affair, is not sufficient; we must have certainty. There happily remains a mode of testing the matter﻿—the bed; I’ll wager it is rumpled up.” Then addressing the mayor, “I shall need a servant to lend me a hand.”
“I’ll help you,” said Plantat, “that will be a quicker way.”
They lifted the top of the bed and set it on the floor, at the same time raising the curtains.
“Hum!” cried M. Lecoq, “was I right?”
“True,” said M. Domini, surprised, “the bed is rumpled.”
“Yes; and yet no one has lain in it.”
“But﻿—” objected M. Courtois.
“I am sure of what I say,” interrupted the detective.

===Chinese===

沉思着方才提到接骨郎中罗伯洛时普朗塔脸上掠过的那阵莫名慌乱。法官的高谈阔论将他从沉思中拉了回来；他站起身，说道：
“这里还有一个疑点；把时间往后拨对盖斯潘或许有利，但对他那个同谋贝尔托来说，却无疑是灭顶之灾。”
“然而，”多米尼先生反驳道，“很可能事先根本没同贝尔托商量过。至于盖斯潘，他不去参加婚宴想必有他的难言之隐。犯下这等弥天大罪之后，他内心的惊惶失措极有可能会在众目睽睽之下露出马脚。”
勒科克先生一直冷眼旁观，尚未开口表态。就像病榻前的大夫一样，他必须百分之百确诊自己的判断。他重新走回壁炉台前，再度拨动了座钟的指针。钟声依次敲响了11点半、12点、12点半、1点。
一边拨动指针，他一边在嘴里不停地咕哝着：
“新手——半路出家的山贼！算你们诡计多端，真见鬼，可惜你们百密一疏。你们随手把指针往前拨，却忘了让打鸣报时的发条齿轮同指针保持一致。等到老练的刑警——一只熟稔套路的老油条赶到现场，你们的小花招当场就现了原形。”
多米尼先生和普朗塔双双沉默不语。勒科克先生大步走到他们面前。
“法官大人，”他说，“现在或许可以确信，案发时间正是昨晚10点半了吧。”
“除非，”普朗塔先生打断道，“这座钟的机芯早在大案发生前就已经失灵坏掉了。”
“这种事常有，”库尔图瓦先生连忙附和道，“我客厅里的座钟毛病多得很，我从来就没从它那儿弄准过时辰。”
勒科克先生沉吟片刻。
“普朗塔先生的疑虑不无道理，”他说，“从概率上讲，事实支持在下的论断；但在这样的人命大案面前，单凭概率远远不够；我们必须掌握板上钉钉的铁证。好在眼下还保留着一个查验真相的绝佳试金石——那就是那张床；我敢打赌那张床是被人故意揉乱的。”说着，他转向镇长，“我需要一个下人来搭把手。”
“我来帮您，”普朗塔说，“这样动作更快些。”
他们合力掀起床罩放倒在地板上，同时撩起了床幔。
“哼！”勒科克先生喊道，“在下说得没错吧？”
“确实，”多米尼先生诧异地说，“床铺被弄得凌乱不堪。”
“没错；然而却根本没有人躺在上面睡过。”
“但是——”库尔图瓦先生试图提出异议。
“在下对自己的论断有十足把握，”刑警打断道。

===Original===

“The sheets, it is true, have been thrown back, perhaps someone has rolled about in the bed; the pillows have been tumbled, the quilts and curtains ruffled, but this bed has not the appearance of having been slept in. It is, perhaps, more difficult to rumple up a bed than to put it in order again. To make it up, the coverings must be taken off, and the mattresses turned. To disarrange it, one must actually lie down in it, and warm it with the body. A bed is one of those terrible witnesses which never misguide, and against which no counter testimony can be given. Nobody has gone to bed in this﻿—”
“The countess,” remarked Plantat, “was dressed; but the count might have gone to bed first.”
“No,” answered M. Lecoq, “I’ll prove to the contrary. The proof is easy, indeed, and a child of ten, having heard it, wouldn’t think of being deceived by this intentional disorder of the bedclothes.”
M. Lecoq’s auditors drew up to him. He put the coverings back upon the middle of the bed, and went on:
“Both of the pillows are much rumpled, are they not? But look under the bolster﻿—it is all smooth, and you find none of those wrinkles which are made by the weight of the head and the moving about of the arms. That’s not all; look at the bed from the middle to the foot. The sheets being laid carefully, the upper and under lie close together everywhere. Slip your hand underneath﻿—there﻿—you see there is a resistance to your hand which would not occur if the legs had been stretched in that place. Now Monsieur de Trémorel was tall enough to extend the full length of the bed.”
This demonstration was so clear, its proof so palpable, that it could not be gainsaid.
“This is nothing,” continued M. Lecoq. “Let us examine the second mattress. When a person purposely disarranges a bed, he does not think of the second mattress.”
He lifted up the upper mattress, and observed that the covering of the under one was perfectly even.
“H’m, the second mattress,” muttered M. Lecoq, as if some memory crossed his mind.
“It appears to be proved,” observed the judge, “that Monsieur de Trémorel had not gone to bed.”
“Besides,” added the doctor, “if he had been murdered in his bed, his clothes would be lying here somewhere.”
“Without considering,” suggested M. Lecoq, “that some blood must have been found on the sheets. Decidedly, these criminals were not shrewd.”
“What seems to me surprising,” M.

===Chinese===

“被单确实被掀开了，或许还有人故意在床上打了几个滚；枕头被压得皱皱巴巴，被褥和床帘也被扯得乱七八糟，但这铺床绝没有真正睡过人的痕迹。要把一张床故意揉乱，或许比把它收拾整齐还要困难得多。要把床铺好，必须揭开被褥翻转床垫。而要想让床铺呈现出真正睡过的模样，非得有人实实在在地合衣躺在上面，用体温把床焐热不可。床铺是世间那些从不撒谎、且绝无任何反证能够推翻的铁证之一。昨夜根本没有人在这张床上就寝——”
“伯爵夫人，”普朗塔指出，“遇害时衣着整齐；但伯爵或许先一步上床睡下了呢。”
“绝无可能，”勒科克先生回答道，“在下能证明绝非如此。证明起来其实易如反掌，一个10岁的孩童听完这番解释，也绝不会被这套故意揉乱的被褥障眼法所蒙骗。”
旁听的众人纷纷围拢到大侦探身边。他把被褥重新铺回到床铺正中央，接着说道：
“两只枕头都被压得皱皱巴巴，不是吗？但诸位瞧瞧长枕套底下——那里平整如初，根本找不到任何因头颅的重量与手臂的挪动而形成的褶皱痕迹。这还不是全部；再瞧瞧从床铺中央一直延伸到床尾的这部分。被单被平整地铺开，上层与下层在每一处都紧紧贴合在一起。把手伸进去试试——瞧——诸位能感受到一股阻力，如果双腿曾在那片地方伸展挪动过，绝不可能留有这样的阻力。而特雷莫雷尔先生身形魁梧，身高足足能从床头一直延伸到床尾。”
这番剖析是如此清晰确凿，其论据如此无可辩驳，任谁也找不出半点反驳的余地。
“这还不算什么，”勒科克先生接着说道，“咱们再来查验第二层床垫。一个人故意伪造床铺凌乱时，绝想不到去动第二层床垫的手脚。”
他一把掀开上层厚床垫，只见下层床垫的垫套平整得犹如镜面一般。
“嗯，第二层床垫，”勒科克先生喃喃自语，脑海中似乎掠过了一丝遥远的回忆。
“看来足以证实，”法官评价道，“特雷莫雷尔先生昨夜确实未曾就寝。”
“此外，”医生补充道，“如果他真是在床上遇害的，他的衣服理该丢在屋里的某个角落。”
“更不必说，”勒科克先生提醒道，“被单上必定会沾染大滩血迹。显而易见，这伙罪犯的手段并不见得有多么高明。”
“让我大感惊奇的是，”普朗塔先生对法官说。

===Original===

Plantat observed to the judge, “is that anybody would succeed in killing, except in his sleep, a young man so vigorous as Count Hector.”
“And in a house full of weapons,” added Dr. Gendron; “for the count’s cabinet is full of guns, swords and hunting knives; it’s a perfect arsenal.”
“Alas!” sighed M. Courtois, “we know of worse catastrophes. There is not a week that the papers don’t﻿—”
He stopped, chagrined, for nobody was listening to him. Plantat claimed the general attention, and continued:
“The confusion in the house seems to you surprising; well now, I’m surprised that it is not worse than it is. I am, so to speak, an old man; I haven’t the energy of a young man of thirty-five; yet it seems to me that if assassins should get into my house, when I was there, and up, it would go hard with them. I don’t know what I would do; probably I should be killed; but surely I would give the alarm. I would defend myself, and cry out, and open the windows, and set the house afire.”
“Let us add,” insisted the doctor, “that it is not easy to surprise a man who is awake. There is always an unexpected noise which puts one on his guard. Perhaps it is a creaking door, or a cracking stair. However cautious the murderer, he does not surprise his victim.”
“They may have used firearms;” struck in the worthy mayor, “that has been done. You are quietly sitting in your chamber; it is summer, and your windows are open; you are chatting with your wife, and sipping a cup of tea; outside, the assassins are supplied with a short ladder; one ascends to a level with the window, sights you at his ease, presses the trigger, the bullet speeds﻿—”
“And,” continued the doctor, “the whole neighborhood, aroused by it, hastens to the spot.”
“Permit me, pardon, permit me,” said M. Courtois, testily, “that would be so in a populous town. Here, in the midst of a vast park, no. Think, doctor, of the isolation of this house. The nearest neighbor is a long way off, and between there are many large trees, intercepting the sound. Let us test it by experience. I will fire a pistol in this room, and I’ll wager that you will not hear the echo in the road.”
“In the daytime, perhaps, but not in the night.”
“Well,” said M. Domini, who had been reflecting while M.

===Chinese===

“凶手究竟是如何得手的？除非是在睡梦中暴起突袭，否则怎能轻易手刃像埃克托伯爵这样年轻力壮、身手敏捷的男子呢。”
“况且这宅邸里到处都是兵刃，”让德龙医生补充道，“伯爵的书房里摆满了猎枪、利剑和猎刀；简直就是一座名副其实的小型军械库。”
“唉！”库尔图瓦先生长叹了一口气，“世间比这更惨烈的惨祸我们见得还少吗？报纸上哪周不报道几起——”
他讪讪地打住了话头，满腹委屈，因为根本没人理会他的插话。普朗塔重新吸引了所有人的注意力，接着说道：
“屋里的凌乱在诸位看来或许触目惊心；而在我看来，没被破坏得更惨烈反倒让我大感诧异。平心而论，我也算是一把老骨头了；自然没有35岁年轻人的充沛精力；然而依我看，要是有凶手闯进我的宅邸，趁我尚未就寝、清醒坐着的时候行凶，那他们可休想全身而退。我不知道自己会使出什么手段；很可能我最终也会丢了性命；但我必定会弄出惊天动地的动静来报警。我会殊死抵抗，放声呼救，砸开窗户，哪怕一把火把房子点着也在所不惜。”
“我们还必须补充一点，”医生强调道，“要想神不知鬼不觉偷袭一个毫无睡意的人，绝非易事。总会有意想不到的微小动静让人警觉起来。或许是一声门轴的吱呀，或者是一级台阶的轻微爆响。凶手动作再谨小慎微，也休想轻易打受害者一个措手不及。”
“他们很可能是动用了火器，”可敬的镇长插嘴道，“以往就有过先例。你安闲地坐在卧室里；正值盛夏，窗户大敞着；你一边同妻子闲聊，一边抿着茶水；在屋外，凶手扛来了一架短梯；其中一人顺着梯子爬到同窗户齐平的高度，从容不迫地瞄准，扣动扳机，子弹呼啸而出——”
“然后呢，”医生反驳道，“巨响立刻惊动四邻八乡，所有人都会闻声火速赶来。”
“请原谅，容我说一句，容我说一句，”库尔图瓦先生急躁地辩解道，“在人烟稠密的闹市确实会如此。但在这片广袤无垠的庄园深处，可绝非如此。医生，您想想这栋宅邸有多么与世隔绝吧。最近的邻居隔着大老远，其间还耸立着无数参天大树，早把声响阻隔得严严实实。咱们不妨当场做个试验。我在这间屋里放上一枪手枪，我敢打赌在村道上你们休想听见半点回音。”
“白天或许听不见，到了万籁俱寂的深夜可未必。”
“好吧，”多米尼先生沉思了良久，开口道。

===Original===

Courtois was talking, “if against all hope, Guespin does not decide to speak tonight, or tomorrow, the count’s body will afford us a key to the mystery.”
During this discussion, M. Lecoq had continued his investigations, lifting the furniture, studying the fractures, examining the smallest pieces, as if they might betray the truth. Now and then, he took out an instrument-case, from which he produced a shank, which he introduced and turned in the locks. He found several keys on the carpet, and on a rack, a towel, which he carefully put one side, as if he deemed it important. He came and went from the bedroom to the count’s cabinet, without losing a word that was said; noting in his memory, not so much the phrases uttered, as the diverse accents and intonations with which they were spoken. In an inquest such as that of the crime of Orcival, when several officials find themselves face to face, they hold a certain reserve toward each other. They know each other to have nearly equal experience, to be shrewd, clearheaded, equally interested in discovering the truth, not disposed to confide in appearances, difficult to surprise. Each one, likely enough, gives a different interpretation to the facts revealed; each may have a different theory of the deed; but a superficial observer would not note these differences. Each, while dissimulating his real thoughts, tries to penetrate those of his neighbor, and if they are opposed to his own, to convert him to his opinion. The great importance of a single word justifies this caution. Men who hold the liberty and lives of others in their hands, a scratch of whose pen condemns to death, are apt to feel heavily the burden of their responsibility. It is an ineffable solace, to feel that this burden is shared by others. This is, why no one dares take the initiative, or express himself openly; but each awaits other opinions, to adopt or oppose them. They exchange fewer affirmations than suggestions. They proceed by insinuation; then they utter commonplaces, ridiculous suppositions, asides, provocative, as it were, of other explanations.
In this instance, the judge of instruction and Plantat were far from being of the same opinion; they knew it before speaking a word. But M. Domini, whose opinion rested on material and palpable facts, which appeared to him indisputable, was not disposed to provoke contradiction.

===Chinese===

“如果打破一切侥幸，盖斯潘今晚或明早依然负隅顽抗拒不开口，伯爵的遗体终究会为我们揭开整桩迷局的谜底。”
在这番激烈的争论中，勒科克先生片刻未歇地继续着他的侦查，搬动家具，推敲裂痕，检验着最细碎的蛛丝马迹，仿佛它们随时能吐露真相一般。不时地，他摸出一个工具盒，从中取出一根探针，插进锁孔里转动摸索。他在地毯上摸出了好几把钥匙，并在一个挂架上发现了一条毛巾，他小心翼翼地把毛巾收拢到一旁，仿佛认定那是一件极为关键的物证。他在卧室与伯爵书房之间穿梭往返，在座众人的每一字每一句他都一字不落听在耳里；留在脑海深处的，与其说是大家说出的字面语句，不如说是字里行间流露出的各种微妙口吻与顿挫抑扬。在像奥尔西瓦尔大案这样的重大刑事侦查中，当数位官府中人狭路相逢时，彼此之间往往保持着某种极有分寸的戒备。他们深知彼此拥有旗鼓相当的阅历，精明强干，头脑清醒，同样急于勘破真相，同样不肯轻信表面文章，同样极难被轻易蒙蔽。对于眼前浮现出的种种事实，每个人很可能都怀有一套截然不同的阐释；每个人心头都自有一套关于案情始末的假说；然而肤浅的旁观者却绝难察觉出这些暗流涌动的分歧。每个人在极力掩饰自己真实意图的同时，又在千方百计探寻同僚的内心隐秘，一旦发觉对方的看法与自己背道而驰，便暗自设法将对方拉拢到自己的阵营中来。只言片语所关涉的重大干系，完全足以证明这种谨小慎微的正当性。手握他人身家性命与自由的大权者，笔尖轻轻一划便能判人死罪，往往深切感受到肩头重压的分量。发觉这份沉重的担子能有人共同分担，是一种难以言喻的心灵慰藉。这正是为何任谁也不敢贸然出头挑大梁，或开门见山表态的原因；每个人都在静候他人的高见，以便采纳或是反驳。他们极少直截了当地做出断言，而是惯用推敲暗示。他们旁敲侧击，随口抛出陈词滥调、荒诞不经的猜想或是私下耳语，借以激引出更深层的解释。
在眼下的关头，预审法官与普朗塔两人的意见可以说是南辕北辙；甚至在开口之前他们就对此心知肚明。然而多米尼先生的定见建立在他自认无可辩驳的真凭实据之上，因此并不急于引发正面冲突。

===Original===

Plantat, on the contrary, whose system seemed to rest on *impressions*, on a series of logical deductions, would not clearly express himself, without a positive and pressing invitation. His last speech, impressively uttered, had not been replied to; he judged that he had advanced far enough to sound the detective.
“Well, Monsieur Lecoq,” asked he, “have you found any new traces?”
M. Lecoq was at that moment curiously examining a large portrait of the Count Hector, which hung opposite the bed. Hearing M. Plantat’s question, he turned.
“I have found nothing decisive,” answered he, “and I have found nothing to refute my conjectures. But﻿—”
He did not finish; perhaps he too, recoiled before his share of the responsibility.
“What?” insisted M. Domini, sternly.
“I was going to say,” resumed M. Lecoq, “that I am not yet satisfied. I have my lantern and a candle in it; I only need a match﻿—”
“Please preserve your decorum,” interrupted the judge severely.
“Very well, then,” continued M. Lecoq, in a tone too humble to be serious, “I still hesitate. If the doctor, now, would kindly proceed to examine the countess’s body, he would do me a great service.”
“I was just going to ask the same favor, Doctor,” said M. Domini.
The doctor answering, “Willingly,” directed his steps toward the door.
M. Lecoq caught him by the arm.
“If you please,” said he, in a tone totally unlike that he had used up to this time, “I would like to call your attention to the wounds on the head, made by a blunt instrument, which I suppose to be a hammer. I have studied these wounds, and though I am no doctor, they seem to me suspicious.”
“And to me,” M. Plantat quickly added. “It seemed to me, that in the places struck, there was no emission of blood in the cutaneous vessels.”
“The nature of these wounds,” continued M. Lecoq, “will be a valuable indication, which will fix my opinion.” And, as he felt keenly the brusque manner of the judge, he added:
“It is you, Doctor, who hold the match.”
M. Gendron was about to leave the room, when Baptiste, the mayor’s servant﻿—the man who wouldn’t be scolded﻿—appeared. He bowed and said:
“I have come for Monsieur the Mayor.”
“For me? why?” asked M. Courtois. “What’s the matter? They don’t give me a minute’s rest! Answer that I am busy.”
“It’s on account of madame,” resumed the placid Baptiste; “she isn’t at all well.” The excellent mayor grew slightly pale.

===Chinese===

反观普朗塔，他的整套推理完全建立在直觉感受、建立在一连串环环相扣的逻辑演绎之上，除非得到对方明确而急切的探询，否则绝不肯轻易全盘托出。他方才那番慷慨陈词并未得到法官的接茬应和；他审时度势，认为火候已足，便决定试探试探这位大侦探的底细。
“那么，勒科克先生，”他探询道，“您可有什么新发现吗？”
勒科克先生此刻正饶有兴致地审视着悬挂在床铺正对面的一幅埃克托伯爵的大幅肖像画。听到普朗塔先生的发问，他转过身来。
“在下并未发现任何决定性的铁证，”他回答道，“但也没找到任何足以推翻我心头推论的反证。不过——”
他欲言又止，戛然而止；或许面对自己所需承担的重大责任，他也有些心生退缩。
“不过什么？”多米尼先生严厉地追问道。
“在下正想说，”勒科克先生接着说道，“在下心里尚未踏实。我的灯笼有了，里面的蜡烛也备齐了；眼下就差一根擦燃蜡烛的火柴了——”
“请注意你的言辞体统！”法官声色俱厉地打断道。
“好吧，既然如此，”勒科克先生换上一副谦卑得有些过分的语调接着说，“在下依然举棋不定。若是医生眼下能行个方便，去对伯爵夫人的遗体展开细致解剖验尸，那可真是帮了在下一个天大的忙。”
“我也正想劳烦医生做这桩差事呢，医生，”多米尼先生附和道。
医生应了声“乐意效劳”，便迈步朝门外走去。
勒科克先生一把拉住了他的胳膊。
“如果可以的话，”他说，口吻完全脱去了方才那种嬉皮笑脸的油滑，变得凝重无比，“在下想请您格外留意死者头部的几处伤口，那些伤口显然是由钝器造成的，我推测是铁锤。我曾仔细端详过那些创口，虽说我不是大夫，但在我看来那些伤痕可疑得很。”
“我也深有同感，”普朗塔先生迅速抢白道，“在我看来，在遭受重击的创口周围，皮下血管似乎根本没有出现明显的渗血凝结。”
“这些创口的性质，”勒科克先生接着说，“将是一条极其宝贵的重要指引，足以彻底敲定在下的判断。”随后，由于深感法官方才生硬斥责的难堪，他意有所指地补充了一句：
“全看您老手中握着的那根火柴了，医生。”
让德龙医生正要举步出门，镇长家的男仆巴蒂斯特——那个自诩谁也不敢训斥的刁奴——赫然出现在门口。他躬身施礼道：
“小的奉命来请镇长大人。”
“找我？为了何事？”库尔图瓦先生诧异地问，“出了什么岔子？真是连一分钟的清静都不肯给我！就说我公务缠身。”
“是关于太太的急事，”神气活现的巴蒂斯特接茬道，“太太身子骨突然极不妥当。”这位可敬的镇长脸色顿时泛起了一层惨白。

===Original===

“My wife!” cried he, alarmed. “What do you mean? Explain yourself.”
“The postman arrived just now,” returned Baptiste with a most tranquil air, “and I carried the letters to madame, who was in the drawing-room. Hardly had I turned on my heels when I heard a shriek, and the noise of someone falling to the floor.” Baptiste spoke slowly, taking artful pains to prolong his master’s anguish.
“Speak! go on!” cried the mayor, exasperated. “Speak, won’t you?”
“I naturally opened the drawing-room door again. What did I see? madame, at full length on the floor. I called for help; the chambermaid, cook, and others came hastening up, and we carried madame to her bed. Justine said that it was a letter from Mademoiselle Laurence which overcame my mistress﻿—”
At each word Baptiste hesitated, reflected; his eyes, giving the lie to his solemn face, betrayed the great satisfaction he felt in relating his master’s misfortunes.
His master was full of consternation. As it is with all of us, when we know not exactly what ill is about to befall us, he dared not ask any questions. He stood still, crushed; lamenting, instead of hastening home. M. Plantat profited by the pause to question the servant, with a look which Baptiste dared not disobey.
“What, a letter from Mademoiselle Laurence? Isn’t she here, then?”
“No, sir: she went away a week ago, to pass a month with one of her aunts.”
“And how is madame?”
“Better, sir; only she cries piteously.”
The unfortunate mayor had now somewhat recovered his presence of mind. He seized Baptiste by the arm.
“Come along,” cried he, “come along!”
They hastened off.
“Poor man!” said the judge of instruction. “Perhaps his daughter is dead.”
M. Plantat shook his head.
“If it were only that!” muttered he. He added, turning to M. Domini:
“Do you recall the allusions of Bertaud, monsieur?”

===Chinese===

“我夫人！”他心惊肉跳地失声喊道，“你这话什么意思？快给我把话说清楚！”
“刚才邮差登门了，”巴蒂斯特神色自若、慢条斯理地回答道，“小人便把信函呈送给正在客厅里的太太。小人前脚刚转过身，后脚就听见屋里传来一声凄厉的惨叫，紧接着是重物摔倒在地的闷响。”巴蒂斯特故意拖长声调，处心积虑地借以拉长折磨主人的焦虑。
“快说！接着往下说！”镇长急得暴跳如雷，大吼道，“你哑巴了吗，还不快招！”
“小人自然立刻推开了客厅大门。诸位猜怎么着？太太整个人直挺挺仰面栽倒在地板上。小人连忙大声呼救；女仆、厨娘一帮人慌忙赶来，大家七手八脚把太太抬回了床上。朱斯蒂娜（Justine）说，是一封洛朗斯小姐（Mademoiselle Laurence）的来信把太太吓瘫过去的——”
巴蒂斯特字斟句酌，每吐出一个字都要停顿思量一番；他那双骨碌乱转的眼珠子同庄重刻板的面孔截然相反，出卖了他绘声绘色讲述东家大祸时内心那股难以名状的幸灾乐祸。
他的东家早已吓得魂不附体。正像我们常人一样，当预感到某种可怕的不祥之兆即将当头砸下时，他甚至连刨根问底的勇气都丧失了。他整个人瘫软在原地，除了捶胸顿足哀号之外，甚至忘了拔腿赶回家中。普朗塔先生趁着这个间歇，投向男仆一记不容抗拒的威严目光，厉声盘问道：
“什么，洛朗斯小姐的来信？难道她眼下不在府里吗？”
“不在，先生：她一周前就出门了，去她的一位姑妈家住上一个月。”
“太太眼下情形如何？”
“好些了，先生；只是哭得死去活来，凄惨得很。”
大难临头的不幸镇长总算勉强找回了一丝神智。他一把死死拽住巴蒂斯特的胳膊。
“快走，”他失魂落魄地喊道，“快跟我走！”
主仆二人匆匆离去。
“可怜的人啊！”预审法官感叹道，“兴许是他女儿暴病亡故了吧。”
普朗塔先生沉重地摇了摇头。
“要是仅止于此倒还好了！”他喃喃自语道。接着他转过身，目光如炬地望向多米尼先生：
“法官先生，您可还记得方才贝尔托那些言不由衷的弦外之音吗？”

## VII / 第七章
===Original===

The judge of instruction, the doctor, and M. Plantat exchanged a significant look. What misfortune had befallen M. Courtois, this worthy, and despite his faults, excellent person? Decidedly, this was an ill-omened day!
“If we are to speak of Bertaud’s allusions,” said M. Lecoq, “I have heard two very curious stories, though I have been here but a few hours. It seems that this Mademoiselle Laurence﻿—”
M. Plantat abruptly interrupted the detective.
“Calumnies! odious calumnies! The lower classes, to annoy the rich, do not hesitate to say all sorts of things against them. Don’t you know it? Is it not always so? The gentry, above all, those of a provincial town, live in glass houses. The lynx eyes of envy watch them steadily night and day, spy on them, surprise what they regard as their most secret actions to arm themselves against them. The bourgeois goes on, proud and content; his business prospers; he possesses the esteem and friendship of his own class; all this while, he is vilified by the lower classes, his name dragged in the dust, soiled by suppositions the most mischievous. Envy, Monsieur, respects nothing, no one.”
“If Laurence has been slandered,” observed Dr. Gendron, smiling, “she has a good advocate to defend her.”
The old justice of the peace (the man of bronze, as M. Courtois called him) blushed slightly, a little embarrassed.
“There are causes,” said he, quietly, “which defend themselves. Mademoiselle Courtois is one of those young girls who has a right to all respect. But there are evils which no laws can cure, and which revolt me. Think of it, monsieurs, our reputations, the honor of our wives and daughters, are at the mercy of the first petty rascal who has imagination enough to invent a slander. It is not believed, perhaps; but it is repeated, and spreads. What can be done? How can we know what is secretly said against us; will we ever know it?”
“Eh!” replied the doctor, “what matters it? There is only one voice, to my mind, worth listening to﻿—that of conscience. As to what is called ‘public opinion,’ as it is the aggregate opinion of thousands of fools and rogues, I only despise it.”
This discussion might have been prolonged, if the judge of instruction had not pulled out his watch, and made an impatient gesture.
“While we are talking, time is flying,” said he. “We must hasten to the work that still remains.”

===Chinese===

预审法官、医生与普朗塔先生交换了一个意味深长的眼神。到底有什么天大的灾祸降临到了可敬的库尔图瓦先生头上呢？尽管他毛病不少，骨子里却是个极好的人。毫无疑问，今天真是个大凶之日！
“如果说到贝尔托话里有话的影射，”勒科克先生说，“尽管在下才赶到这儿几个钟头，倒也耳闻了两个颇为稀奇的故事。据说那位洛朗斯小姐——”
普朗塔先生粗暴地打断了大侦探的话头。
“全是无耻的诽谤！令人作呕的造谣中伤！底层下流之辈为了恶心富人，向来毫无顾忌地信口雌黄四处罗织罪名。难道你还不清楚这一点吗？世道何尝不是向来如此？富人阶层，尤其是外省小镇上的体面人家，简直就活在玻璃房子里。嫉妒的鹰隼之眼日日夜夜死死盯视着他们，暗中窥伺，窥探他们自以为最隐秘的私事，以此作为伤害攻讦他们的致命武器。布尔乔亚安步当车，骄傲自足；生意蒸蒸日上；在同阶层圈子里享有崇高的声望与友谊；然而就在此时此刻，下层无赖却在背后肆意诋毁他，把他尊贵的姓名践踏在污泥之中，用最下流恶毒的凭空臆测肆意玷污。先生，嫉妒这东西从不知敬畏为何物，更不知放过任何人。”
“如果洛朗斯真蒙受了不白之冤，”让德龙医生微笑着评价道，“她倒是有了一位极善辩护的好律师呢。”
老治安法官（库尔图瓦先生口中的铁石心肠之人）双颊微微泛红，显得有些局促不安。
“有些公道正义，”他平静地答道，“是不言自明的。库尔图瓦小姐正是那种理应受到世人全部敬重的纯洁闺秀。然而世间偏偏存在着许多法律鞭长莫及的恶疾，令我深恶痛绝。诸位先生，想想看吧，我们男人的清白名声，我们妻女的贞洁节操，竟然全凭第一个想得出下流谣言的小无赖随口捏造摆布。旁人起初或许将信将疑；但谣言一经传播，便如瘟疫般蔓延四散。我们能怎么办？我们怎能探知旁人在暗地里如何咬舌根诋毁我们；我们难道真能防不胜防吗？”
“嗨！”医生反驳道，“那又有什么要紧？在我看来，世间唯有一种声音值得侧耳倾听——那就是良知的呼唤。至于人们常说的所谓‘社会舆论’，不过是成千上万蠢货与恶棍愚昧偏见的合集，我唯有报以彻头彻尾的鄙视。”
这场争辩很可能会无休止地进行下去，如果预审法官没有不耐烦地掏出怀表、做出一个烦躁的手势的话。
“咱们光顾着拌嘴，工夫全耽搁了，”法官说，“剩下的勘验差事我们必须抓紧办妥。”

===Original===

It was then agreed that while the doctor proceeded to his autopsy, the judge should draw up his report of the case. M. Plantat was charged with watching Lecoq’s investigations.
As soon as the detective found himself alone with M. Plantat:
“Well,” he said, drawing a long breath, as if relieved of a heavy burden, “now we can get on.”
Plantat smiled; the detective munched a lozenge, and added:
“It was very annoying to find the investigation already going on when I reached here. Those who were here before me have had time to get up a theory, and if I don’t adopt it at once, there is the deuce to pay!”
M. Domini’s voice was heard in the entry, calling out to his clerk.
“Now there’s the judge of instruction,” continued Lecoq, “who thinks this a very simple affair; while I, Lecoq, the equal at least of Gévrol, the favorite pupil of Papa Tabaret﻿—I do not see it at all clearly yet.”
He stopped; and after apparently going over in his mind the result of his discoveries, went on: “No; I’m off the track, and have almost lost my way. I see something underneath all this﻿—but what? what?”
M. Plantat’s face remained placid, but his eyes shone.
“Perhaps you are right,” said he, carelessly; “perhaps there is something underneath.” The detective looked at him; he didn’t stir. His face seemed the most undisturbed in the world. There was a long silence, by which M. Lecoq profited to confide to the portrait of the defunct the reflections which burdened his brain.
“See here, my dear darling,” said he, “this worthy person seems a shrewd old customer, and I must watch his actions and gestures carefully. He does not argue with the judge; he’s got an idea that he doesn’t dare to tell, and we must find it out. At the very first he guessed me out, despite these pretty blond locks. As long as he thought he could, by misleading me, make me follow M. Domini’s tack, he followed and aided me showing me the way. Now that he sees me on the scent, he crosses his arms and retires. He wants to leave me the honor of the discovery. Why? He lives here﻿—perhaps he is afraid of making enemies. No. He isn’t a man to fear much of anything. What then? He shrinks from his own thoughts. He has found something so amazing, that he dares not explain himself.”
A sudden reflection changed the course of M. Lecoq’s confidences.
“A thousand imps!” thought he. “Suppose I’m wrong!

===Chinese===

于是大家当场商定，趁着医生进行尸检的当口，法官负责草拟案件的预审笔录。普朗塔先生则奉命全程监督勒科克的大案侦查。
大侦探一发觉身边只剩下普朗塔先生一人：
“好啦，”他长长舒了一口气，宛如卸下了千斤重担，“眼下咱们总算能放开手脚干了。”
普朗塔微微一笑；刑警嚼了一颗含片，接着说道：
“一赶到现场就发觉侦查早已拉开帷幕，真叫人说不出的别扭。比我早到的人早已先入为主炮制出了一套推论假说，要是我不立刻全盘照搬，简直就成了十恶不赦的弥天大罪！”
门厅里传来了多米尼先生呼唤书记官的声音。
“诸位瞧瞧那位预审法官大人，”勒科克接着说，“他认定这是一桩再一目了然不过的简单案子；而我，勒科克，至少在本事上同热夫罗尔（Gévrol）旗鼓相当、身为塔巴雷老爹最得意的关门弟子——我眼下却还完全看不清庐山真面目呢。”
他顿了顿；在脑海中飞快复盘梳理着方才的一系列发现，随后接着说道：“不；我的思路完全偏离了正轨，几乎快要迷失方向了。我总觉得这一切底下包藏着别的勾当——究竟是什么？到底是什么？”
普朗塔先生面色波澜不惊，双眼却炯炯发亮。
“或许您是对的，”他若无其事地接话道，“或许这底下确实大有文章。”刑警审视地盯着他；老人却纹丝未动。他的神色显得是天底下最无动于衷的模样。四周陷入了一阵漫长的沉默，勒科克先生借机向盒盖上先室的画像吐露起充斥脑海的千头万绪。
“你瞧瞧，亲爱的宝贝，”他暗自念叨着，“这位可敬的先生显然是个极其深沉老练的老狐狸，我必须死死盯紧他的一举一动。他从不当面反驳法官；他心头早有一套不敢公之于众的推论，我们必须把它掏出来。打一开始他就看穿了我的真面目，哪怕我顶着这头漂亮的浅色假发。只要他觉得自己通过误导能让我顺着多米尼先生的思路查下去，他就紧紧跟随在一旁，替我引路帮腔。如今发觉我摸到了真凶的嗅觉，他反倒抱起双臂抽身退步了。他这是想把破案的头功拱手让给我呢。为什么？他是本地常住官员——也许是怕树敌招怨？不对。他绝不是那种前怕狼后怕虎的懦夫。那又是为了什么？他这是被自己内心深处的惊人想法吓住了。他发现的隐秘是如此骇人听闻，以至于连他自己都不敢宣之于口。”
然而一个突如其来的念头骤然打乱了勒科克先生的心思。
“见鬼！”他暗忖，“万一我全料错了呢！

===Original===

Suppose this old fellow is not shrewd at all! Suppose he hasn’t discovered anything, and only obeys the inspirations of chance! I’ve seen stranger things. I’ve known so many of these folks whose eyes seem so very mysterious, and announce such wonders; after all, I found nothing, and was cheated. But I intend to sound this old fellow well.”
And, assuming his most idiotic manner, he said aloud:
“On reflection, Monsieur, little remains to be done. Two of the principals are in custody, and when they make up their minds to talk﻿—they’ll do it, sooner or later, if the judge is determined they shall﻿—we shall know all.”
A bucket of ice-water falling on M. Plantat’s head could not have surprised him more, or more disagreeably, than this speech.
“What!” stammered he, with an air of frank amazement, “do you, a man of experience, who﻿—”
Delighted with the success of his ruse, Lecoq could not keep his countenance, and Plantat, who perceived that he had been caught in the snare, laughed heartily. Not a word, however, was exchanged between these two men, both subtle in the science of life, and equally cunning in its mysteries. They quite understood each other.
“My worthy old buck,” said the detective to himself, “you’ve got something in your sack; only it’s so big, so monstrous, that you won’t exhibit it, not for a cannonball. You wish your hand forced, do you? Very well!”
“He’s sly,” thought M. Plantat. “He knows that I’ve got an idea; he’s trying to get at it﻿—and I believe he will.”
M. Lecoq had restored his lozenge-box to his pocket, as he always did when he went seriously to work. His amour-propre was enlisted; he played a part﻿—and he was a rare comedian.
“Now,” cried he, “let’s to horse. According to the mayor’s account, the instrument with which all these things were broken has been found.”
“In the room in the second story,” answered M. Plantat, “overlooking the garden, we found a hatchet on the floor, near a piece of furniture which had been assailed, but not broken open; I forbade anyone to touch it.”
“And you did well. Is it a heavy hatchet?”
“It weighs about two pounds.”
“Good. Let’s see it.”
They ascended to the room in question, and M. Lecoq, forgetting his part of a haberdasher, and regardless of his clothes, went down flat on his stomach, alternately scrutinizing the hatchet﻿—which was a heavy, terrible weapon﻿—and the slippery and well-waxed oaken floor.

===Chinese===

万一这老头根本就不是什么精明之辈呢！万一他根本就没发现半点门道，纯粹是在瞎猫碰死耗子撞大运呢！比这更荒唐离奇的事我都见识过。我见过太多双眼里写满了高深莫测、看似要吐露惊天奇迹的人物；结果查到底，什么名堂都没有，白白被他们戏耍了一场。不过我今天非得好好称量称量这个老家伙的斤两不可。”
于是，他故意换上一副最呆若木鸡的愚蠢神情，大声说道：
“平心而论，法官先生，剩下的差事其实没多少了。两名主犯已经被缉拿归案，只要他们横下心来招供——早晚的事嘛，只要法官大人铁腕审讯——咱们就能把案情查个底朝天。”
一桶冰水兜头浇在普朗塔先生脑袋上，恐怕也不会比这番话带给他更大的震惊与失望了。
“什么！”他结结巴巴地说，脸上流露出真真切切的惊愕，“难道连您这样一位经验老到、明察秋毫的刑侦高手，竟然也——”
眼见自己的试探诡计得逞，勒科克再也绷不住那副假正经的嘴脸，而普朗塔发觉自己中了圈套，不由得开怀大笑起来。然而在这两位深谙人情世故、在世态奥秘中旗鼓相当的高手之间，无需多吐半句废话。他们彼此心领神会。
“我可敬的老伙计，”大侦探暗自思忖，“你的锦囊里绝对揣着猛料；只因为那料实在太大、太耸人听闻了，哪怕拿大炮轰你，你也不肯轻易露底。你非得让人逼着你摊牌不可，是吧？那好办！”
“这小子滑头得很，”普朗塔先生暗想，“他知道我心头有了眉目；正千方百计想套我的话呢——而且我相信他早晚套得出来。”
勒科克先生重新把含片盒塞回了大衣口袋，每当他动真格办案时向来如此。他的职业自尊心彻底被激发了；他是在演戏——而他无疑是一位罕见的戏骨。
“好啦，”他喊道，“咱们立刻动真格的。照镇长的笔录，打碎所有陈设的凶器已经找到了。”
“在三楼朝向花园的那间屋里，”普朗塔先生回答道，“我们在地板上发现了一把短斧，就扔在一件遭受过劈砍但未被破开的家具旁；我严令任何人不得触碰。”
“处置得极其妥当。那是一把沉重的斧头吗？”
“重约两磅。”
“很好。咱们上去瞧瞧。”
他们登楼来到了那间屋子，勒科克先生把零售小布商的伪装行头彻底抛到了九霄云外，顾不上身上的衣服，整个人平趴在地板上，轮流仔细端详着那把沉重凶险的短斧，以及打蜡打得锃亮光滑的厚实橡木地板。

===Original===

“I suppose,” observed M. Plantat, “that the assassins brought this hatchet up here and assailed this cupboard, for the sole purpose of putting us off our scent, and to complicate the mystery. This weapon, you see, was by no means necessary for breaking open the cupboard, which I could smash with my fist. They gave one blow﻿—only one﻿—and quietly put the hatchet down.”
The detective got up and brushed himself.
“I think you are mistaken,” said he. “This hatchet wasn’t put on the floor gently; it was thrown with a violence betraying either great terror or great anger. Look here; do you see these three marks, near each other, on the floor? When the assassin threw the hatchet, it first fell on the edge﻿—hence this sharp cut; then it fell over on one side; and the flat, or hammer end left this mark here, under my finger. Therefore, it was thrown with such violence that it turned over itself and that its edge a second time cut in the floor, where you see it now.”
“True,” answered M. Plantat. The detective’s conjectures doubtless refuted his own theory, for he added, with a perplexed air:
“I don’t understand anything about it.”
M. Lecoq went on:
“Were the windows open this morning as they are now?”
“Yes.”
“Ah! The wretches heard some noise or other in the garden, and they went and looked out. What did they see? I can’t tell. But I do know that what they saw terrified them, that they threw down the hatchet furiously, and made off. Look at the position of these cuts﻿—they are slanting of course﻿—and you will see that the hatchet was thrown by a man who was standing, not by the cupboard, but close by the open window.”
Plantat in his turn knelt down, and looked long and carefully. The detective was right. He got up confused, and after meditating a moment, said:
“This perplexes me a little; however﻿—”
He stopped, motionless, in a revery, with one of his hands on his forehead.
“All might yet be explained,” he muttered, mentally searching for a solution of the mystery, “and in that case the time indicated by the clock would be true.”
M. Lecoq did not think of questioning his companion. He knew that he would not answer, for pride’s sake.
“This matter of the hatchet puzzles me, too,” said he. “I thought that these assassins had worked leisurely; but that can’t be so. I see they were surprised and interrupted.”
Plantat was all ears.
“True,” pursued M.

===Chinese===

“在我看来，”普朗塔先生推敲道，“凶手把这把斧头带上楼来劈砍这个柜子，纯粹是为了转移我们的视线，把案情搅得更加扑朔迷离。您瞧，对于这只我赤手空拳一拳就能砸烂的薄板柜子来说，根本无需动用这般沉重的凶器。他们只砍了一下——仅仅砍了一记——便从容不迫地把斧头放在了地板上。”
刑警从地上爬了起来，拍了拍身上的灰尘。
“依在下之见，您恐怕料错了，”他说，“这把斧头绝不是被从容轻轻搁在地板上的；它是被带着极度狂暴的力道狠狠摔在地上的，要么出于极度的惊恐，要么出于极度的狂怒。您瞧：看到地板上紧挨在一起的这三处痕迹了吗？凶手狠狠掷出斧头时，斧头首先是刃口着地——因此留下了这道狭长的深切口；随后它向一侧翻滚倒下；斧头平整的锤击端便在我的手指底下砸出了这记凹痕。显而易见，投掷的力道是如此狂暴凶猛，以至于它在反弹翻滚后，刃口又在地上切出了第二道深口，正是诸位眼下瞧见它的姿态。”
“确实如此，”普朗塔先生应承道。大侦探的推论显然推翻了他自己先前的设想，于是他带着大惑不解的神情补充道：
“我对此完全摸不着头脑了。”
勒科克先生接着说道：
“今早案发时，窗户就像现在这般大敞着吗？”
“是的。”
“啊！那伙恶徒必定是在花园里听到了某种异样的动静，于是他们快步冲到窗前探头张望。他们看到了什么？在下尚不敢妄断。但我敢断定的是，他们所目睹的景象让他们吓破了胆，以至于惊慌失措地暴怒掷下斧头，拔腿逃之夭夭。您仔细瞧瞧这些切口的角度——自然是倾斜的——您就能看明白，掷出斧头的人当时绝不是站在柜子旁，而是紧挨着大敞的窗户站立的。”
轮到普朗塔跪倒在地，巨细靡遗地端详了良久。大侦探说得丝毫不差。他神情凝重地站起身来，沉思片刻后说道：
“这倒让我有些费解了；然而——”
他呆立在原地，陷入了深沉的遐想，一只手抚着额头。
“一切或许依然解释得通，”他喃喃自语，在脑海中苦苦搜寻着解开谜团的钥匙，“而且若是如此，那座钟所指示的钟点或许才是真正的大案发生时刻。”
勒科克先生没打算当场逼问他的同伴。他心知肚明，出于骄傲，对方绝不会轻易吐露心声。
“这把斧头的蹊跷之处同样让在下困惑不已，”刑警说，“我原本认定这伙恶徒是好整以暇、慢条斯理作案的；但眼下看来绝非如此。我发觉他们显然是遭遇了突如其来的惊扰与打断。”
普朗塔屏息凝神侧耳倾听。
“确实如此，”勒科克先生语调缓慢地接着说道。

===Original===

Lecoq, slowly, “we ought to divide these indications into two classes. There are the traces left on purpose to mislead us﻿—the jumbled-up bed, for instance; then there are the real traces, undesigned, as are these hatchet cuts. But here I hesitate. Is the trace of the hatchet true or false, good or bad? I thought myself sure of the character of these assassins: but now﻿—” He paused; the wrinkles on his face, the contraction of his mouth, betrayed his mental effort.
“But now?” asked M. Plantat.
M. Lecoq, at this question, seemed like a man just roused from sleep.
“I beg your pardon,” said he. “I forgot myself. I’ve a bad habit of reflecting aloud. That’s why I almost always insist on working alone. My uncertainty, hesitation, the vacillation of my suspicions, lose me the credit of being an astute detective﻿—of being an agent for whom there’s no such thing as a mystery.”
Worthy M. Plantat gave the detective an indulgent smile.
“I don’t usually open my mouth,” pursued M. Lecoq, “until my mind is satisfied; then I speak in a peremptory tone, and say﻿—this is thus, or this is so. But today I am acting without too much restraint, in the company of a man who knows that a problem such as this seems to me to be, is not solved at the first attempt. So I permit my gropings to be seen without shame. You cannot always reach the truth at a bound, but by a series of diverse calculations, by deductions and inductions. Well, just now my logic is at fault.”
“How so?”
“Oh, it’s very simple. I thought I understood the rascals, and knew them by heart; and yet I have only recognized imaginary adversaries. Are they fools, or are they mighty sly? That’s what I ask myself. The tricks played with the bed and clock had, I supposed, given me the measure and extent of their intelligence and invention. Making deductions from the known to the unknown, I arrived, by a series of very simple consequences, at the point of foreseeing all that they could have imagined, to throw us off the scent. My point of departure admitted, I had only, in order to reach the truth, to take the contrary of that which appearances indicated. I said to myself:
“A hatchet has been found in the second story; therefore the assassins carried it there, and designedly forgot it.
“They left five glasses on the dining-room table; therefore they were more or less than five, but they were not five.

===Chinese===

“我们理应把现场发现的所有线索划分为截然不同的两大类。一类是凶手处心积虑故意留下以误导我们视线的假象——比方说那张被故意揉乱的床铺；另一类则是仓皇之下无意中遗留下的真正痕迹，正如这几处斧刃切痕。然而在这里在下却不得不犹豫不决了。斧头的痕迹究竟是真实的还是伪造的，是破案的指引还是迷魂阵？我原本自以为彻底吃透了这伙凶犯的心性；可眼下——”他顿了顿；额头的皱纹与紧抿的嘴角，无不昭示着他内心剧烈的推敲权衡。
“可眼下又如何呢？”普朗塔先生追问道。
面对这个发问，勒科克先生宛如刚从梦魇中惊醒一般。
“请原谅在下的失态，”他说，“我走神了。我有个毛病，总爱把心里琢磨的事大声嘀咕出来。这正是在下为何办案向来坚持独来独往的缘故。我的游移不定、举棋不定与怀疑反复，难免会砸了我身为精明干练大侦探的金字招牌——一个世间绝无任何谜案能难倒的顶尖密探。”
可敬的普朗塔先生向大侦探投去一记宽容温和的微笑。
“在下平日里心头没有十足把握之前，”勒科克先生继续说道，“向来绝不轻易启齿；唯有大局已定时，才会用一锤定音的断然口吻宣布——事实如此这般，真相正是这样。然而今天面对一位深谙人情世故、深知像眼前这样重大的难题绝非一蹴而就能够破解的高人，在下反倒毫无拘泥保留了。因此我毫不避讳地暴露出自己的摸索彷徨。通往真相的道路绝无法一步登天，唯有一步一个脚印通过繁复的运算推演、归纳与演绎方能抵达。然而就在眼下，我的逻辑链条却卡壳断裂了。”
“此话怎讲？”
“哎呀，其实再简单不过了。我原本以为自己彻底看穿了这伙无赖，把他们摸得烂熟于心；然而到头来我才发觉自己交锋的不过是脑海中臆想出来的假想敌。他们究竟是一群蠢猪，还是精明到了极点的绝顶高手？这正是眼下盘旋在在下心头的拷问。床铺和座钟上玩弄的把戏，我原本以为已经丈量出了他们的智慧斤两与诡计限度。从已知推导未知，通过一系列水到渠成的逻辑后果，我原本已能未卜先知般预判出他们为了混淆视听所能炮制出的一切伎俩。只要确立了这一逻辑起点，为了抵近真相，我只需将眼前所有表象反其道而行之即可。我曾对自己暗自说道：
“既然在三楼地板上发现了一把短斧；那么必定是凶手故意把它带上楼去，并居心叵测地故意遗留在现场。
“既然他们在餐厅餐桌上留下了5只酒杯；那么凶手人数必定多于或少于五人，但绝非恰好五人。

===Original===

“There were the remains of a supper on the table; therefore they neither drank nor ate.
“The countess’s body was on the riverbank; therefore it was placed there deliberately. A piece of cloth was found in the victim’s hand; therefore it was put there by the murderers themselves.
“Madame de Trémorel’s body is disfigured by many dagger-strokes, and horribly mutilated; therefore she was killed by a single blow﻿—”
“Bravo, yes, bravo,” cried M. Plantat, visibly charmed.
“Eh! no, not bravo yet,” returned M. Lecoq. “For here my thread is broken; I have reached a gap. If my deductions were sound, this hatchet would have been very carefully placed on the floor.”
“Once more, bravo,” added the other, “for this does not at all affect our general theory. It is clear, nay certain, that the assassins intended to act as you say. An unlooked-for event interrupted them.”
“Perhaps; perhaps that’s true. But I see something else﻿—”
“What?”
“Nothing﻿—at least, for the moment. Before all, I must see the dining-room and the garden.”
They descended at once, and Plantat pointed out the glasses and bottles, which he had put one side. The detective took the glasses, one after another, held them level with his eye, toward the light, and scrutinized the moist places left on them.
“No one has drank from these glasses,” said he, firmly.
“What, from neither one of them?”
The detective fixed a penetrating look upon his companion, and in a measured tone, said:
“From neither one.”
M. Plantat only answered by a movement of the lips, as if to say, “You are going too far.”
The other smiled, opened the door, and called:
“François!”
The valet hastened to obey the call. His face was suffused with tears; he actually bewailed the loss of his master.
“Hear what I’ve got to say, my lad,” said M. Lecoq, with true detective-like familiarity. “And be sure and answer me exactly, frankly, and briefly.”
“I will, sir.”
“Was it customary here at the château, to bring up the wine before it was wanted?”
“No, sir; before each meal, I myself went down to the cellar for it.”
“Then no full bottles were ever kept in the dining-room?”
“Never.”
“But some of the wine might sometimes remain in draught?”
“No; the count permitted me to carry the dessert wine to the servants’ table.”
“And where were the empty bottles put?”

===Chinese===

“餐桌上赫然留有残羹剩饭；那么他们必定既未动过酒，也未吃过肉。
“伯爵夫人的遗体横陈在河岸边；那么必定是凶手深思熟虑故意弃尸于彼。死者掌心攥着一截布条；那么必定是凶手亲手塞入死者指间的。
“特雷莫雷尔夫人的身躯布满了乱刀刺痕，被残害得惨不忍睹；那么她必定是被一刀毙命的——”
“精彩！没错，太精彩了！”普朗塔先生失声叫好，显然被这套环环相扣的反向推理深深折服。
“嗨！先别急着喝彩，”勒科克先生苦笑道，“因为就在这里，我的线头硬生生崩断了；我遭遇了一道无法逾越的断崖。要是在下的演绎推论当真天衣无缝，这把短斧原本理该被凶手极其从容平稳地摆放在地板上的。”
“我还是要为您喝彩，”普朗塔补充道，“因为这丝毫不能推翻我们的大方向假设。凶手最初企图按照您方才所说的那样伪造现场，这是明摆着、甚至确凿无疑的。只不过突如其来的意外变故打乱了他们的全盘节奏。”
“或许吧；或许确实如此。然而在下却看出了别的蹊跷——”
“什么蹊跷？”
“没什么——至少眼下还不能妄下断语。在此之前，我必须亲眼勘验餐厅和花园。”
二人立刻举步下楼，普朗塔指着被他单独收拢在一旁的酒杯和酒瓶。大侦探拿起一只只酒杯，平举到同视线齐平的高度，迎着光线，巨细靡遗地审视着杯壁上残留的湿润痕迹。
“这些杯子，根本没有一个人真正凑近唇边喝过一口，”他斩钉截铁地断言道。
“什么，连一只都没喝过？”
刑警将锐利的目光射向身旁的同伴，字斟句酌地宣布：
“连一只都没有。”
普朗塔先生只是撇了撇嘴角，仿佛在说：“您未免有些言过其实了吧。”
大侦探会心一笑，拉开门，高声唤道：
“弗朗索瓦！”
男仆慌忙应召赶来。他脸上泪痕未干；显然是真心实意为痛失东家而悲恸不已。
“听好了，小伙子，”勒科克先生带着刑警特有的那种居高临下的亲昵口吻说道，“务必如实、准确、言简意赅地回答我的盘问。”
“小的必定知无不言，先生。”
“城堡里平日有用膳前提前把酒端上来的规矩吗？”
“回先生，没有；每次开饭前，都是小的亲自下地窖去取酒。”
“这么说，餐厅里平日绝不存放开过封或未开封的整瓶酒咯？”
“绝没有过。”
“但喝剩的残酒总会留在屋里吧？”
“不会；伯爵恩准小的把餐后的甜酒端去下人桌上分着喝。”
“那么空酒瓶都收在何处？”

===Original===

“I put them in this corner cupboard, and when they amounted to a certain number, I carried them down cellar.”
“When did you last do so?”
“Oh”﻿—François reflected﻿—“at least five or six days ago.”
“Good. Now, what liqueurs did the count drink?”
“The count scarcely ever drank liqueurs. If, by chance, he took a notion to have a small glass of eau-de-vie, he got it from the liqueur closet, there, over the stove.”
“There were no decanters of rum or cognac in any of the cupboards?”
“No.”
“Thanks; you may retire.”
As François was going out, M. Lecoq called him back.
“While we are about it, look in the bottom of the closet, and see if you find the right number of empty bottles.”
The valet obeyed, and looked into the closet.
“There isn’t one there.”
“Just so,” returned M. Lecoq. “This time, show us your heels for good.”
As soon as François had shut the door, M. Lecoq turned to Plantat and asked:
“What do you think now?”
“You were perfectly right.”
The detective then smelt successively each glass and bottle.
“Good again! Another proof in aid of my guess.”
“What more?”
“It was not wine that was at the bottom of these glasses. Among all the empty bottles put away in the bottom of that closet, there was one﻿—here it is﻿—which contained *vinegar*; and it was from this bottle that they turned what they thought to be wine into the glasses.”
Seizing a glass, he put it to M. Plantat’s nose, adding:
“See for yourself.”
There was no disputing it; the vinegar was good, its odor of the strongest; the villains, in their haste, had left behind them an incontestable proof of their intention to mislead the officers of justice. While they were capable of shrewd inventions, they did not have the art to perform them well. All their oversights could, however, be accounted for by their sudden haste, caused by the occurrence of an unlooked-for incident. “The floors of a house where a crime has just been committed,” said a famous detective, “burn the feet.” M. Lecoq seemed exasperated, like a true artist, before the gross, pretentious, and ridiculous work of some green and bungling scholar.
“These are a parcel of vulgar ruffians, truly! able ones, certainly; but they don’t know their trade yet, the wretches.”
M. Lecoq, indignant, ate three or four lozenges at a mouthful.
“Come, now,” said Plantat, in a paternally severe tone. “Don’t let’s get angry.

===Chinese===

“小的全随手码在墙角这个橱柜底下，等攒到一定数目，再一并送回地窖。”
“你最后一次送空瓶是什么时候？”
“噢”——弗朗索瓦挠头思忖着——“起码是5、6天前的事了。”
“很好。那么，伯爵平日都喝什么烈酒？”
“伯爵平生几乎滴酒不沾烈酒。倘若偶尔心血来潮想抿上一小盅生命之水白兰地，他都是从壁炉上方那个专用酒柜里取。”
“别的橱柜里绝没有存放过朗姆酒或干邑白兰地的分酒器吗？”
“绝没有。”
“多谢；你可以退下了。”
就在弗朗索瓦正要跨出门槛的当口，勒科克先生又把他叫了回来。
“既然话说到这儿了，你探头瞧瞧橱柜最底下，看看那些空酒瓶数目对不对。”
男仆依言照办，探身往橱柜底层打量了一番。
“回先生，里头一个空瓶都没有了。”
“果然不出所料，”勒科克先生接话道，“这回你可以彻底走人了。”
弗朗索瓦刚一把门带上，勒科克先生便转过身来望向普朗塔：
“您老眼下作何感想？”
“您料得丝毫不差。”
刑警随即凑上前去，逐一嗅闻着每一只酒杯与酒瓶。
“妙极了！又添了一项佐证我推论的铁证。”
“又发现了什么？”
“留在这些杯底的，根本就不是葡萄酒。在当初堆放在橱柜底部的所有空瓶里，有这么一只——就是这只——里面原本装的是醋；而恶徒正是从这个瓶子里，把他们自以为是葡萄酒的液体倒进了这几只酒杯里。”
说着，他一把抓起一只酒杯递到普朗塔先生鼻尖下，补充道：
“您亲自闻闻看。”
事实无可辩驳；醋的成色极其纯正，刺鼻的酸气扑面而来；恶徒在惊慌失措之中，留下了无可抵赖的确凿铁证，坐实了他们伪造现场企图误导司法办案的罪恶企图。尽管这帮人肚子里不乏精巧绝伦的构想，动起手来却缺乏缜密执行的手段功夫。然而他们所有的百密一疏，完全可以用突发意外迫使他们仓皇逃窜来合理解释。“刚刚犯下弥天大罪的凶案现场地板，”一位声名显赫的传奇刑警曾有名言，“是烫脚的。”勒科克先生显得怒不可遏，宛如一位真正的艺术巨匠在目睹了某个乳臭未干的蹩脚学徒粗制滥造、哗众取宠的拙劣画作时那般痛心疾首。
“真是一帮粗鄙下流的亡命之徒！手段固然狠毒；可惜这帮恶棍还没真正学出师呢。”
义愤填膺的勒科克先生一口气吞下了三四颗含片。
“好啦，好啦，”普朗塔用慈父般沉稳严厉的口吻规劝道，“用不着动肝火。

===Original===

The people have failed in address, no doubt; but reflect that they could not, in their calculations, take account of the craft of a man like you.”
M. Lecoq, who had the vanity which all actors possess, was flattered by the compliment, and but poorly dissimulated an expression of pleasure.
“We must be indulgent; come now,” pursued Plantat. “Besides,” he paused a moment to give more weight to what he was going to say, “besides, you haven’t seen everything yet.”
No one could tell when M. Lecoq was playing a comedy. He did not always know, himself. This great artist, devoted to his art, practised the feigning of all the emotions of the human soul, just as he accustomed himself to wearing all sorts of costumes. He was very indignant against the assassins, and gesticulated about in great excitement; but he never ceased to watch Plantat slyly, and the last words of the latter made him prick up his ears.
“Let’s see the rest, then,” said he.
As he followed his worthy comrade to the garden, he renewed his confidences to the dear defunct.
“Confound this old bundle of mystery! We can’t take this obstinate fellow by surprise, that’s clear. He’ll give us the word of the riddle when we have guessed it; not before. He is as strong as we, my darling; he only needs a little practice. But look you﻿—if he has found something which has escaped us, he must have previous information, that we don’t know of.”
Nothing had been disturbed in the garden.
“See here, Monsieur Lecoq,” said the old justice of the peace, as he followed a winding pathway which led to the river. “It was here that one of the count’s slippers was found; below there, a little to the right of these geraniums, his silk handkerchief was picked up.”
They reached the riverbank, and lifted, with great care, the planks which had been placed there to preserve the footprints.
“We suppose,” said M. Plantat, “that the countess, in her flight, succeeded in getting to this spot; and that here they caught up with her and gave her a finishing blow.”
Was this really Plantat’s opinion, or did he only report the morning’s theory? M. Lecoq could not tell.
“According to my calculations,” he said, “the countess could not have fled, but was brought here already dead, or logic is not logic. However, let us examine this spot carefully.”
He knelt down and studied the sand on the path, the stagnant water, and the reeds and water-plants.

===Chinese===

作案的人手段固然露出了破绽，但您想想，凭他们的算计，怎么可能把您这样一位明察秋毫的克星算计进去呢。”
勒科克先生骨子里有着所有戏骨都具备的虚荣心，被这句奉承搔到了痒处，脸上掩饰不住得意之色。
“咱们得宽宏大度些；走吧，”普朗塔继续说道。接着他顿了顿，好让自己即将吐露的话语显得更具分量，“更何况，您还没把现场看全呢。”
世间没人能看穿勒科克先生何时是在逢场作戏。有时甚至连他自己也分不清楚。这位全身心献祭给侦探艺术的大师，早已练就了随意模拟人类所有七情六欲的绝技，正如他习惯于驾驭形形色色的伪装行头一样。他表面上对凶犯咬牙切齿、手舞足蹈大发雷霆；暗地里却片刻未歇地冷眼打量着普朗塔的一举一动，老人的最后那句话让他立刻竖起了耳朵。
“那咱们就去瞧瞧剩下的排场吧，”他说。
跟随着这位可敬的同伴走向花园时，他重新向亲爱的先室念叨起来：
“真见鬼，这个浑身写满谜团的老古董！咱们休想轻易打他个措手不及，这是明摆着的。除非咱们自己猜中谜底，否则他绝不会主动把底牌亮出来。这老头本事绝不下于咱们，我的宝贝；他无非是差了点办案实操经验。然而你瞧好了——如果他当真发现了连咱们都漏掉的破绽，那他手里必定掌握着咱们一无所知的内情线索。”
花园里一切尚保持着原样。
“瞧瞧这儿，勒科克先生，”老治安法官一边顺着通往塞纳河的蜿蜒小径前行，一边指点道，“伯爵的一只拖鞋就是在这儿被捡到的；再往前走，就在那些天竺葵右侧不远的地方，人们拾获了他的丝绸手帕。”
他们来到了河岸边，极其小心地掀起了事先平铺在泥滩上用来保护脚印的两块木板。
“我们推测，”普朗塔先生说，“伯爵夫人一路狂奔奔逃至此；凶手在这里追上了她，并给了她致命的一击。”
这究竟是普朗塔内心的真实推断，还是他不过在转述今早众人的定论？勒科克先生一时间也难以捉摸。
“按照在下的精密推演，”他说，“伯爵夫人绝不可能一路奔逃至此，她是早在气绝身亡之后才被抬到这里的，否则逻辑就成了一纸空文。不管怎样，咱们且把这片地方巨细靡遗勘验一番。”
他单膝跪地，仔细审视着小径上的泥沙、死水微澜的河滩，以及四周的芦苇水草。

===Original===

Then going along a little distance, he threw a stone, approaching again to see the effect produced on the mud. He next returned to the house, and came back again under the willows, crossing the lawn, where were still clearly visible traces of a heavy burden having been dragged over it. Without the least respect for his pantaloons, he crossed the lawn on all-fours, scrutinizing the smallest blades of grass, pulling away the thick tufts to see the earth better, and minutely observing the direction of the broken stems. This done, he said:
“My conclusions are confirmed. The countess was carried across here.”
“Are you sure of it?” asked Plantat.
There was no mistaking the old man’s hesitation this time; he was clearly undecided, and leaned on the other’s judgment for guidance.
“There can be no error, possibly.”
The detective smiled, as he added:
“Only, as two heads are better than one, I will ask you to listen to me, and then, you will tell me what you think.”
M. Lecoq had, in searching about, picked up a little flexible stick, and while he talked, he used it to point out this and that object, like the lecturer at the panorama.
“No,” said he, “Madame de Trémorel did not fly from her murderers. Had she been struck down here, she would have fallen violently; her weight, therefore, would have made the water spirt to some distance, as well as the mud; and we should certainly have found some splashes.”
“But don’t you think that, since morning, the sun﻿—”
“The sun would have absorbed the water; but the stain of dry mud would have remained. I have found nothing of the sort anywhere. You might object, that the water and mud would have spirted right and left; but just look at the tufts of these flags, lilies, and stems of cane﻿—you find a light dust on every one. Do you find the least trace of a drop of water? No. There was then no splash, therefore no violent fall; therefore the countess was not killed here; therefore her body was brought here, and carefully deposited where you found it.”
M. Plantat did not seem to be quite convinced yet.
“But there are the traces of a struggle in the sand,” said he.
His companion made a gesture of protest.
“Monsieur deigns to have his joke; those marks would not deceive a schoolboy.”
“It appears to me, however﻿—”
“There can be no mistake, Monsieur Plantat. Certain it is that the sand has been disturbed and thrown about.

===Chinese===

随后他挪出一段距离，随手掷出一枚石子，重新凑上前去查看石子落入泥滩激起的飞溅痕迹。接着他折返回宅邸前，穿过草坪重新走回柳树下，草地上重物拖拽碾压的痕迹依然清晰可辨。他丝毫不顾惜自己的裤子，四肢着地在草坪上爬行，检视着最细小的草叶，扒开茂密的草丛仔细端详泥土，甚至入微入细地观察着折断草茎的倒伏朝向。勘验完毕，他宣布：
“在下的结论得到了彻底证实。伯爵夫人是被人从草坪上拖运过来的。”
“您当真有十足把握？”普朗塔问。
这回老人的游移不定毫无伪装的痕迹；他显然陷入了迟疑不决，迫切希望依仗对方的裁决作为指引。
“绝无可能出现任何差池。”
大侦探会心一笑，补充道：
“不过，常言道三个臭皮匠顶个诸葛亮，在下不妨将推论原原本本讲给您听，然后请您老指教斧正。”
勒科克先生在搜寻时捡起了一根柔韧的小树枝，说话间，他挥动着树枝指点着这件或那件物件，宛如全景画展览馆里的讲解员。
“不，”他说，“特雷莫雷尔夫人绝没有在凶手追击下狂奔。倘若她真是在这里被击倒在地的，她必定会重重摔落；因此，她的身躯重量必定会将河水和泥浆溅射出老远；而在现场我们必定能找到大片飞溅的泥点。”
“但您难道不觉得，从清晨到现在，日头的曝晒——”
“烈日固然会蒸干水分；但干涸的泥浆印迹必定会完好无损地保留下来。而在下在四周根本找不到半点泥斑痕迹。您或许会反驳说，泥水很可能朝左右两侧飞溅了出去；但您瞧瞧这几丛菖蒲、睡莲和芦苇茎秆——每一片叶片上都覆盖着一层薄薄的浮尘。您在上头找得到哪怕一滴水珠溅落的痕迹吗？半点皆无。既然没有水花飞溅，自然就没有激烈的摔倒；因此伯爵夫人绝非在此遇害；因此她的遗体是被人抬运至此，并极其从容细致地安放在诸位今早发现她的位置上的。”
普朗塔先生似乎尚未被完全说服。
“然而沙滩上打斗挣扎的痕迹又该如何解释呢？”他追问道。
他的同伴做出了一个断然抗议的手势。
“先生可真是拿在下开玩笑了；那些痕迹连个蒙童都哄骗不过去。”
“但在我看来——”
“绝不可能看走眼，普朗塔先生。沙滩确实被翻动搅乱过。

===Original===

But all these trails that lay bare the earth which was covered by the sand, were made by the same foot. Perhaps you don’t believe it. They were made, too, with the end of the foot; that you may see for yourself.”
“Yes, I perceive it.”
“Very well, then; when there has been a struggle on ground like this, there are always two distinct kinds of traces﻿—those of the assailant and those of the victim. The assailant, throwing himself forward, necessarily supports himself on his toes, and imprints the fore part of his feet on the earth. The victim, on the contrary, falling back, and trying to avoid the assault, props himself on his heels, and therefore buries the heels in the soil. If the adversaries are equally strong, the number of imprints of the toes and the heels will be nearly equal, according to the chances of the struggle. But what do we find here?”
M. Plantat interrupted:
“Enough; the most incredulous would now be convinced.” After thinking a moment, he added:
“No, there is no longer any possible doubt of it.”
M. Lecoq thought that his argument deserved a reward, and treated himself to two lozenges at a mouthful.
“I haven’t done yet,” he resumed. “Granted, that the countess could not have been murdered here; let’s add that she was not carried hither, but dragged along. There are only two ways of dragging a body; by the shoulders, and in this case the feet, scraping along the earth, leave two parallel trails; or by the legs﻿—in which case the head, lying on the earth, leaves a single furrow, and that a wide one.”
Plantat nodded assent.
“When I examined the lawn,” pursued M. Lecoq, “I found the parallel trails of the feet, but yet the grass was crushed over a rather wide space. How was that? Because it was the body, not of a man, but of a woman, which was dragged across the lawn﻿—of a woman full-dressed, with heavy petticoats; that, in short, of the countess, and not of the count.”
M. Lecoq paused, in expectation of a question, or a remark.
But the old justice of the peace did not seem to be listening, and appeared to be plunged in the deepest meditation. Night was falling; a light fog hung like smoke over the Seine.
“We must go in,” said M. Plantat, abruptly, “and see how the doctor has got on with his autopsy.”
They slowly approached the house. The judge of instruction awaited them on the steps. He appeared to have a satisfied air.

===Chinese===

然而所有这些被沙子覆盖而显露出来的深浅印迹，全是由同一只脚踩踏出来的。您或许不信。而且全是用脚尖捣腾出来的；这一点您亲自瞧瞧便一目了然。”
“是的，我确实看出来了。”
“那么很好；在这样的沙滩泥地上若是当真发生过殊死肉搏，现场必定会留下两类截然不同的足迹——攻击者的足迹与受害者的足迹。攻击者前扑发力，重心必然落在脚趾上，因而在地面上踩出深深的前掌印。反之，受害者仓皇踉跄后退，试图躲避扑击，重心必然落在脚跟上，因而在泥土中深陷后脚跟。若是双方势均力敌，根据搏斗的胜负胶着，脚尖与脚跟的印迹数量理该大致相当。然而我们在现场瞧见了什么？”
普朗塔先生打断了他：
“够了；哪怕疑心最重的人此刻也该心服口服了。”沉思片刻后，他补充道：
“是的，对此再无任何怀疑的余地了。”
勒科克先生认为自己的雄辩理应得到犒赏，于是往嘴里塞了两颗含片。
“在下的话还没说完呢，”他接着说，“既然断定伯爵夫人绝不可能在此遇害；咱们还得补充一句，她不是被抬过来的，而是被拖拽过来的。要把一具尸体拖行在地上，唯独有两种法子：要么抓住肩膀拖，若是如此，双脚在泥地上划过，会留下两道平行的拖痕；要么抓住双腿拖——若是如此，头颅紧贴地面，会在泥地上留下一道宽阔的单一凹槽沟痕。”
普朗塔点头赞同。
“当在下勘验草坪时，”勒科克先生接着说道，“我发现的是双脚拖曳留下的两道平行痕迹，然而草地被碾压抹平的面积却格外宽阔。这是为何？因为被拖过草坪的，绝非男人的躯体，而是一具女尸——一个穿戴整齐、下身裹着厚重沉实衬裙的女人；简而言之，那是伯爵夫人的遗体，而绝非伯爵的尸体。”
勒科克先生停住了话头，静候着对方的提问或评述。
然而老治安法官似乎根本没有在听，整个人深陷在极其深沉的冥想沉思之中。夜幕正悄然降临；一抹轻纱般的薄雾如轻烟般笼罩在塞纳河水面上。
“我们必须回屋了，”普朗塔先生猛然开口道，“去瞧瞧医生的验尸进展如何。”
二人缓缓踱回宅邸前。预审法官正伫立在台阶上迎候着他们。他的脸上洋溢着大功告成的自得神色。

===Original===

“I am going to leave you in charge,” said he to M. Plantat, “for if I am to see the procureur, I must go at once. When you sent for him this morning, he was absent.”
M. Plantat bowed.
“I shall be much obliged if you will watch this affair to the end. The doctor will have finished in a few minutes, he says, and will report tomorrow morning. I count on your cooperation to put seals wherever they are necessary, and to select the guard over the château. I shall send an architect to draw up an exact plan of the house and garden. Well, sir,” asked M. Domini, turning to the detective, “have you made any fresh discoveries?”
“I have found some important facts; but I cannot speak decisively till I have seen everything by daylight. If you will permit me, I will postpone making my report till tomorrow afternoon. I think I may say, however, that complicated as this affair is﻿—”
M. Domini did not let him finish.
“I see nothing complicated in the affair at all; everything strikes me as very simple.”
“But,” objected M. Lecoq, “I thought﻿—”
“I sincerely regret,” continued the judge, “that you were so hastily called, when there was really no serious reason for it. The evidences against the arrested men are very conclusive.”
Plantat and Lecoq exchanged a long look, betraying their great surprise.
“What!” exclaimed the former, “have, you discovered any new indications?”
“More than indications, I believe,” responded M. Domini. “Old Bertaud, whom I have again questioned, begins to be uneasy. He has quite lost his arrogant manner. I succeeded in making him contradict himself several times, and he finished by confessing that he saw the assassins.”
“The assassins!” exclaimed M. Plantat. “Did he say assassins?”
“He saw at least one of them. He persists in declaring that he did not recognize him. That’s where we are. But prison walls have salutary terrors. Tomorrow after a sleepless night, the fellow will be more explicit, if I mistake not.”
“But Guespin,” anxiously asked the old man, “have you questioned him?”
“Oh, as for him, everything is clear.”
“Has he confessed?” asked M. Lecoq, stupefied.
The judge half turned toward the detective, as if he were displeased that M. Lecoq should dare to question him.
“Guespin has *not* confessed,” he answered, “but his case is none the better for that. Our searchers have returned.

===Chinese===

“这里的大局我就全权托付给您了，”法官对普朗塔先生说，“因为如果我要去面见检察官，必须立刻动身赶回科尔贝伊。今早您派人去请他时，他恰巧外出不在。”
普朗塔先生躬身应诺。
“如果您能替我将这桩案子监督到底，本官将不胜感激。医生说他再过几分钟便能结束验尸，明早递交正式验尸报告。我指望您从旁协助，在所有必要的门户上贴上封条，并为城堡选派守卫人员。明早我会派遣一名建筑测绘师前来绘制整座宅邸与花园的精密格局图。那么，这位先生，”多米尼先生转向大侦探问道，“你可有什么崭新的收获？”
“在下确实摸清了几项极为关键的重要事实；但在大白天亲眼复核一切之前，在下尚不敢妄下断语。如果您恩准，在下请求将正式调查报告推迟到明午呈递。不过我想我可以断言的是，尽管案情错综复杂——”
多米尼先生粗暴地打断了他。
“本官压根看不出这案子有何错综复杂之处；依本官看来，一切都再简单不过了。”
“但是，”勒科克先生抗辩道，“在下以为——”
“本官由衷感到遗憾，”法官接着训斥道，“当初在毫无正当严肃理由的情况下，官府竟然这般火急火燎把你从巴黎调遣了过来。压在两名被捕案犯身上的罪证早已铁证如山、无可辩驳。”
普朗塔与勒科克对视了良久，彼此眼中写满了极度的错愕。
“怎么！”老人失声问道，“难道您又掌握了什么新的线索？”
“我相信远不止是线索，”多米尼先生自信满满地回答道，“老贝尔托，经本官再度提审，已经开始阵脚大乱了。他早已彻底收敛了先前那副张狂跋扈的嚣张气焰。本官成功迫使他当场自相矛盾了好几次，最终他不得不招供，承认自己昨夜亲眼目睹了凶手。”
“凶手！”普朗塔先生惊呼道，“他亲口说了‘凶手’？”
“他至少看见了其中的一人。他咬死声称自己没能辨认出那人的模样。眼下口供止步于此。然而大牢的高墙自有一种令人洗心革面的威慑力。明早经过一夜未眠的煎熬，要是本官没料错的话，这个老骨头必定会交代得更彻底。”
“那么盖斯潘呢，”老人焦虑不安地追问道，“您也重新提审他了吗？”
“噢，至于他，案情早已水落石出。”
“他招供了？”勒科克先生惊愕得目瞪口呆。
法官半转过身子，目光冷冷地瞥向刑警，显然对勒科克胆敢盘问自己大感不悦。
“盖斯潘并未招供，”他回答道，“但这丝毫改变不了他的灭顶之灾。我们派去搜寻的队伍刚刚折返回来。

===Original===

They haven’t yet found the count’s body, and I think it has been carried down by the current. But they found at the end of the park, the count’s other slipper, among the roses; and under the bridge, in the middle of the river, they discovered a thick vest which still bears the marks of blood.”
“And that vest is Guespin’s?”
“Exactly so. It was recognized by all the domestics, and Guespin himself did not hesitate to admit that it belonged to him. But that is not all﻿—”
M. Domini stopped as if to take breath, but really to keep Plantat in suspense. As they differed in their theories, he thought Plantat betrayed a stupid opposition to him; and he was not sorry to have a chance for a little triumph.
“That is not all,” he went on; “this vest had, in the right pocket, a large rent, and a piece of it had been torn off. Do you know what became of that piece of Guespin’s vest?”
“Ah,” muttered M. Plantat, “it was that which we found in the countess’s hand.”
“You are right, Monsieur. And what think you of this proof, pray, of the prisoner’s guilt?”
M. Plantat seemed amazed; his arms fell at his side. As for M. Lecoq, who, in presence of the judge, had resumed his haberdasher manner, he was so much surprised that he nearly strangled himself with a lozenge.
“A thousand devils!” exclaimed he. “That’s tough, that is!” He smiled sillily, and added in a low tone, meant only for Plantat’s ear.
“Mighty tough! Though quite foreseen in our calculations. The countess held a piece of cloth tightly in her hand; therefore it was put there, *intentionally*, by the murderers.”
M. Domini did not hear this remark. He shook hands with M. Plantat and made an appointment to meet him on the morrow, at the courthouse. Then he went away with his clerk.
Guespin and old Bertaud, handcuffed, had a few minutes before being led off to the prison of Corbeil, under the guard of the Orcival gendarmes.

===Chinese===

他们至今尚未寻获伯爵的尸体，本官推测多半已被湍急的河水冲向下游了。然而在庄园公园尽头的玫瑰丛中，他们找到了伯爵的另一只拖鞋；而且在塞纳河中央的铁索桥下，他们从水底打捞出了一件至今依然残留着斑斑血迹的厚背心。”
“那件背心是盖斯潘的？”
“一点不错。城堡里的所有仆役一眼就认了出来，而盖斯潘本人也毫不犹豫承认那是他的物件。然而这还不是全部——”
多米尼先生顿了顿，仿佛是在喘口气，实则是为了吊普朗塔的胃口。由于彼此在推论上南辕北辙，他认定普朗塔是在愚蠢地同自己唱反调；能有这么一个耀武扬威的小胜良机，他自然十分受用。
“这还不是全部，”法官接着宣布，“这件背心的右侧口袋上，有一道巨大的撕裂破口，上面硬生生被扯下了一整块布料。诸位可知从盖斯潘背心上扯下的那块碎布如今何在吗？”
“啊，”普朗塔先生失声喃喃道，“就是今早我们在伯爵夫人手中攥着的那块布条。”
“您料得丝毫不差，先生。面对压在囚犯头上的这等如山铁证，请问诸位眼下作何感想？”
普朗塔先生仿佛被晴天霹雳击中了一般，双臂颓然垂落在身侧。至于在法官面前早已重新换上小布商畏缩嘴脸的勒科克先生，更是惊得差点被嘴里的含片活活噎死。
“真见鬼！”他失声嚷道，“这可真够硬扎的啊！”他傻乎乎地咧嘴一笑，随后压低嗓门凑近普朗塔耳边补充道：
“够硬扎！然而这全都在咱们的意料算计之中。伯爵夫人临死前手中死死攥着一截布条；显而易见，那是凶手居心叵测、故意塞进她掌心里的。”
多米尼先生根本没有听清这句耳语。他同普朗塔先生握了握手，相约明早在法院碰头。随后便携同书记官扬长而去。
被戴上锃亮手铐的盖斯潘与老贝尔托，就在几分钟前已在奥尔西瓦尔宪兵的押解下，踏上了押往科尔贝伊大牢的囚途。

## VIII / 第八章
===Original===

Dr. Gendron had just finished his sad task in the billiard-room. He had taken off his long coat, and pulled up his shirtsleeves above his elbows. His instruments lay on a table near him; he had covered the body with a long white sheet. Night had come, and a large lamp, with a crystal globe, lighted up the gloomy scene. The doctor, leaning over a water-basin, was washing his hands, when the old justice of the peace and the detective entered.
“Ah, it’s you, Plantat,” said the doctor in a suppressed tone; “where is Monsieur Domini?”
“Gone.”
The doctor did not take the trouble to repress a vexed motion.
“I must speak with him, though,” said he, “it’s absolutely necessary﻿—and the sooner the better; for perhaps I am wrong﻿—I may be mistaken﻿—”
M. Lecoq and M. Plantat approached him, having carefully closed the door. The doctor was paler than the corpse which lay under the sheet. His usually calm features betrayed great distress. This change could not have been caused by the task in which he had been engaged. Of course it was a painful one; but M. Gendron was one of those experienced practitioners who have felt the pulse of every human misery, and whose disgust had become torpid by the most hideous spectacles. He must have discovered something extraordinary.
“I am going to ask you what you asked me a while ago,” said M. Plantat. “Are you ill or suffering?”
M. Gendron shook his head sorrowfully, and answered, slowly and emphatically:
“I will answer you, as you did me; ’tis nothing, I am already better.”
Then these two, equally profound, turned away their heads, as if fearing to exchange their ideas; they doubted lest their looks should betray them.
M. Lecoq advanced and spoke.
“I believe I know the cause of the doctor’s emotion. He has just discovered that Madame de Trémorel was killed by a single blow, and that the assassins afterward set themselves to disfiguring the body, when it was nearly cold.”
The doctor’s eyes fastened on the detective, with a stupefied expression.
“How could you divine that?” he asked.
“Oh, I didn’t guess it alone; I ought to share the honor of the theory which has enabled us to foresee this fact, with Monsieur Plantat.”
“Oh,” cried the doctor, striking his forehead, “now, I recollect your advice; in my worry, I must say, I had quite forgotten it.
“Well,” he added, “your foresight is confirmed.

===Chinese===

让德龙医生刚刚在台球室里完成了他那桩令人心酸的差事。他脱下了长外套，把衬衫衣袖挽到了手肘上方。他的解剖器械摆在身旁的一张桌上；他用一床长长的白床单覆盖住了遗体。夜幕已经降临，一盏带有水晶玻璃罩的大油灯照亮了这阴森凄惨的场景。医生正俯身在一个水盆上方洗手，老治安法官和大侦探推门走了进来。
“啊，是你啊，普朗塔，”医生压低嗓门说道，“多米尼先生人呢？”
“走了。”
医生甚至都懒得掩饰自己懊恼不悦的神态。
“可我必须立刻同他谈谈，”他说，“刻不容缓——越快越好；因为很可能我料错了——我也许会看走眼——”
勒科克先生与普朗塔先生仔细合上房门，走近了他身边。医生的脸色比白床单底下覆盖的尸体还要惨白。他平日里沉着冷静的面容此刻写满了难以名状的痛苦与不安。这种神态的剧变绝不可能是由于方才经手的解剖差事引起的。那自然是一桩令人痛苦的差事；然而让德龙医生是一位见多识广的老资格医家，早已把脉过人间所有的苦难，面对哪怕最惨不忍睹的恐怖景象也早已见怪不怪、麻木不仁。他必定是发现了某种惊天动地的异样。
“我也想用您方才问我的那句话来问问您，”普朗塔先生说，“您是身子不适，还是正饱受痛苦的折磨？”
让德龙医生悲伤地摇了摇头，语气缓慢而加重分量地回答道：
“我的回答同您方才的应答别无二致：没什么，我已经好多了。”
随后这两位城府同样深不可测的高手各自扭过头去，仿佛生怕交换彼此心底的念头；他们提防着彼此的目光会出卖内心的隐秘。
勒科克先生上前一步，开了口：
“在下猜想自己知晓医生心绪难平的缘由。您老刚刚查出特雷莫雷尔夫人其实是被一刀毙命的，而凶手是在事后、在遗体几乎已经冰凉透了的时候，才动手疯狂残害破坏尸体的，对吧。”
医生的双眼死死钉在大侦探脸上，神情错愕得目瞪口呆。
“你怎么可能未卜先知猜出这一层？”他惊问。
“噢，这并非在下一人之功；能够事先预见到这一事实的推论假说的功劳，理该由在下与普朗塔先生共同分享。”
“啊，”医生拍了拍额头失声叫道，“这会儿我总算记起你方才的提醒了；方才我心乱如麻，说实在的，全给抛到脑后了。
“好吧，”他接着说道，“你们的预见得到了彻底证实。

===Original===

Perhaps not so much time as you suppose elapsed between the first blow and the rest; but I am convinced that the countess had ceased to live nearly three hours, when the last blows were struck.”
M. Gendron went to the billiard-table, and slowly raised the sheet, discovering the head and part of the bust.
“Let us inform ourselves, Plantat,” he said.
The old justice of the peace took the lamp, and passed to the other side of the table. His hand trembled so that the globe tingled. The vacillating light cast gloomy shadows upon the walls. The countess’s face had been carefully bathed, the blood and mud effaced. The marks of the blows were thus more visible, but they still found upon that livid countenance, the traces of its beauty. M. Lecoq stood at the head of the table, leaning over to see more clearly.
“The countess,” said Dr. Gendron, “received eighteen blows from a dagger. Of these, but one is mortal; it is this one, the direction of which is nearly vertical﻿—a little below the shoulder, you see.” He pointed out the wound, sustaining the body in his left arm. The eyes had preserved a frightful expression. It seemed as if the half-open mouth were about to cry “Help! Help!”
Plantat, the man with a heart of stone, turned away his head, and the doctor, having mastered his first emotion, continued in a professionally apathetic tone:
“The blade must have been an inch wide, and eight inches long. All the other wounds﻿—those on the arms, breast, and shoulders, are comparatively slight. They must have been inflicted at least two hours after that which caused death.”
“Good,” said M. Lecoq.
“Observe that I am not positive,” returned the doctor quickly. “I merely state a probability. The phenomena on which I base my own conviction are too fugitive, too capricious in their nature, to enable me to be absolutely certain.”
This seemed to disturb M. Lecoq.
“But, from the moment when﻿—”
“What I can affirm,” interrupted Dr. Gendron, “what I would affirm under oath, is, that all the wounds on the head, excepting one, were inflicted after death. No doubt of that whatever﻿—none whatever. Here, above the eye, is the blow given while the countess was alive.”
“It seems to me, Doctor,” observed M. Lecoq, “that we may conclude from the proved fact that the countess, after death, was struck by a flat implement, that she had also ceased to live when she was mutilated by the knife.”
M.

===Chinese===

从第一记致命伤到随后的乱刀破坏，其间相隔的时辰或许没有你们推想的那么漫长；但我敢断言，当最后几刀刺中尸体时，伯爵夫人早已气绝身亡近三个小时了。”
让德龙医生踱到台球桌旁，缓缓揭起白床单，露出了死者的头颅与部分胸部。
“咱们亲自来验看一下吧，普朗塔，”他说。
老治安法官端起油灯，绕到了球桌的另一侧。他的手抖得厉害，以至于灯罩发出阵阵微弱的叮当声。摇曳不定的烛光在四壁投下阴森斑驳的黑影。伯爵夫人的面容已被仔细擦洗过，血迹和污泥全被清洗干净。创口的痕迹因而变得更加清晰刺目，然而人们依然能在那张惨白无血色的面孔上寻觅到昔日倾国倾城的美貌印记。勒科克先生立在桌头，俯下身子凝神细瞧。
“伯爵夫人，”让德龙医生说，“一共挨了18刀短剑刺伤。其中，唯有一处是致命的；正是这一处，刺入的角度几乎垂直——就在肩胛骨下方一点，你们瞧。”他托住遗体，用手指点着伤口。死者的双眼依然保持着骇人的惊恐神采。那半张着的双唇仿佛随时要绝望地呼救：“救命！救命啊！”
普朗塔，那个被众人视作铁石心肠的男人，痛苦地扭过头去，而医生在克制住最初的情感波澜后，用医家冷漠的客观口吻接着说道：
“凶器的刀刃必定宽约一英寸，长约八英寸。其余的所有伤口——手臂上、胸脯上以及双肩上的创口，相对而言都极其轻微。那些伤痕必定是在导致死亡的致命一击至少两小时之后才被刺上去的。”
“漂亮，”勒科克先生赞道。
“请注意我并未下百分之百的断语，”医生急忙澄清道，“我不过是在陈述一种极大的概率。作为我个人内心确信依据的生理现象实在太转瞬即逝、太变幻莫测了，根本不足以让我得出绝对毋庸置疑的定论。”
这番保留似乎让勒科克先生颇感不安。
“但是，既然已经查实——”
“我能够断言、且敢于在法庭上指天发誓的是，”让德龙医生打断道，“头部除了一处伤痕之外，所有的钝器伤全是在死后击打形成的。对此绝无半点疑问——毫无疑问。唯独这处伤，位于眼眶上方，是伯爵夫人生前遭受的打击。”
“依在下之见，医生，”勒科克先生指出，“既然已经查明伯爵夫人在气绝身亡之后曾遭受过钝器打击，我们自然同样有理由推论出，当她遭受乱刀残害时，她同样早已不在人世了。”

===Original===

Gendron reflected a moment.
“It is possible that you are right; as for me, I am persuaded of it. Still the conclusions in my report will not be yours. The physician consulted by the law, should only pronounce upon patent, demonstrated facts. If he has a doubt, even the slightest, he should hold his tongue. I will say more; if there is any uncertainty, my opinion is that the accused, and not the prosecution, should have the benefit of it.”
This was certainly not the detective’s opinion, but he was cautious not to say so. He had followed Dr. Gendron with anxious attention, and the contraction of his face showed the travail of his mind.
“It seems to me now possible,” said he, “to determine how and where the countess was struck.”
The doctor had covered the body, and Plantat had replaced the lamp on the little table. Both asked M. Lecoq to explain himself.
“Very well,” resumed the detective. “The direction of the wound proves to me that the countess was in her chamber taking tea, seated, her body inclined a little forward, when she was murdered. The assassin came up behind her with his arm raised; he chose his position coolly, and struck her with terrific force. The violence of the blow was such that the victim fell forward, and in the fall, her forehead struck the end of the table; she thus gave herself the only fatal blow which we have discovered on the head.”
M. Gendron looked from one to the other of his companions, who exchanged significant glances. Perhaps he suspected the game they were playing.
“The crime must evidently have been committed as you say,” said he.
There was another embarrassing silence. M. Lecoq’s obstinate muteness annoyed Plantat, who finally asked him:
“Have you seen all you want to see?”
“All for today; I shall need daylight for what remains. I am confident, indeed, that with the exception of one detail that worries me, I have the key to the mystery.”
“We must be here, then, early tomorrow morning.”
“I will be here at any hour you will name.”
“Your search finished, we will go together to Monsieur Domini, at Corbeil.”
“I am quite at your orders.”
There was another pause.
M. Plantat perceived that M. Lecoq guessed his thoughts; and did not understand the detective’s capriciousness; a little while before, he had been very loquacious, but now held his tongue. M.

===Chinese===

让德龙沉思片刻。
“或许你是对的；就我个人而言，我也对此深信不疑。然而在呈交法庭的正式法医报告中，我的结论却绝不能像你这般草率。受聘于司法的医官，唯有依据确凿无疑、经得起检验的事实发表定论。倘若心中存有一丝一毫的怀疑，哪怕最微弱的一丝，他就应当紧闭双唇。我还要多说一句：倘若存在任何模糊不确定之处，依我之见，应当将这种利益归于被告，而非控方。”
这显然与大侦探一贯的职业操守大相径庭，但他极其审慎地没有当面反驳。他自始至终怀着焦虑的关注紧随让德龙医生的论断，脸上每一寸紧绷的肌肉无不昭示着他心智的高速运转。
“在下眼下似乎已经能够断定，”刑警开口道，“伯爵夫人究竟是在何处、以何种姿态遭受致命一击的了。”
医生重新用白单盖好了遗体，普朗塔把油灯放回到了小桌上。两人纷纷敦促勒科克先生详加剖析。
“好吧，”大侦探接着说道，“从伤口刺入的角度足可向在下证明，遇害之时，伯爵夫人正安坐在这间寝居里喝茶，身子微微前倾。凶手高举利刃悄然潜行至她身后；他极其从容冷静地选准了致命身位，凝聚全身蛮力狠狠刺了下去。这一击的力道是如此狂暴凶猛，以至于受害者猝不及防猛向前栽倒，在前倾摔落的一瞬间，她的前额重重磕在了茶几边缘；正是在这剧烈的撞击中，她自己给自己造成了我们在她头部发现的唯一一记生前致命伤。”
让德龙医生轮流打量着身旁的两位同伴，两人正心照不宣地交换着意味深长的眼神。或许医生也隐隐猜到了他们暗中较劲的心思。
“大案显然正是按照你方才所说的经过犯下的，”医生说道。
四周再度陷入了一阵尴尬的沉寂。勒科克先生执拗的缄默让普朗塔颇感心焦，老人终于忍不住发问道：
“你想看的现场都已经看全了吗？”
“今天能看的全看齐了；剩下的线索需要借助大白天的日光。说实在的，除了一个至今依然困扰在下的细节之外，在下坚信自己已经紧紧攥住了通往这桩离奇谜案的钥匙。”
“那么明早咱们得趁早赶到这里。”
“只要您定下时辰，在下随时恭候。”
“等您的勘查彻底完工，咱们便一同动身前往科尔贝伊拜会多米尼先生。”
“悉听尊便。”
两人再度陷入了沉默。
普朗塔先生敏锐地察觉到勒科克先生猜透了自己的心思；却捉摸不透大侦探古怪的脾气秉性；就在不久之前，这小子还口若悬河滔滔不绝，此刻却把嘴巴闭得像个蚌壳。

===Original===

Lecoq, on the other hand, was delighted to puzzle the old man a little, and formed the intention to astonish him the next morning, by giving him a report which should faithfully reflect all his ideas. Meanwhile he had taken out his lozenge-box, and was entrusting a hundred secrets to the portrait.
“Well,” said the doctor, “there remains nothing more to be done except to retire.”
“I was just going to ask permission to do so,” said M. Lecoq. “I have been fasting ever since morning.”
M. Plantat now took a bold step.
“Shall you return to Paris tonight, Monsieur Lecoq?” asked he, abruptly.
“No; I came prepared to remain overnight; I’ve brought my nightgown, which I left, before coming up here, at the little roadside inn below. I shall sup and sleep there.”
“You will be poorly off at the Faithful Grenadier,” said the old justice of the peace. “You will do better to come and dine with me.”
“You are really too good, Monsieur﻿—”
“Besides, we have a good deal to say, and so you must remain the night with me; we will get your nightclothes as we pass along.”
M. Lecoq bowed, flattered and grateful for the invitation.
“And I shall carry you off, too, Doctor,” continued M. Plantat, “whether you will or not. Now, don’t say no. If you insist on going to Corbeil tonight, we will carry you over after supper.”
The operation of fixing the seals was speedily concluded; narrow strips of parchment, held by large waxen seals, were affixed to all the doors, as well as to the bureau in which the articles gathered for the purposes of the investigation had been deposited.

===Chinese===

反观勒科克，暗自得意于能让这位老先生捉摸不透，并暗下决心明早要呈递出一份完美契合老人全部构想的惊天勘查报告，好让老家伙惊得合不拢嘴。与此同时，他又摸出了那个牛角含片盒，借着盒盖上的画像向亡妻倾诉着100个不可告人的秘密。
“好啦，”医生说，“眼下除了打道回府，也没什么差事可办了。”
“在下也正想告退呢，”勒科克先生说，“实不相瞒，打今早起在下可就一直水米未进呢。”
普朗塔先生此时做出了一项大胆的决断。
“勒科克先生，您今晚打算赶回巴黎吗？”他冷不丁发问道。
“不回了；在下来时就做好了留宿过夜的准备；我把随身带的睡袍押在山脚下路边的那间小客栈里了。我打算在那儿对付一顿晚饭合眼睡上一觉。”
“在‘忠诚近卫兵’客栈你可歇息不好，”老治安法官说道，“不如屈尊上我府上共进晚餐吧。”
“先生实在太客气了——”
“更何况，咱们还有许多要紧话要促膝长谈呢，所以今晚你务必住在我府上；咱们路过客栈时顺便把你的换洗衣裳取上就是了。”
勒科克先生躬身致谢，对这番热忱的邀约深感受用与感激。
“至于您，医生，我也要把您强拉了去，”普朗塔先生接着说道，“不管您乐意不乐意。可千万别推辞。要是您执意今晚非回科尔贝伊不可，晚饭后我们自会备车把您妥妥帖帖送回去。”
查封贴条的差事转眼间便告竣；用厚实火漆大印盖紧的狭长羊皮纸封条，严严实实贴在了所有的门户上，以及那个存放着大案侦查所有搜集证物的写字台上。

## X / 第十章
===Original===

M. Plantat’s house was small and narrow; a philosopher’s house. Three large rooms on the ground-floor, four chambers in the first story, an attic under the roof for the servants, composed all its apartments. Everywhere the carelessness of a man who has withdrawn from the world into himself, for years, ceasing to have the least interest in the objects which surround him, was apparent. The furniture was shabby, though it had been elegant; the mouldings had come off, the clocks had ceased to keep time, the chairs showed the stuffing of their cushions, the curtains, in places, were faded by the sun. The library alone betrayed a daily care and attention.
Long rows of books in calf and gilt were ranged on the carved oaken shelves, a movable table near the fireplace contained M. Plantat’s favorite books, the discreet friends of his solitude. A spacious conservatory, fitted with every accessory and convenience, was his only luxury. In it flourished one hundred and thirty-seven varieties of briars.
Two servants, the widow Petit, cook and housekeeper, and Louis, gardener, inhabited the house. If they did not make it a noisy one, it was because Plantat, who talked little, detested also to hear others talk. Silence was there a despotic law. It was very hard for Mme. Petit, especially at first. She was very talkative, so talkative that when she found no one to chat with, she went to confession; to confess was to chat. She came near leaving the place twenty times; but the thought of an assured pension restrained her. Gradually she became accustomed to govern her tongue, and to this cloistral silence. But she revenged herself outside for the privations of the household, and regained among the neighbors the time lost at home.
She was very much wrought up on the day of the murder. At eleven o’clock, after going out for news, she had prepared monsieur’s dinner; but he did not appear. She waited one, two hours, five hours, keeping her water boiling for the eggs; no monsieur. She wanted to send Louis to look for him, but Louis being a poor talker and not curious, asked her to go herself. The house was besieged by the female neighbors, who, thinking that Mme. Petit ought to be well posted, came for news; no news to give.
Toward five o’clock, giving up all thought of breakfast, she began to prepare for dinner.

===Chinese===

普朗塔先生的宅邸小巧而狭长；那是一处属于哲学家的清幽居所。底楼三间大屋，二楼四间起居室，外加屋顶下一间供下人起居的阁楼，便构成了全部的格局。整座屋子无处不显露出一名多年来离群索居、深居简出、对周遭陈设已彻底丧失哪怕半分兴致之人的漫不经心。家具虽然曾经颇为考究，如今却已破旧不堪；木饰雕花早已剥落，座钟早已停摆，座椅露出了坐垫里的填充棉绒，窗帘更有好几处被日光晒得褪了色。唯独藏书室显露出日复一日的精心打理与悉心呵护。
刻花橡木书架上整整齐齐码放着一排排烫金小牛皮精装书，壁炉旁的一张活动书桌上摆着普朗塔先生平日最心爱的读物，那些是他孤独岁月中含蓄寡言的知己。一间配备了各种完备设施与便利构件的宽敞温室，则是他唯一的奢华排场。里面郁郁葱葱繁育着137种各色欧石楠与刺花。
两名仆役侍奉在这栋宅邸里：掌勺兼管家的珀蒂寡妇（widow Petit），以及园丁路易（Louis）。他们之所以没把屋里弄得喧嚣吵闹，全是因为平日少言寡语的普朗塔先生深恶痛绝听到旁人喋喋不休。静默在这里是一道专制而森严的律法。这对珀蒂夫人来说尤其难熬，起初更是苦不堪言。她是个天生极爱唠叨的长舌妇，多嘴到了若是找不着人闲扯就跑去教堂忏悔的地步；在她眼里，忏悔就是找神父拉家常。她曾动过20多次辞工不干的念头；然而一想到那笔旱涝保丰收的养老津贴，便又咬牙忍了下来。渐渐地，她学会了管住自己的舌头，适应了这种近乎修道院般的死寂。然而她跑到屋外疯狂弥补在家里遭受的憋闷，把在家里耽搁的闲白全在街坊四邻间一股脑补了回来。
命案发生的那一天，她整个人简直坐立难安。上午11点，打探了一圈消息回来后，她给先生备好了午饭；然而先生却迟迟不见踪影。她等了一个小时、两个小时、五个小时，始终烧着热水预备给先生煮鸡蛋；可先生依旧杳无音信。她想打发路易出门去寻，但路易生性拙嘴笨舌又缺乏好奇心，反劝她自己走一趟。宅邸大门被街坊女人们围得水泄不通，大家认定珀蒂夫人必定消息灵通，纷纷跑来打探虚实；然而她却无料可爆。
到了下午将近5点，她彻底放弃了午饭的念头，开始张罗起晚饭来。

===Original===

But when the village bell struck eight o’clock, monsieur had not made his appearance. At nine, the good woman was beside herself, and began to scold Louis, who had just come in from watering the garden, and, seated at the kitchen table, was soberly eating a plate of soup.
The bell rung.
“Ah, there’s monsieur, at last.”
No, it was not monsieur, but a little boy, whom M. Plantat had sent from Valfeuillu to apprise Mme. Petit that he would soon return, bringing with him two guests who would dine and sleep at the house. The worthy woman nearly fainted. It was the first time that M. Plantat had invited anyone to dinner for five years. There was some mystery at the bottom of it﻿—so thought Mme. Petit, and her anger doubled with her curiosity.
“To order a dinner at this hour,” she grumbled. “Has he got common sense, then?” But reflecting that time pressed, she continued:
“Go along, Louis; this is not the moment for two feet to stay in one shoe. Hurry up, and wring three chickens’ heads; see if there ain’t some ripe grapes in the conservatory; bring on some preserves; fetch some wine from the cellar!” The dinner was well advanced when the bell rung again. This time Baptiste appeared, in exceeding bad humor, bearing M. Lecoq’s nightgown.
“See here,” said he to the cook, “what the person, who is with your master, gave me to bring here.”
“What person?”
“How do I know? He’s a spy sent down from Paris about this Valfeuillu affair; not much good, probably﻿—ill-bred﻿—a brute﻿—and a wretch.”
“But he’s not alone with monsieur?”
“No; Doctor Gendron is with them.”
Mme. Petit burned to get some news out of Baptiste; but Baptiste also burned to get back and know what was taking place at his master’s﻿—so off he went, without having left any news behind.
An hour or more passed, and Mme. Petit had just angrily declared to Louis that she was going to throw the dinner out the window, when her master at last appeared, followed by his guests. They had not exchanged a word after they left the mayor’s. Aside from the fatigues of the evening, they wished to reflect, and to resume their self-command. Mme. Petit found it useless to question their faces﻿—they told her nothing. But she did not agree with Baptiste about M. Lecoq: she thought him good-humored, and rather silly.

===Chinese===

然而当村里钟楼的钟声敲响8点时，先生依旧没有露面。到了9点，这位好心的妇人急得六神无主，开始数落起刚给花园浇完水、正坐在厨房餐桌旁老老实实喝着一碗热汤的路易。
门铃响了。
“哎呀，先生总算回来了。”
不，来者并非先生，而是一个小男孩，是普朗塔先生从瓦尔菲伊派来给珀蒂夫人报信的，告诉她自己很快便会回府，并带回两位在此用膳并留宿的贵客。这位可敬的妇人闻言差点当场背过气去。这可是普朗塔先生5年内头一遭破天荒邀请客人来家里用膳。这背后必定藏着天大的谜团——珀蒂夫人心中暗忖，而她的怒火也随着强烈的好奇心翻倍暴涨。
“在这个节骨眼上叫张罗饭菜，”她嘟囔着，“他难道连半点常理都不讲了吗？”然而一想到时间紧迫，她接着吩咐道：
“快动起来，路易；眼下可不是一只脚踩在另一只鞋里磨蹭的当口。麻利点，拧死3只鸡的脖子；去温室里瞧瞧有没有熟透的葡萄；端几样腌渍蜜饯出来；再去地窖里抱几瓶好酒！”
晚饭刚筹备得差不多，门铃又急促地响了起来。这一回现身的是巴蒂斯特，满脸悻悻然的不悦神色，手里抱着勒科克先生的睡袍。
“拿着吧，”他对厨娘说，“这是跟在你们家先生身边的那位主儿让我给送过来的。”
“哪位主儿？”
“我哪知道？是个为了瓦尔菲伊大案从巴黎调下来的暗探；八成不是什么正经货色——粗鲁无礼——简直就是个粗鄙恶劣的混球。”
“可他就一个人同先生在一块儿？”
“不；让德龙医生也同他们在一起呢。”
珀蒂夫人急不可耐想从巴蒂斯特嘴里套出些案情猛料；然而巴蒂斯特同样急着火速赶回东家府上打探大祸的究竟——于是他拔腿便走，连半句风声都没留下。
一个多小时过去了，珀蒂夫人正怒气冲冲地对路易嚷嚷着要把整桌饭菜全从窗户倒出去，这时她的主人终于领着客人现身了。离开镇长宅邸后，三人一路上没交谈过半句。除了应付一整晚侦查的疲惫之外，他们更急于沉淀心智，重新稳住内心的镇定。珀蒂夫人发觉打量他们的神色纯属白费心机——他们的面孔上看不出半点波澜。不过她可不同意巴蒂斯特对勒科克先生的诋毁：在她看来，这位客人和蔼可亲，甚至透着几分傻气。

===Original===

Though the party was less silent at the dinner-table, all avoided, as if by tacit consent, any allusion to the events of the day. No one would ever have thought that they had just been witnesses of, almost actors in, the Valfeuillu drama, they were so calm, and talked so glibly of indifferent things. From time to time, indeed, a question remained unanswered, or a reply came tardily; but nothing of the sensations and thoughts, which were concealed beneath the uttered commonplaces, appeared on the surface.
Louis passed to and fro behind the diners, his white cloth on his arm, carving and passing the wine.
