The effect of the famous detective’s name on his antagonist was magical. He naturally laid down his arms and surrendered, straightway becoming respectful and obsequious. It almost flattered him to be roughly handled by such a celebrity. He muttered, in an abashed and admiring tone:
“What, is it possible? You, Monsieur Lecoq!”
“Yes, it is I, young man; but console yourself; I bear no grudge against you.

===Chinese===

“这桩细微末节很可能干系重大。针对盖斯潘最不利的铁证是什么？正是从他衣兜里搜出来的那笔现银。咱们不妨暂且做个假设：前天夜里10点，他在巴黎破开了一张面值1000法郎的纸币。得到那张千法郎纸钞，能成为瓦尔菲伊凶案的作案动机吗？绝无可能，因为在那一刻凶案根本尚未发生。那张钞票从何而来？眼下我无暇深究。但倘若我的推断无误，司法部门便不得不承认，从盖斯潘身上搜出的那数百法郎现款，完全可以、而且必然正是破开那张纸钞找零剩下的零钱。”
“那充其量只是推测构想，”多米尼先生有些恼火地辩白道。
“确实如此；但这项推测完全可能坐实为铁案。接下来我还想问问这位警员，盖斯潘买下那些器具后究竟是如何带走的？是随手揣进了口袋，还是让人打包成捆？若是打了包，又是怎么包扎的？”
侦探的声音尖锐、冰冷而强硬，言辞间夹杂着尖刻的冷嘲热讽，吓得他的科尔贝伊同行那点底气彻底荡然无存。
“我不知道，”后者结结巴巴地嘟囔道，“他们没跟我提——我还以为——”
勒柯克先生高高扬起双手，仿佛在呼唤苍天为这等蠢材作证；但在他心底，能逮住这个绝佳良机好好回敬多米尼先生先前的傲慢蔑视，简直令他暗爽不已。他不能也不敢当面顶撞法官；但他完全有权拿这个警员开涮，将一肚子怨气全撒在他身上。
“哈，我说好伙计，”他说道，“那你大老远跑去巴黎究竟干了些什么？难道只是把盖斯潘的相片拿给人瞧瞧，顺便向‘火神锻造坊’的人详述一番案情？人家倒是该好好谢谢你；不过换作普朗塔先生的管家珀蒂夫人去办，成效也绝不比你差。”
遭此当头一棒，警员终于按捺不住怒火了；他倒竖双眉，粗声大气地嚷嚷起来：
“我说你小子，你算个——”
“得了吧，闭嘴，”勒柯克先生打断道，“少跟我吆五喝六，睁大眼睛看清楚是在跟谁说话。鄙人正是勒柯克先生。”
这位名震四海的大侦探的名字，在对手身上起到了立竿见影的神奇效力。大汉几乎本能地缴械投降，顷刻间变得低眉顺眼、毕恭毕敬。能被如此赫赫有名的大人物痛斥训斥一顿，甚至让他感到受宠若惊。他用羞愧而敬仰的语调喃喃道：
“怎么，天哪，这怎么可能？您就是勒柯克先生！”
“不错，正是在下，小伙子；不过放宽心，我犯不着同你计较。

===Original===

You don’t know your trade, but you have done me a service and you have brought us a convincing proof of Guespin’s innocence.”
M. Domini looked on at this scene with secret chagrin. His recruit went over to the enemy, yielding without a struggle to a confessed superiority. M. Lecoq’s presumption, in speaking of a prisoner’s innocence whose guilt seemed to the judge indisputable, exasperated him.
“And what is this tremendous proof, if you please?” asked he.
“It is simple and striking,” answered M. Lecoq, putting on his most frivolous air as his conclusions narrowed the field of probabilities.
“You doubtless recollect that when we were at Valfeuillu we found the hands of the clock in the bedroom stopped at twenty minutes past three. Distrusting foul play, I put the striking apparatus in motion﻿—do you recall it? What happened? The clock struck eleven. That convinced us that the crime was committed before that hour. But don’t you see that if Guespin was at the Vulcan’s Forges at ten he could not have got back to Valfeuillu before midnight? Therefore it was not he who did the deed.”
The detective, as he came to this conclusion, pulled out the inevitable box and helped himself to a lozenge, at the same time bestowing upon the judge a smile which said:
“Get out of that, if you can.”
The judge’s whole theory tumbled to pieces if M. Lecoq’s deductions were right; but he could not admit that he had been so much deceived; he could not renounce an opinion formed by deliberate reflection.
“I don’t pretend that Guespin is the only criminal,” said he. “He could only have been an accomplice; and that he was.”
“An accomplice? No, Judge, he was a victim. Ah, Trémorel is a great rascal! Don’t you see now why he put forward the hands? At first I didn’t perceive the object of advancing the time five hours; now it is clear. In order to implicate Guespin the crime must appear to have been committed after midnight, and﻿—”
He suddenly checked himself and stopped with open mouth and fixed eyes as a new idea crossed his mind. The judge, who was bending over his papers trying to find something to sustain his position, did not perceive this.
“But then,” said the latter, “how do you explain Guespin’s refusal to speak and to give an account of where he spent the night?”
M. Lecoq had now recovered from his emotion, and Dr. Gendron and M.

===Chinese===

你虽然不懂行规手艺，但你实实在在帮了我一个大忙，并且为我们带回了证明盖斯潘清白无辜的如山铁证。”
多米尼先生看在眼里，心头暗生闷气。自己手下的新兵临阵倒戈倒向了对手，在压倒性的权威面前不战自降。而勒柯克先生竟然胆敢为一个在法官看来罪无可赦的囚徒洗刷冤屈，这种狂妄自大更令他火冒三丈。
“那么请教高明，你口中那份了不得的铁证究竟指什么？”他冷声问道。
“事情既浅显又醒目，”勒柯克先生答道，随着结论逐步缩紧了概率范围，他重新摆出了一副玩世不恭的轻浮神态。
“想必您还记得，当我们在瓦尔菲伊卧房勘验时，壁炉钟的指针停在3点20分。由于怀疑有人做过手脚，我拨动了报时机械——您可曾记得？结果如何？钟声敲了11下。这足以让我们确信，凶案是在11点之前发生的。但您难道没瞧出来吗？如果盖斯潘在夜里10点还身处‘火神锻造坊’，他在午夜之前无论如何也绝不可能赶回瓦尔菲伊！因此，动手的绝不可能是他。”
侦探道出这个推断时，掏出了那只雷打不动的糖果盒，含了一颗糖球，同时朝法官投去一个充满挑衅意味的微笑，仿佛在说：
“有本事，您倒推翻试试看。”
倘若勒柯克的推演无误，法官的全套理论便彻底轰然坍塌；但他绝不甘心承认自己竟被蒙蔽至深，更不愿轻易推翻自己经过深思熟虑建立起来的定见。
“我并没断言盖斯潘是独行大盗，”法官辩称道，“他完全可能是从犯；而且他必然是从犯。”
“从犯？不，法官大人，他是个受害者。哈，特雷莫雷尔真是一个狡猾透顶的恶棍！难道您现在还不明白他为何要故意拨快钟摆时针吗？起初我也不明白把时间平白拨快5个小时用意何在；如今一切昭然若揭。为了嫁祸栽赃给盖斯潘，凶案现场必须伪装成是午夜之后发生的，而——”
他猛然刹住话头，张着嘴巴，双目发直，脑中灵光一现浮现出一个全新的构想。法官此时正伏在案头埋首翻阅公文卷宗，试图寻觅能支撑自己立场的依据，并未察觉到侦探的神情异样。
“即便如此，”法官开口道，“那又该如何解释盖斯潘坚决拒绝交代口供、拒不说明自己那一夜究竟身在何处呢？”
勒柯克先生此刻已从震惊中平复过来，而让德龙医生与普朗塔先生

===Original===

Plantat, who were watching him with the deepest attention, saw a triumphant light in his eyes. Doubtless he had just found a solution of the problem which had been put to him.
“I understand,” replied he, “and can explain Guespin’s obstinate silence. I should be perfectly amazed if he decided to speak just now.”
M. Domini misconstrued the meaning of this; he thought he saw in it a covert intention to banter him.
“He has had a night to reflect upon it,” he answered. “Is not twelve hours enough to mature a system of defence?”
The detective shook his head doubtfully.
“It is certain that he does not need it,” said he. “Our prisoner doesn’t trouble himself about a system of defence, that I’ll swear to.”
“He keeps quiet, because he hasn’t been able to get up a plausible story.”
“No, no; believe me, he isn’t trying to get up one. In my opinion, Guespin is a victim; that is, I suspect Trémorel of having set an infamous trap for him, into which he has fallen, and in which he sees himself so completely caught that he thinks it useless to struggle. The poor wretch is convinced that the more he resists the more surely he will tighten the web that is woven around him.”
“I think so, too,” said M. Plantat.
“The true criminal, Count Hector,” resumed the detective, “lost his presence of mind at the last moment, and thus lost all the advantages which his previous caution had gained. Don’t let us forget that he is an able man, perfidious enough to mature the most infamous stratagems, and unscrupulous enough to execute them. He knows that justice must have its victims, one for every crime; he does not forget that the police, as long as it has not the criminal, is always on the search with eye and ear open; and he has thrown us Guespin as a huntsman, closely pressed, throws his glove to the bear that is close upon him. Perhaps he thought that the innocent man would not be in danger of his life; at all events he hoped to gain time by this ruse; while the bear is smelling and turning over the glove, the huntsman gains ground, escapes and reaches his place of refuge; that was what Trémorel proposed to do.”
The Corbeil policeman was now undoubtedly Lecoq’s most enthusiastic listener. Goulard literally drank in his chief’s words.

===Chinese===

一直在全神贯注凝视着他，敏锐地捕捉到了他眼眸中闪烁的胜利光芒。毫无疑问，他刚刚觅得了摆在他面前这道难题的答案。
“我全想通了，”他答道，“我完全能解释盖斯潘为何顽固地守口如瓶。如果他此刻当真肯开口招供，我反而才会大吃一惊呢。”
多米尼先生曲解了这番话的含义，以为对方又在变相挖苦自己。
“他已经有整整一夜的工夫反省推敲，”法官反驳道，“难道12个小时还不够他构思出一套天衣无缝的辩解防线吗？”
侦探满腹疑虑地摇了摇头。
“可以肯定的是，他根本用不着辩解套路，”他说道，“我可以对天发誓，咱们这位嫌犯压根就没费心去琢磨过什么脱罪辞令。”
“他之所以一声不吭，是因为他编造不出合情合理的说辞。”
“不，不；请相信我，他根本没想去编造什么。依我之见，盖斯潘纯属受害者；换句话说，我怀疑特雷莫雷尔为他布下了一个极其恶毒的圈套，而他一头栽了进去，眼见自己被网罗得如此严实，深知任何挣扎皆是徒劳。这个可怜虫深信，自己反抗得越凶，笼罩在他身上的罗网就勒得越紧。”
“我也深有同感，”普朗塔先生说道。
“真正的元凶埃克托伯爵，”侦探继续剖析道，“在最后一刻慌了神乱了方寸，从而将先前那般深思熟虑所积攒的所有胜势葬送殆尽。咱们绝不能忘记，他是个老谋深算的高手，阴险狡诈足以构思出最卑劣的毒计，心狠手辣足以将其付诸实施。他深知司法审判必须有人伏法抵命，每一桩血案都得抓到替罪羊；他更清楚只要警署一天抓不到真凶，就会睁大双眼、竖起耳朵穷追不舍；于是，他便把盖斯潘抛给了我们，正如一个被逼入绝境的猎人在黑熊扑向自己的千钧一发之际，随手把手套扔向凶兽一般。或许他自以为这个无辜之人不至于丢掉性命；无论如何，他指望借这一金蝉脱壳之计赢得逃跑的时机。就在狗熊嗅闻、拨弄手套的当口，猎人早已拉开距离拔腿飞奔，遁入避难之所；特雷莫雷尔打的正是这副如意算盘。”
此时此刻，科尔贝伊警员无疑成了勒柯克最忠实的狂热拥趸。古拉尔简直是在如饥似渴地痛饮着这位前辈长官字字珠玑的教诲。

===Original===

He had never heard any of his colleagues express themselves with such fervor and authority; he had had no idea of such eloquence, and he stood erect, as if some of the admiration which he saw in all the faces were reflected back on him. He grew in his own esteem as he thought that he was a soldier in an army commanded by such generals. He had no longer any opinion excepting that of his superior. It was not so easy to persuade, subjugate, and convince the judge.
“But,” objected the latter, “you saw Guespin’s countenance?”
“Ah, what matters the countenance﻿—what does that prove? Don’t we know if you and I were arrested tomorrow on a terrible charge, what our bearing would be?”
M. Domini gave a significant start; this hypothesis scarcely pleased him.
“And yet you and I are familiar with the machinery of justice. When I arrested Lanscot, the poor servant in the Rue Marignan, his first words were: ‘Come on, my account is good.’ The morning that Papa Tabaret and I took the Viscount de Commarin as he was getting out of bed, on the accusation of having murdered the widow Lerouge, he cried: ‘I am lost.’ Yet neither of them were guilty; but both of them, the viscount and the valet, equal before the terror of a possible mistake of justice, and running over in their thoughts the charges which would be brought against them, had a moment of overwhelming discouragement.”
“But such discouragement does not last two days,” said M. Domini.
M. Lecoq did not answer this; he went on, growing more animated as he proceeded.
“You and I have seen enough prisoners to know how deceitful appearances are, and how little they are to be trusted. It would be foolish to base a theory upon a prisoner’s bearing. He who talked about ‘the cry of innocence’ was an idiot, just as the man was who prated about the ‘pale stupor’ of guilt. Neither crime nor virtue have, unhappily, any especial countenance. The Simon girl, who was accused of having killed her father, absolutely refused to answer any questions for twenty-two days; on the twenty-third, the murderer was caught. As to the Sylvain affair﻿—”
M. Domini rapped lightly on his desk to check the detective. As a man, the judge held too obstinately to his opinions; as a magistrate he was equally obstinate, but was at the same time ready to make any sacrifice of his self-esteem if the voice of duty prompted it. M.

===Chinese===

他平生从未听闻任何同行能以如此澎湃的热忱与威严侃侃而谈；他做梦也想不到世间竟有这等雄辩口才，整个人不由得昂首挺胸，仿佛在座所有人脸上流露出的钦佩倾倒也分了一抹光晕照耀在自己身上一般。一想到自己也是由这等绝世统帅所统领的大军中的一名小卒，他心头的自豪油然而生。自此，除了这位上峰的断言，他再也没有任何自己的成见了。然而，要想彻底说服、降伏并感化那位顽固的法官，可绝非这般轻而易举。
“但是，”法官反诘道，“你方才也亲眼见到了盖斯潘那副神态嘴脸？”
“哈，神态嘴脸能顶什么用——那能证明什么？倘若明天你我二人突然因一项弥天大罪被捕归案，你我难道能预知自己会表现出何等形容仪态？”
多米尼先生闻言神色一震，这番打比方的假设显然令他颇为不悦。
“更何况你我皆深谙司法机器的运转机理。当年我去马里尼昂街缉捕那个可怜的仆人朗斯科特时，他的头一句话就是：‘来吧，反正我这辈子算交代了。’想当年我和塔巴雷老爹趁着科马兰子爵刚从床上下地便以谋杀勒鲁热遗孀的罪名将他拿办时，他失声痛哭道：‘我全完了。’然而他们两个全无罪过；可无论是贵为子爵还是身为仆役，在司法审判可能酿成冤假错案的可怕威压面前，在脑中飞速盘算着即将扣在自己头上的如山罪名时，人人皆会在一瞬间陷入彻底击垮内心的万念俱灰。”
“但那种心灰意冷绝不会持续两天之久，”多米尼先生反驳道。
勒柯克先生没有直接驳斥这句质疑；他继续往下说，言辞随着情绪的激昂愈发神采飞扬。
“你我审讯过的案犯不知凡几，深知外表是何等善于作伪、何等不足凭信。若是将案情推论建立在犯人的神色仪态上，无异于痴人说梦。那些高谈阔论什么‘无辜者发自肺腑的呐喊’的文人全是不知天高地厚的蠢才，正如那些夸夸其谈什么罪恶必伴随‘惨白惊恐’的家伙一样可笑。不幸的是，罪恶与美德皆没有专属于自己的特殊嘴脸。当年被控弑父的西蒙家闺女，足足咬紧牙关坚持了22天拒绝回答任何提问；直到第23天，真正的凶手才落网归案。至于西尔万一案——”
多米尼先生在书案上轻轻叩击了几下，打断了侦探的长篇大论。就个人性情而言，这位法官对自己的定见固执到了极点；作为司法官员，他固然同样固执己见，但只要职责的号召摆在眼前，他也甘愿做出牺牲个人颜面尊严的一切退让。

===Original===

Lecoq’s arguments had not shaken his convictions, but they imposed on him the duty of informing himself at once, and to either conquer the detective or avow himself conquered.
“You seem to be pleading,” said he to M. Lecoq. “There is no need of that here. We are not counsel and judge; the same honorable intentions animate us both. Each, in his sphere, is searching after the truth. You think you see it shining where I only discern clouds; and you may be mistaken as well as I.”
Then by an act of heroism, he condescended to add:
“What do you think I ought to do?”
The judge was at least rewarded for the effort he made by approving glances from M. Plantat and the doctor. But M. Lecoq did not hasten to respond; he had many weighty reasons to advance; that, he saw, was not what was necessary. He ought to present the facts, there and at once, and produce one of those proofs which can be touched with the finger. How should he do it? His active mind searched eagerly for such a proof.
“Well?” insisted M. Domini.
“Ah,” cried the detective. “Why can’t I ask Guespin two or three questions?”
The judge frowned; the suggestion seemed to him rather presumptuous. It is formally laid down that the questioning of the accused should be done in secret, and by the judge alone, aided by his clerk. On the other hand it is decided, that after he has once been interrogated he may be confronted with witnesses. There are, besides, exceptions in favor of the members of the police force. M. Domini reflected whether there were any precedents to apply to the case.
“I don’t know,” he answered at last, “to what point the law permits me to consent to what you ask. However, as I am convinced the interests of truth outweigh all rules, I shall take it on myself to let you question Guespin.”
He rang; a bailiff appeared.
“Has Guespin been carried back to prison?”
“Not yet, Monsieur.”
“So much the better; have him brought in here.”
M. Lecoq was beside himself with joy; he had not hoped to achieve such a victory over one so determined as M. Domini.
“He will speak now,” said he, so full of confidence that his eyes shone, and he forgot the portrait of the dear defunct, “for I have three means of unloosening his tongue, one of which is sure to succeed. But before he comes I should like to know one thing. Do you know whether Trémorel saw Jenny after Sauvresy’s death?”
“Jenny?” asked M.

===Chinese===

勒柯克先生的一番雄辩虽然未能彻底撼动他的成见，但却让他深感自己有恪尽职守立刻彻查的重任，要么将这名侦探彻底驳倒，要么就坦承自己甘拜下风。
“听您的口气宛如在法庭辩护，”他对勒柯克先生说道，“咱们眼下大可不必如此。咱们并非辩护律师与审判法官的关系；同样的崇高初衷激励着你我双方。各自身处其位，我们都在追寻真理真相。在您自以为看到了真理光芒万丈之处，我却只瞧见了重重迷雾；或许您也可能同我一样犯错。”
随后，出于一种近乎英勇的胸襟，他屈尊补充了一句：
“依您之见，我现在应当如何处置？”
法官做出的这番巨大克制退让，至少赢得了普朗塔先生与医生投来的赞许目光。然而勒柯克先生并未急于应答；他固然有成堆举足轻重的论据可以陈述，但他深知此刻需要的不是空谈大道理。他必须当机立断、当场摆出铁一般的客观事实，拿出哪怕用手指都能触碰得到的铁证。他该如何着手？他那机敏过人的大脑在飞速搜寻着这样一份证据。
“如何？”多米尼先生追问道。
“哈，”侦探叫道，“为何不容我当面盘问盖斯潘两三个问题呢？”
法官皱起了眉头；这项提议在他看来未免有些过于越权放肆。司法典章白纸黑字严格规定，对嫌犯的审讯必须秘密进行，且只能由法官本人在书记员的襄助下独立主审。另一方面司法也裁定，嫌犯一经初次审讯后，便可与证人对质。此外，针对警界刑侦人员亦有特例豁免权。多米尼先生反复权衡是否有先例可循。
“我不知道，”他最终答道，“法理律条究竟在多大程度上允许我批准您的请求。不过，既然我深信探求真相的利益凌驾于一切条条框框之上，我愿破例担起干系，由您亲自盘问盖斯潘。”
他按响了桌铃；一名法警应声入内。
“盖斯潘押解回牢房了吗？”
“回大人，还没有。”
“那太好了；立刻把他带到这儿来。”
勒柯克先生欣喜若狂；他万万没想到自己竟能在多米尼先生这样铁石心肠之人身上取得如此重大的胜利。
“他这回肯开口了，”他信心百倍地说道，双眼炯炯放光，甚至把亡妻小像都忘到了脑后，“因为我手头握有3种撬开他嘴巴的手段，其中任何一种都必定奏效。但在他被押来之前，我想先弄清楚一件事。您可知晓索夫勒齐死后，特雷莫雷尔私下里是否还见过珍妮？”
“珍妮？”普朗塔先生略感诧异地反问道。

===Original===

Plantat, a little surprised.
“Yes.”
“Certainly he did.”
“Several times?”
“Pretty often. After the scene at the Belle Image the poor girl plunged into terrible dissipation. Whether she was smitten with remorse, or understood that it was her conduct which had killed Sauvresy, or suspected the crime, I don’t know. She began, however, to drink furiously, falling lower and lower every week﻿—”
“And the count really consented to see her again?”
“He was forced to do so; she tormented him, and he was afraid of her. When she had spent all her money she sent to him for more, and he gave it. Once he refused; and that very evening she went to him the worse for wine, and he had the greatest difficulty in the world to send her away again. In short, she knew what his relations with Madame Sauvresy had been, and she threatened him; it was a regular blackmailing operation. He told me all about the trouble she gave him, and added that he would not be able to get rid of her without shutting her up, which he could not bring himself to do.”
“How long ago was their last interview?”
“Why,” answered the doctor, “not three weeks ago, when I had a consultation at Melun, I saw the count and this demoiselle at a hotel window; when he saw me he suddenly drew back.”
“Then,” said the detective, “there is no longer any doubt﻿—”
He stopped. Guespin came in between two gendarmes.
The unhappy gardener had aged twenty years in twenty-four hours. His eyes were haggard, his dry lips were bordered with foam.
“Let us see,” said the judge. “Have you changed your mind about speaking?”
The prisoner did not answer.
“Have you decided to tell us about yourself?”
Guespin’s rage made him tremble from head to foot, and his eyes became fiery.
“Speak!” said he hoarsely. “Why should I?”
He added with the gesture of a desperate man who abandons himself, renounces all struggling and all hope:
“What have I done to you, my God, that you torture me this way? What do you want me to say? That I did this crime﻿—is that what you want? Well, then﻿—yes﻿—it was I. Now you are satisfied. Now cut my head off, and do it quick﻿—for I don’t want to suffer any longer.”
A mournful silence welcomed Guespin’s declaration. What, he confessed it!
M. Domini had at least the good taste not to exult; he kept still, and yet this avowal surprised him beyond all expression.
M.

===Chinese===

“正是。”
“毫无疑问，他见过。”
“见过许多回？”
“颇为频繁。自打在‘美景客栈’闹出那一幕风波之后，那可怜的姑娘便一头扎进了穷奢极欲的放浪生活之中。究竟是因为良心受到谴责、明白了是自己的所作所为害死了索夫勒齐，还是暗中怀疑到了这桩罪孽，我不得而知。然而她自此开始酗酒如命，每周都在一步步堕入更深的深渊——”
“而伯爵居然当真肯再次接见她？”
“他是被逼无奈；那女人成天缠磨要挟他，他打心眼里惧怕她。一旦手头的钱挥霍一空，她便差人去向他索要，而他次次照给不误。有一次他拒不给钱；就在当天傍晚，她借着酒劲醉醺醺地找上门来，他费了九牛二虎之力才好不容易把她打发走。简而言之，她对特雷莫雷尔与索夫勒齐夫人当年的奸情心知肚明，借此肆意勒索要挟；这完全是家常便饭的敲诈勒索勾当。伯爵曾向我大倒苦水倾诉过她带来的无穷烦恼，还补充说除非把她送进疯人院关起来，否则永远摆脱不掉她的纠缠，可他又实在狠不下心来这么做。”
“他们最近一次私会是在多久之前？”
“哎呀，”医生答道，“不过3周之前，我去默伦出诊会诊时，曾亲眼瞧见伯爵和那位年轻女子站在一家旅舍的窗口前；对方一瞅见我，便冷不丁猛地缩回了身子。”
“既然如此，”侦探说道，“一切便再无半分疑虑了——”
他话音一顿。两名宪兵押解着盖斯潘走了进来。
这个不幸的园丁在短短24小时里仿佛苍老了20岁。他双目空洞憔悴，干瘪的嘴角挂着白沫。
“咱们且来看看，”法官开口道，“你如今改变主意肯招认了吗？”
囚犯闭口不答。
“你决定向咱们交代你自己的行踪了吗？”
盖斯潘在狂怒之下浑身瑟瑟发抖，眼眸中几乎要喷出怒火。
“招认！”他声音沙哑粗粝地吼道，“我凭什么招认？”
他做出了一个破罐子破摔、彻底放弃抵抗与希望的绝望手势，补充道：
“老天爷啊，我到底造了什么孽，你们非要这般严刑折磨我？你们究竟想听我说什么？承认这案子是我干的——你们要听的就是这个吗？行啊——没错——人就是我杀的！这下你们该称心如意了吧！现在就把我的脑袋砍下来吧，动手痛快点——因为我再也忍受不了这种折磨了！”
一阵令人窒息的凄凉沉寂笼罩了公事房，迎接着盖斯潘这番决绝的供白。什么，他居然当场招供了！
多米尼先生至少尚存体面风度，并未流露出丝毫得色；他一言不发，然而这份招供带给他的震惊简直难以言表。

===Original===

Lecoq alone, although surprised, was not absolutely put out of countenance. He approached Guespin and tapping him on the shoulder, said in a paternal tone:
“Come, comrade, what you are telling us is absurd. Do you think the judge has any secret grudge against you? No, eh? Do you suppose I am interested to have you guillotined? Not at all. A crime has been committed, and we are trying to find the assassin. If you are innocent, help us to find the man who isn’t: What were you doing from Wednesday evening till Thursday morning?”
But Guespin persisted in his ferocious and stupid obstinacy.
“I’ve said what I have to say,” said he.
M. Lecoq changed his tone to one of severity, stepping back to watch the effect he was about to produce upon Guespin.
“You haven’t any right to hold your tongue. And even if you do, you fool, the police know everything. Your master sent you on an errand, didn’t he, on Wednesday night; what did he give you? A one-thousand-franc note?”
The prisoner looked at M. Lecoq in speechless amazement.
“No,” he stammered. “It was a five-hundred-franc note.”
The detective, like all great artists in a critical scene, was really moved. His surprising genius for investigation had just inspired him with a bold stroke, which, if it succeeded, would assure him the victory.
“Now,” said he, “tell me the woman’s name.”
“I don’t know.”
“You are only a fool then. She is short, isn’t she, quite pretty, brown and pale, with very large eyes?”
“You know her, then?” said Guespin, in a voice trembling with emotion.
“Yes, comrade, and if you want to know her name, to put in your prayers, she is called﻿—Jenny.”
Men who are really able in some specialty, whatever it may be, never uselessly abuse their superiority; their satisfaction at seeing it recognized is sufficient reward. M. Lecoq softly enjoyed his triumph, while his hearers wondered at his perspicacity. A rapid chain of reasoning had shown him not only Trémorel’s thoughts, but also the means he had employed to accomplish his purpose.
Guespin’s astonishment soon changed to anger. He asked himself how this man could have been informed of things which he had every reason to believe were secret. Lecoq continued:
“Since I have told you the woman’s name, tell me now, how and why the count gave you a five-hundred-franc note.”
“It was just as I was going out.

===Chinese===

唯有勒柯克先生一人虽然略感诧异，神色却未见丝毫慌乱。他走到盖斯潘跟前，像慈父般拍了拍他的肩膀，用温和的语气说道：
“好啦，伙计，你方才这番胡话简直荒谬透顶。你当真以为法官大人跟你有什么私仇宿怨吗？没有吧？你以为我很乐意看到你被送上断头台吗？绝非如此。眼下发生了一桩凶案，我们正在竭力缉拿真正的杀人犯。如果你是无辜的，就该帮我们把那个丧尽天良的恶徒揪出来：从星期三傍晚直到星期四清晨，你究竟都在干些什么？”
然而盖斯潘依然死咬着那份凶狠而愚蠢的执拗不放。
“该说的话我都说尽了，”他说道。
勒柯克先生的面孔骤然变得严厉起来，他后退一步，冷眼旁观着自己接下来的话在盖斯潘身上所激起的反应。
“你根本没有任何权利咬死牙关不招！即便你守口如瓶，你这个蠢货，警署对一切也早已了如指掌。你家主人在星期三夜里差遣你出门办了一趟差事，对不对？他当时塞给了你什么？一张1000法郎的纸钞？”
囚犯目瞪口呆地望着勒柯克先生，惊得哑口无言。
“不，”他结结巴巴地嘟囔道，“那是一张500法郎的纸钞。”
侦探宛如身处惊心动魄高潮戏份中的艺术巨匠，内心亦掀起了波澜。他那超凡脱俗的刑侦天才，刚刚启发他走出了一记险着妙棋；此计一旦奏效，胜利便唾手可得。
“现在，”他说道，“把那个女人的名字告诉我。”
“我不知道。”
“那你可真是个不折不扣的大傻瓜。她身材娇小玲珑，颇有几分姿色，皮肤白皙中透着微褐，生着一双硕大无比的眼睛，对不对？”
“你……你居然认识她？”盖斯潘声音剧烈颤抖着问道。
“不错，伙计；若是你想在祈祷时念诵她的芳名，她叫——珍妮。”
凡是在某一专门行当真正具备超卓才干的人，无论身处何等领域，向来不屑于滥用自己的绝对优势；能亲眼目睹旁人承认这份卓绝，对他们而言便已是莫大的奖赏。勒柯克先生静静体味着胜利的喜悦，而满屋聆听的众人无不对其惊世骇俗的洞察力叹为观止。一条环环相扣的飞速推理链条，不仅向他彻底揭示了特雷莫雷尔的腹中算盘，甚至连其达成阴谋所动用的一招一式都展现无遗。
盖斯潘的惊骇转瞬化作了羞愤。他百思不得其解，眼前这个男人究竟是通过何种手腕，探知了这些他深信绝无第三人知晓的隐秘之事。勒柯克继续追问道：
“既然我已经连那女人的名字都替你抖搂出来了，现在老实交代吧，伯爵究竟为何给你那张500法郎纸币，又是如何交到你手里的？”
“那正好是在我刚准备动身离家的当口。

===Original===

The count had no change, and did not want to send me to Orcival for it. I was to bring back the rest.”
“And why didn’t you rejoin your companions at the wedding in the Batignolles?”
No answer.
“What was the errand which you were to do for the count?”
Guespin hesitated. His eyes wandered from one to another of those present, and he seemed to discover an ironical expression on all the faces. It occurred to him that they were making sport of him, and had set a snare into which he had fallen. A great despair took possession of him.
“Ah,” cried he, addressing M. Lecoq, “you have deceived me. You have been lying so as to find out the truth. I have been such a fool as to answer you, and you are going to turn it all against me.”
“What? Are you going to talk nonsense again?”
“No, but I see just how it is, and you won’t catch me again! Now I’d rather die than say a word.”
The detective tried to reassure him; but he added:
“Besides, I’m as sly as you; I’ve told you nothing but lies.”
This sudden whim surprised no one. Some prisoners intrench themselves behind a system of defence, and nothing can divert them from it; others vary with each new question, denying what they have just affirmed, and constantly inventing some new absurdity which anon they reject again. M. Lecoq tried in vain to draw Guespin from his silence; M. Domini made the same attempt, and also failed; to all questions he only answered, “I don’t know.”
At last the detective waxed impatient.
“See here,” said he to Guespin, “I took you for a young man of sense, and you are only an ass. Do you imagine that we don’t know anything? Listen: On the night of Madame Denis’s wedding, you were getting ready to go off with your comrades, and had just borrowed twenty francs from the valet, when the count called you. He made you promise absolute secrecy (a promise which, to do you justice, you kept); he told you to leave the other servants at the station and go to Vulcan’s Forges, where you were to buy for him a hammer, a file, a chisel, and a dirk; these you were to carry to a certain woman. Then he gave you this famous five-hundred-franc note, telling you to bring him back the change when you returned next day. Isn’t that so?”
An affirmative response glistened in the prisoner’s eyes; still, he answered, “I don’t recollect it.”
“Now,” pursued M. Lecoq, “I’m going to tell you what happened afterwards.

===Chinese===

伯爵手头没有零钱，又不想差我去奥尔西瓦尔破钱。他吩咐我把找零剩下的钱带回来交还给他。”
“那为什么你没有去巴蒂尼奥勒赶赴婚礼酒席与同伴会合？”
没有任何回答。
“伯爵打发你办的究竟是桩什么差事？”
盖斯潘迟疑不决。他的目光从在座每一个人脸上扫过，似乎在所有人的神情中都捕捉到了嘲讽讥诮的意味。他蓦然觉得这群人纯粹是在拿他寻开心，给他布下了一个让他越陷越深的陷阱。一阵巨大的绝望骤然攫住了他的心神。
“啊，”他指着勒柯克先生撕心裂肺地吼道，“你骗了我！你满嘴谎言套我的真话！我居然蠢到老老实实回答你的话，你回头全要把这些当作置我于死地的把柄！”
“怎么？你又要开始胡言乱语了吗？”
“不，我彻底看清你们的把戏了，你们休想再套出我一个字！现在我宁死也绝不再吐露半句！”
侦探试图安抚他的情绪；可他又歇斯底里地补充道：
“再者说，我也跟你们一样精明狡猾；我刚才说的全是一派胡言，全是我编造的谎话！”
这突如其来的反复无常并未出乎在座任何人的意料。有的犯人死死筑起一套自以为坚不可摧的抗辩防线，任凭风吹雨打绝不退让半步；另一些人则随着每一次盘问变幻无常，前脚刚承认后脚就矢口否认，不断编造荒唐怪诞的新说辞，旋即又亲手推翻。勒柯克先生百般诱导试图打破盖斯潘的死寂沉默，终归徒劳无功；多米尼先生亦作了相同的尝试，同样碰了一鼻子灰；面对连珠炮般的质问，犯人翻来覆去只有一句：“我不知道。”
侦探的耐性终于彻底耗尽了。
“听着，”他对盖斯潘呵斥道，“我原以为你是个明事理的小伙子，没成想你蠢得像头驴。你当真以为我们对案情两眼一抹黑吗？你给我听好了：就在德尼夫人举行婚礼的那个傍晚，你正准备跟同伴们一起动身进城，刚从男仆手里借了20法郎，伯爵突然把你叫了去。他逼你起誓严守秘密（凭良心说，你确实做到了守口如瓶）；他嘱咐你在火车站甩掉其他仆人，径直赶往‘火神锻造坊’，替他采买一把铁锤、一把锉刀、一柄平凿和一柄匕首短剑；买齐之后，送到某个女人手上。随后他递给你那张人尽皆知的500法郎纸币，吩咐你次日回府时把找零剩下的钱交还给他。事情难道不是这样吗？”
囚犯的双眼闪过一丝肯定的光芒；但他嘴上依然硬撑着答道：“我不记得了。”
“现在，”勒柯克先生继续说道，“我再告诉你后来究竟发生了什么。

===Original===

You drank something and got tipsy, and in short spent a part of the change of the note. That explains your fright when you were seized yesterday morning, before anybody said a word to you. You thought you were being arrested for spending that money. Then, when you learned that the count had been murdered during the night, recollecting that on the evening before you had bought all kinds of instruments of theft and murder, and that you didn’t know either the address or the name of the woman to whom you gave up the package, convinced that if you explained the source of the money found in your pocket, you would not be believed﻿—then, instead of thinking of the means to prove your innocence, you became afraid, and thought you would save yourself by holding your tongue.”
The prisoner’s countenance visibly changed; his nerves relaxed; his tight lips fell apart; his mind opened itself to hope. But he still resisted.
“Do with me as you like,” said he.
“Eh! What should we do with such a fool as you?” cried M. Lecoq angrily. “I begin to think you are a rascal too. A decent fellow would see that we wanted to get him out of a scrape, and he’d tell us the truth. You are prolonging your imprisonment by your own will. You’d better learn that the greatest shrewdness consists in telling the truth. A last time, will you answer?”
Guespin shook his head; no.
“Go back to prison, then; since it pleases you,” concluded the detective. He looked at the judge for his approval, and added:
“Gendarmes, remove the prisoner.”
The judge’s last doubt was dissipated like the mist before the sun. He was, to tell the truth, a little uneasy at having treated the detective so rudely; and he tried to repair it as much as he could.
“You are an able man, Monsieur Lecoq,” said he. “Without speaking of your clearsightedness, which is so prompt as to seem almost like second sight, your examination just now was a masterpiece of its kind. Receive my congratulations, to say nothing of the reward which I propose to recommend in your favor to your chiefs.”
The detective at these compliments cast down his eyes with the abashed air of a virgin.

===Chinese===

你喝了些酒，喝得酩酊大醉，简而言之把找零剩下的钱挥霍掉了一部分。这就解释了为什么昨天早晨你甚至还没等任何人开口审问，一被擒获便吓得面无人色。你当时还以为自己是因为私吞挪用了那笔钱才遭拘捕的。紧接着，当你获悉伯爵竟然在一夜之间惨遭横祸，回想起自己头天晚上刚刚买齐了全套行窃杀人的凶器，而你连那个收货女人的住址和姓名皆一无所知；你认定即便自己辩白衣兜里现钱的来路也绝不会有人相信——于是，你非但没有设法自证清白，反而吓破了胆，妄图靠咬死牙关守口如瓶来保全性命。”
囚犯的容色肉眼可见地变了；紧绷的神经松弛了下来；紧抿的嘴唇微微张开；心扉向希望重新敞开了一丝缝隙。但他依然在负隅顽抗。
“你们爱怎么处置我就怎么处置吧，”他说道。
“呸！谁爱搭理你这种蠢驴！”勒柯克先生怒斥道，“我看你不仅是个蠢货，八成还是个无赖。但凡是个正经体面人，早该看出我们是在千方百计拉你出泥潭，早该向我们坦白全盘真相了。你纯粹是拿自己的自由开玩笑，硬要把自己往牢里多关几天。你最好明白，世间最高明的精明，莫过于老老实实交代真情。最后问你一次，你答还是不答？”
盖斯潘摇了摇头；不肯。
“那就回牢房去吧；既然你乐意蹲大狱，”侦探下了定论。他望向法官征求许可，随后补充道：
“宪兵，把犯人带下去。”
法官心头最后的疑虑如晨雾迎骄阳般烟消云散。说句实在话，一想到自己先前对侦探态度如此生硬粗暴，他心头颇感过意不去，极力试图加以弥补。
“您真是一位了不起的大才，勒柯克先生，”法官说道，“且不说您那敏锐绝伦、宛如未卜先知般的洞察力，单凭您方才这番盘诘审问，便堪称该领域的传世杰作。请接受我的由衷祝贺，更不用说我随后定会向您的上峰极力保荐、为您请功嘉奖。”
面对这番盛赞，侦探垂下双眸，装出一副宛如待字闺中纯情少女般羞怯腼腆的模样。

===Original===

He looked tenderly at the dear defunct’s portrait, and doubtless said to it:
“At last, darling, we have defeated him﻿—this austere judge who so heartily detests the force of which we are the brightest ornament, makes his apologies; he recognizes and applauds our services.”
He answered aloud:
“I can only accept half of your eulogies, Monsieur; permit me to offer the other half to my friend Monsieur Plantat.”
M. Plantat tried to protest.
“Oh,” said he, “only for some bits of information! You would have ferreted out the truth without me all the same.”
The judge arose and graciously, but not without effort, extended his hand to M. Lecoq, who respectfully pressed it.
“You have spared me,” said the judge, “a great remorse. Guespin’s innocence would surely sooner or later have been recognized; but the idea of having imprisoned an innocent man and harassed him with my interrogatories, would have disturbed my sleep and tormented my conscience for a long time.”
“God knows this poor Guespin is not an interesting youth,” returned the detective. “I should be disposed to press him hard were I not certain that he’s half a fool.”
M. Domini gave a start.
“I shall discharge him this very day,” said he, “this very hour.”
“It will be an act of charity,” said M. Lecoq; “but confound his obstinacy; it was so easy for him to simplify my task. I might be able, by the aid of chance, to collect the principal facts﻿—the errand, and a woman being mixed up in the affair; but as I’m no magician, I couldn’t guess all the details. How is Jenny mixed up in this affair? Is she an accomplice, or has she only been made to play an ignorant part in it? Where did she meet Guespin and whither did she lead him? It is clear that she made the poor fellow tipsy so as to prevent his going to the Batignolles. Trémorel must have told her some false story﻿—but what?”
“I don’t think Trémorel troubled his head about so small a matter,” said M. Plantat. “He gave Guespin and Jenny some task, without explaining it at all.”
M. Lecoq reflected a moment.
“Perhaps you are right. But Jenny must have had special orders to prevent Guespin from putting in an alibi.”
“But,” said M. Domini, “Jenny will explain it all to us.”
“That is what I rely on; and I hope that within forty-eight hours I shall have found her and brought her safely to Corbeil.”

===Chinese===

他含情脉脉地凝视着糖盒上亲爱亡妻的肖像，想必正在心底对她喃喃自语：
“亲爱的，咱们终于彻底降伏了他——这位平日里对咱们警界精英深恶痛绝的严苛法官，如今终于低头谢罪了；他终于承认并赞赏咱们的功劳了。”
他随即朗声回答道：
“大人过奖了，鄙人只能愧领一半的赞誉；请容许鄙人将另一半转赠给我的挚友普朗塔先生。”
普朗塔先生连忙摆手推辞。
“哎呀，”他说道，“我不过是提供了几条零碎情报而已！即便没有我，您也照样能把真相查个水落石出。”
法官站起身来，虽略显吃力却依然优雅地向勒柯克先生伸出手去，后者恭敬地握了握。
“您替我免除了一桩莫大的良心谴责，”法官说道，“盖斯潘的清白早晚必会昭雪；但一想到自己竟曾将一名无辜平民打入死牢、以严词厉色连番逼供，这势必会长久扰乱我的清梦、拷问折磨我的良知。”
“天晓得这个可怜的盖斯潘绝非什么讨喜招人怜爱的小伙子，”侦探回应道，“若不是断定他脑子缺根弦是个半傻子，我非得狠狠治治他不可。”
多米尼先生身形一动。
“我今天就签署释放令将他放归自由，”他说道，“就在此时此刻。”
“这不啻为一桩莫大的善举功德，”勒柯克先生说道，“可真该死于他的冥顽不灵；他原本只需动动嘴皮子就能替我省下多少气力。借着机缘巧合，我固然能拼凑出大体的脉络轮廓——跑腿差事，以及有个女人卷入其中；但我终究不是未卜先知的魔术师，不可能凭空猜透每一处细节。珍妮究竟是如何卷入这场血案的？她是共犯同谋，还是仅仅充当了一个蒙在鼓里的无知棋子？她是在何处同盖斯潘碰头的，又把他带往了何方？很显然，她故意将那倒霉蛋灌得酩酊大醉，就是为了阻止他去巴蒂尼奥勒赴宴。特雷莫雷尔必定编造了什么弥天大谎哄骗了她——但究竟是什么谎言呢？”
“我倒觉得特雷莫雷尔根本没在这些细枝末节上多费心思，”普朗塔先生说道，“他吩咐盖斯潘与珍妮各自办差，根本没向他们作任何解释。”
勒柯克先生沉吟片刻。
“也许您所言极是。但珍妮必定接到了死命令，必须千方百计破坏盖斯潘的不在场证明。”
“不过，”多米尼先生插话道，“珍妮自会向咱们把一切交代清楚的。”
“那正是我全盘指望的依仗；我深信在48小时之内，我必定能将她缉拿归案，安然押送回科尔贝伊。”

===Original===

He rose at these words, took his cane and hat, and turning to the judge, said:
“Before retiring﻿—”
“Yes, I know,” interrupted M. Domini, “you want a warrant to arrest Hector de Trémorel.”
“I do, as you are now of my opinion that he is still alive.”
“I am sure of it.”
M. Domini opened his portfolio and wrote off a warrant as follows:
“By the law:
“We, judge of instruction of the first tribunal, etc., considering articles 91 and 94 of the code of criminal instruction, command and ordain to all the agents of the police to arrest, in conformity with the law, one Hector de Trémorel, etc.”
When he had finished, he said:
“Here it is, and may you succeed in speedily finding this great criminal.”
“Oh, *he’ll* find him,” cried the Corbeil policeman.
“I hope so, at least. As to how I shall go to work, I don’t know yet. I will arrange my plan of battle tonight.”
The detective then took leave of M. Domini and retired, followed by M. Plantat. The doctor remained with the judge to make arrangements for Sauvresy’s exhumation.
M. Lecoq was just leaving the courthouse when he felt himself pulled by the arm. He turned and found that it was Goulard who came to beg his favor and to ask him to take him along, persuaded that after having served under so great a captain he must inevitably become a famous man himself. M. Lecoq had some difficulty in getting rid of him; but he at length found himself alone in the street with the old justice of the peace.
“It is late,” said the latter. “Would it be agreeable to you to partake of another modest dinner with me, and accept my cordial hospitality?”
“I am chagrined to be obliged to refuse you,” replied M. Lecoq. “But I ought to be in Paris this evening.”
“But I﻿—in fact, I﻿—was very anxious to talk to you﻿—about﻿—”
“About Mademoiselle Laurence?”
“Yes; I have a plan, and if you would help me﻿—”
M. Lecoq affectionately pressed his friend’s hand.
“I have only known you a few hours,” said he, “and yet I am as devoted to you as I would be to an old friend. All that is humanly possible for me to do to serve you, I shall certainly do.”
“But where shall I see you? They expect me today at Orcival.”
“Very well; tomorrow morning at nine, at my rooms. No. — Rue Montmartre.”
“A thousand thanks; I shall be there.”
When they had reached the Belle Image they separated.

===Chinese===

说完这番话，他站起身来，抄起手杖与礼帽，转身向法官说道：
“在告退之前——”
“是的，我明白，”多米尼先生打断道，“您需要一张缉捕埃克托·德·特雷莫雷尔的拘捕通缉令。”
“正是，既然大人如今已赞同我的判断，确信他尚在人世。”
“我深信不疑。”
多米尼先生翻开公文夹，一气呵成拟好了一纸通缉令，文书如下：
“奉法之名：
“本预审法官，初审法院法官，等等，依照刑事预审法典第91条与第94条之规定，特训令全权委派全体治安警吏，依法捉拿埃克托·德·特雷莫雷尔归案，等等。”
书毕，他开口道：
“文书在此，但愿您能旗开得胜，尽早将这名滔天恶犯捉拿归案。”
“噢，他老人家定能将犯人手到擒来！”科尔贝伊警员高声附和道。
“但愿如此。至于究竟如何着手收网，我眼下尚未胸有成竹。今晚我会通盘拟定作战方略。”
侦探随即向多米尼先生告退离去，普朗塔先生紧随其后。医生则留下来同法官商讨开棺起掘索夫勒齐骸骨的具体事宜。
勒柯克先生刚步出法院大门，忽觉有人从身后拽住了自己的胳膊。他回首一瞧，正是古拉尔眼巴巴地跑来讨好求情，恳求能跟随在他鞍前马后，深信只要能追随在这等绝世名将麾下效力，自己日后也必能飞黄腾达名扬四海。勒柯克好不容易才摆脱了他的纠缠，终于得以同老治安法官二人漫步在街头。
“时辰不早了，”老法官说道，“不知您是否肯屈尊再次同我共进一顿便餐，赏光接纳我诚挚的款待呢？”
“鄙人深感遗憾，不得不辜负您的美意，”勒柯克先生答道，“我今晚必须赶回巴黎。”
“可是我——说实话，我——极渴望同您谈谈——谈谈关于——”
“关于洛朗斯小姐？”
“正是；我心中有个打算，倘若您能助我一臂之力——”
勒柯克先生深情地握紧了挚友的手。
“我与您相识不过短短几个钟头，”他说道，“然而我对您的赤诚，丝毫不亚于多年莫逆之交。凡是人力所能及、能为您效劳之处，鄙人必定万死不辞。”
“可我该去何处寻您呢？奥尔西瓦尔今天正眼巴巴盼着我回去呢。”
“好办；明早9点整，在我下榻的寓所碰头。蒙马特街某号。”
“万分感谢；我定会准时赴约。”
两人行至‘美景客栈’，便拱手作别。

## XXIV / 第二十四章
===Original===

Nine o’clock had just struck in the belfry of the church of St. Eustache, when M. Plantat reached Rue Montmartre, and entered the house bearing the number which M. Lecoq had given him.
“Monsieur Lecoq?” said he to an old woman who was engaged in getting breakfast for three large cats which were mewing around her. The woman scanned him with a surprised and suspicious air. M. Plantat, when he was dressed up, had much more the appearance of a fine old gentleman than of a country attorney; and though the detective received many visits from all sorts of people, it was rarely that the denizens of the Faubourg Saint Germaine rung his bell.
“Monsieur Lecoq’s apartments,” answered the old woman, “are on the third story, the door facing the stairs.”
The justice of the peace slowly ascended the narrow, ill-lighted staircase, which in its dark corners was almost dangerous. He was thinking of the strange step he was about to take. An idea had occurred to him, but he did not know whether it were practicable, and at all events he needed the aid and advice of the detective. He was forced to disclose his most secret thoughts, as it were, to confess himself; and his heart beat fast. The door opposite the staircase on the third story was not like other doors; it was of plain oak, thick, without mouldings, and fastened with iron bars. It would have looked like a prison door had not its sombreness been lightened by a heavily colored engraving of a cock crowing, with the legend “Always Vigilant.” Had the detective put his coat of arms up there? Was it not more likely that one of his men had done it? After examining the door more than a minute, and hesitating like a youth before his beloved’s gate, he rang the bell. A creaking of locks responded, and through the narrow bars of the peephole he saw the hairy face of an old crone.
“What do you want?” said the woman, in a deep, bass voice.
“Monsieur Lecoq.”
“What do you want of him?”
“He made an appointment with me for this morning.”
“Your name and business?”
“Monsieur Plantat, justice of the peace at Orcival.”
“All right. Wait.”
The peephole was closed and the old man waited.
“*Peste!*” growled he. “Everybody can’t get in here, it seems.” Hardly had this reflection passed through his mind when the door opened with a noise as of chains and locks.

===Chinese===

圣厄斯塔什教堂钟楼的钟声刚刚敲过9点，普朗塔先生便赶到了蒙马特街，走进了勒柯克先生留给他的那个门牌号的宅子。
“勒柯克先生在吗？”他向一位正忙着给围着她喵喵叫的3只大猫喂早饭的老妇人打听道。妇人带着既惊讶又警惕的神色上下打量着他。盛装打扮之下的普朗塔先生，看起来更像一位养尊处优的体面绅士，而非乡下的治安法官；尽管这位名侦探平素接待来自三教九流的各色访客，但圣日耳曼贵族区的达官贵人却极少扣响他的门环。
“勒柯克先生的寓所，”老妇人答道，“在3楼，楼梯正对面的那扇门便是。”
老治安法官缓步拾级而上，楼梯逼仄昏暗，几个阴暗的拐角甚至显得有些凶险。他心头暗暗思忖着自己即将迈出的这惊世骇俗的一步。他脑海中萌生了一个念头，却不知究竟是否切实可行，无论如何他都需要这位侦探的鼎力相助与指点。他不得不彻底袒露自己最隐秘的心迹，宛如向神父忏悔一般；他的心房跳动得厉害。3楼正对楼梯的那扇大门与寻常门户截然不同；那是一扇毫无雕花线脚的厚重原木橡木门，上面横七竖八加固着粗壮的铁条。倘若不是门上贴着一张色彩浓重的雄鸡昂首啼鸣版画，配有“永远警惕”的题字，稍微冲淡了其阴森沉重之气，它简直与大牢的铁门无异。难道这只雄鸡是侦探特意为自己张贴的家徽徽章？抑或是他某个手下恶作剧的杰作？老人在门前凝视了足有一分钟之久，宛如情窦初开的少年在心上人门前徘徊不定一般，终于伸手拉响了门铃。门后随即传来了一阵锁簧的嘎吱咬合声，透过窥孔狭窄的铁栅栏，他瞧见了一张毛发蓬乱的老妇人面孔。
“你找谁？”妇人用低沉沙哑的男中音嗓音问道。
“我找勒柯克先生。”
“找他有何贵干？”
“是他约我今早在此碰头的。”
“姓名，公职？”
“普朗塔，奥尔西瓦尔的治安法官。”
“行了。候着吧。”
窥孔啪嗒一声合上了，老人在外头耐心恭候。
“真见鬼！”他暗自咕哝道，“看来这儿可不是谁想进就能随便进的。”这念头刚在他脑海中闪过，大门便在一阵链条与锁簧的铿锵撞击声中豁然洞开。

===Original===

He entered, and the old crone, after leading him through a dining-room whose sole furniture was a table and six chairs, introduced him to a large room, half toilet-room and half working-room, lighted by two windows looking on the court, and guarded by strong, close bars.
“If you will take the trouble to sit,” said the servant, “Monsieur Lecoq will soon be here; he is giving orders to one of his men.”
But M. Plantat did not take a seat; he preferred to examine the curious apartment in which he found himself. The whole of one side of the wall was taken up with a long rack, where hung the strangest and most incongruous suits of clothes. There were costumes belonging to all grades of society; and on some wooden pegs above, wigs of all colors were hanging; while boots and shoes of various styles were ranged on the floor. A toilet-table, covered with powders, essences, and paints, stood between the fireplace and the window. On the other side of the room was a bookcase full of scientific works, especially of physic and chemistry. The most singular piece of furniture in the apartment, however, was a large ball, shaped like a lozenge, in black velvet, suspended beside the looking-glass. A quantity of pins were stuck in this ball, so as to form the letters composing these two names: **Hector﻿—Jenny**.
These names glittering on the black background attracted the old man’s attention at once. This must have been M. Lecoq’s reminder. The ball was meant to recall to him perpetually the people of whom he was in pursuit. Many names, doubtless, had in turn glittered on that velvet, for it was much frayed and perforated. An unfinished letter lay open upon the bureau.
M. Plantat leaned over to read it; but he took his trouble for nothing, for it was written in cipher.
He had no sooner finished his inspection of the room than the noise of a door opening made him turn round. He saw before him a man of his own age, of respectable mien, and polite manners, a little bald, with gold spectacles and a light-colored flannel dressing-gown.
M. Plantat bowed, saying:
“I am waiting here for Monsieur Lecoq.”
The man in gold spectacles burst out laughing, and clapped his hands with glee.
“What, dear sir,” said he, “don’t you know me? Look at me well﻿—it is I﻿—Monsieur Lecoq!” And to convince him, he took off his spectacles. Those might, indeed, be Lecoq’s eyes, and that his voice; M.

===Chinese===

他举步迈进屋内。那个老妪般的妇人引着他穿过一间仅摆着一张餐桌和6把椅子的简陋饭厅，将他领进了一间极其宽敞的大屋。这间屋子半似更衣室半似作坊公事房，由两扇朝向天井院落的大窗采光，窗外均安装了结实致密的粗铁栅栏。
“倘若您不嫌弃请随便坐，”女仆说道，“勒柯克先生片刻就到；他正在外头给手下吩咐公差。”
然而普朗塔先生并未落座；他更愿意仔细打量自己置身的这间奇特居所。整整一面墙壁都被一长排挂架占据，上面挂满了各式各样稀奇古怪、南辕北辙的行头服饰。社会各阶层的装束应有尽有；在衣架上方的木桩上，悬挂着各色假发；地面上则整整齐齐码放着款式各异的靴子与皮鞋。壁炉与窗户之间安放着一张梳妆台，上面堆满了各色香粉、香精和油彩颜料。屋子的另一侧则是一座摆满了自然科学专著的书柜，尤以物理和化学书籍居多。然而，整间屋子里最引人注目的摆设，莫过于挂在穿衣镜旁的一只硕大的菱形黑天鹅绒球。绒球上密密麻麻扎满了大头针，拼凑出了两个名字的字母：埃克托——珍妮。
这两个在黑色背景上熠熠发光的名字，顷刻间吸引了老人的目光。这必定是勒柯克先生的警示备忘录。这只绒球旨在时刻提醒他不忘正在追捕的目标。毫无疑问，曾有无数犯人的名字在这块天鹅绒上依次闪烁过，因为绒面上早已布满了斑驳的磨损与针孔。写字台上摊开着一封尚未写完的信件。
普朗塔先生俯身想要阅读一番；但他纯属白费心机，因为那封信全是用密码密电写成的。
他刚把屋子打量完毕，身后响起的一声开门声便令他蓦然回首。他赫然瞧见眼前站着一位与自己年纪相仿的男子，相貌端庄体面，举止彬彬有礼，头顶微秃，架着一副金丝眼镜，身上套着一件浅色法兰绒晨袍。
普朗塔先生欠身行礼，开口道：
“我正在此恭候勒柯克先生。”
架着金丝眼镜的男子忍不住放声大笑，孩子气地欢喜拍手道：
“怎么，亲爱的朋友，”他说道，“您竟认不出我了？仔细端详我——正是在下——勒柯克先生！”为了让他彻底信服，他一把摘下了眼镜。那双锐利的眼眸确是勒柯克的双眼无疑，嗓音也是他的嗓音；普朗塔先生惊得目瞪口呆。

===Original===

Plantat was confounded.
“I never should have recognized you,” said he.
“It’s true, I have changed a little﻿—but what would you have? It’s my trade.”
And pushing a chair toward his visitor, he pursued:
“I have to beg a thousand pardons for the formalities you’ve had to endure to get in here; it’s a dire necessity, but one I can’t help. I have told you of the dangers to which I am exposed; they pursue me to my very door. Why, last week a railway porter brought a package here addressed to me. Janouille﻿—that’s my old woman﻿—suspected nothing, though she has a sharp nose, and told him to come in. He held out the package, I went up to take it, when pif! paf! off went two pistol-shots. The package was a revolver wrapped up in oilcloth, and the porter was a convict escaped from Cayenne, caught by me last year. Ah, I put him through for this though!”
He told this adventure carelessly, as if it were the most natural thing in the world.
“But let’s not starve ourselves to death,” he continued, ringing the bell. The old hag appeared, and he ordered her to bring on breakfast forthwith, and above all, some good wine.
“You are observing my Janouille,” remarked he, seeing that M. Plantat looked curiously at the servant. “She’s a pearl, my dear friend, who watches over me as if I were her child, and would go through the fire for me. I had a good deal of trouble the other day to prevent her strangling the false railway porter. I picked her out of three or four thousand convicts. She had been convicted of infanticide and arson. I would bet a hundred to one that, during the three years that she has been in my service, she has not even thought of robbing me of so much as a centime.”
But M. Plantat only listened to him with one ear; he was trying to find an excuse for cutting Janouille’s story short, and to lead the conversation to the events of the day before.
“I have, perhaps, incommoded you a little this morning, Monsieur Lecoq?”
“Me? then you did not see my motto﻿—‘always vigilant?’ Why, I’ve been out ten times this morning; besides marking out work for three of my men. Ah, we have little time to ourselves, I can tell you. I went to the Vulcan’s Forges to see what news I could get of that poor devil of a Guespin.”
“And what did you hear?”
“That I had guessed right. He changed a five-hundred-franc note there last Wednesday evening at a quarter before ten.”

===Chinese===

“我做梦也认不出您来，”他说道。
“确实，我改头换面了一番——可又有什么法子呢？干我们这行的全凭这门手艺吃饭。”
说着，他将一把椅子推到客人面前，接着说道：
“您方才进门时不得不领教那套繁文缛节，我向您赔上一千个不是；这纯属迫不得已的戒备，我也实在别无选择。我曾向您提过我平素面临的刀光剑影；那些凶险简直一路尾随追杀到了我的家门口。就在上周，一名火车站搬运工送来了一个指名道姓寄给我的包裹。雅努伊——也就是我那个老女仆——尽管嗅觉灵敏过人，当时也未起疑心，便放他进了屋。那汉子递过包裹，我正欲上前接手，岂料‘砰！啪！’连响了两声手枪。包裹原来是一支用油布包裹好的左轮手枪，而那个搬运工正是去年被我亲手擒获、从开云苦役流放地越狱潜逃的重刑犯。哈，我回头狠狠治了他一顿！”
他轻描淡写地讲述着这桩惊心动魄的历险，语气平淡得宛如在闲话家常一般。
“不过咱们总不能把自己活活饿死，”他一边拉响桌铃一边继续说道。那个老妪应声推门而入，他吩咐她立刻端上早膳，尤其实要开一瓶上等陈酿好酒。
“您在端详我的雅努伊，”勒柯克注意到普朗塔先生正满腹狐疑地打量着那名女仆，便笑着解释道，“那可是个千金不换的宝贝，我的老友；她照料我如同照料亲生骨肉，为了我甚至甘愿赴汤蹈火。前几天我费了好大的劲，才拦住没让她亲手把那个假冒搬运工的恶棍活活勒死。当年我从3000到4000名苦役犯中把她挑了出来。她曾因溺婴与纵火罪被判重刑。我可以拿100比1同人打赌，在她投奔到我麾下当差的3年里，她脑子里连偷我半个子儿的念头都没动过。”
然而普朗塔先生不过是一只耳朵进一只耳朵出；他正在极力寻找借口打断关于雅努伊的絮叨，好将话头引向昨日的案情。
“勒柯克先生，我今早登门拜访，或许未免有些打扰您了吧？”
“打扰我？看来您当真没留心我门上的那句座右铭——‘永远警惕’！今早我都已经外出跑了10次了；更不用说还替我手下的3名弟兄布置停当了全天的脚程。哎，跟您交个底，我们真正属于自己的闲暇少得可怜。方才我还专程跑了一趟‘火神锻造坊’，打探关于盖斯潘那个可怜虫的最新虚实。”
“您都打听到了些什么？”
“正如我所预料的那般分毫不差。就在上星期三傍晚差一刻10点的时候，他确实在那儿破开了一张500法郎的纸钞。”

===Original===

“That is to say, he is saved?”
“Well, you may say so. He will be, as soon as we have found Miss Jenny.”
The old justice of the peace could not avoid showing his uneasiness.
“That will, perhaps, be long and difficult?”
“*Bast!* Why so? She is on my black ball there﻿—we shall have her, accidents excepted, before night.”
“You really think so?”
“I should say I was sure, to anybody but you. Reflect that this girl has been connected with the Count de Trémorel, a man of the world, a prince of the mode. When a girl falls to the gutter, after having, as they say, dazzled all Paris for six months with her luxury, she does not disappear entirely, like a stone in the mud. When she has lost all her friends there are still her creditors, who follow and watch her, awaiting the day when fortune will smile on her once more. She doesn’t trouble herself about them, she thinks they’ve forgotten her; a mistake! I know a milliner whose head is a perfect dictionary of the fashionable world; she has often done me a good turn. We will go and see her if you say so, after breakfast, and in two hours she will give us Jenny’s address. Ah, if I were only as sure of pinching Trémorel!”
M. Plantat gave a sigh of relief. The conversation at last took the turn he wished.
“You are thinking of him, then?” asked he.
“Am I?” shouted M. Lecoq, who started from his seat at the question. “Now just look at my black ball there. I haven’t thought of anybody else, mark you, since yesterday; I haven’t had a wink of sleep all night for thinking of him. I must have him, and I will!”
“I don’t doubt it; but when?”
“Ah, there it is! Perhaps tomorrow, perhaps in a month; it depends on the correctness of my calculations and the exactness of my plan.”
“What, is your plan made?”
“And decided on.”
M. Plantat became attention itself.
“I start from the principle that it is impossible for a man, accompanied by a woman, to hide from the police. In this case, the woman is young, pretty, and in a noticeable condition; three impossibilities more. Admit this, and we’ll study Hector’s character. He isn’t a man of superior shrewdness, for we have found out all his dodges. He isn’t a fool, because his dodges deceived people who are by no means fools. He is then a medium sort of a man, and his education, reading, relations, and daily conversation have procured him a number of acquaintances whom he will try to use.

===Chinese===

“这么说来，他有救了？”
“唔，也可以这么说。只要咱们揪出那位珍妮小姐，他就彻底脱罪了。”
老治安法官掩饰不住心头的忧心忡忡。
“找寻那个女人，恐怕既耗时费力又困难重重吧？”
“得了吧！何出此言？她如今已然钉在我的那只黑绒球上了——只要不出意外，日落之前咱们定能将她缉拿归案。”
“您当真这般笃定？”
“换作旁人我敢把胸脯拍得啪啪响，说自己十拿九稳。请您想想看，这姑娘曾委身于特雷莫雷尔伯爵——那可是一位声名显赫的体面绅士、名震四海的社交界骄子。一个曾在整整6个月的时间里以泼天豪奢晃花全巴黎眼睛的年轻女子，一旦跌落至泥沼沟渠，是绝不可能像泥塘里的石子那样无声无息彻底蒸发的。当她散尽了狐朋狗友之后，身边至少还尾随着一群如影随形的讨债鬼；这群债主寸步不离盯死着她，眼巴巴候着幸运女神再次向她展颜欢笑的那一天。她自己或许对此满不在乎，以为债主们早就把她抛到了九霄云外；大错特错！我恰好相识一位女帽商掌柜，她脑子里简直就是一本巴黎上流社会的活字典；她往日可没少帮我的大忙。只要您首肯，饭后咱们便立刻前去登门拜访，不出2小时她就能将珍妮如今的藏身之所双手奉上。啊，要是我抓捕特雷莫雷尔也能有这般十足的把握该有多好！”
普朗塔先生如释重负地长吁了一口气。话头总算拐到了他所渴盼的轨道上来。
“这么说，您心底一直在盘算着他？”他追问道。
“我还能不想他吗？”勒柯克先生猛地从座椅上一跃而起大声嚷嚷道，“您且瞧瞧那只黑绒球！我跟您交个底，自打昨天起我脑子里除了他再容不下半个人影；为了琢磨他，我整整一夜合不上眼。我非把他生擒活捉不可，而且我一定能办到！”
“对此我深信不疑；可究竟在何时呢？”
“哈，症结恰恰就在此处！或许就在明天，或许还得耗上一个月；全看我的推演推算是否严丝合缝，作战全盘方略是否滴水不漏。”
“怎么，您的全盘方略已经拟定妥当了？”
“而且已然敲定。”
普朗塔先生顿时全神贯注竖起了耳朵。
“我的全盘推理始于一条铁律：一个大活人若是身旁携带着一名女眷，绝无可能在警署眼皮子底下遁形隐匿。在眼下这桩案子里，那位女眷年轻貌美，且身怀六甲形迹极其显眼；这无异于平添了三桩不可能隐匿的死穴。倘若承认这一点，咱们便不妨来深入剖析一下埃克托的性情脾气。他绝非天纵奇才的顶尖智者，因为他的雕虫小技已被咱们彻底识破；但他亦非蠢笨如猪的平庸之辈，因为他的金蝉脱壳诡计成功蒙骗了一众绝非等闲之辈的精明人。因此，他充其量只是个中等资质之流，而他所受的栽培熏陶、博览的群书、结交的人脉以及平日里的交游闲谈，使他积攒了一批各色熟人，他必然会试图加以利用。

===Original===

Now for his mind. We know the weakness of his character; soft, feeble, vacillating, only acting in the last extremity. We have seen him shrinking from decisive steps, trying always to delay matters. He is given to being deceived by illusions, and to taking his desires for accomplished events. In short, he is a coward. And what is his situation? He has killed his wife, he hopes he has created a belief in his own death, he has eloped with a young girl, and he has got nearly or quite a million of francs in his pocket. Now, this position admitted, as well as the man’s character and mind, can we by an effort of thought, reasoning from his known actions, discover what he has done in such and such a case? I think so, and I hope I shall prove it to you.”
M. Lecoq rose and promenaded, as his habit was, up and down the room. “Now let’s see,” he continued, “how I ought to proceed in order to discover the probable conduct of a man whose antecedents, traits, and mind are known to me. To begin with, I throw off my own individuality and try to assume his. I substitute his will for my own. I cease to be a detective and become this man, whatever he is. In this case, for instance, I know very well what I should do if I were Trémorel. I should take such measures as would throw all the detectives in the universe off the scent. But I must forget Monsieur Lecoq in order to become Hector de Trémorel. How would a man reason who was base enough to rob his friend of his wife, and then see her poison her husband before his very eyes? We already know that Trémorel hesitated a good while before deciding to commit this crime. The logic of events, which fools call fatality, urged him on. It is certain that he looked upon the murder in every point of view, studied its results, and tried to find means to escape from justice. All his acts were determined on long beforehand, and neither immediate necessity nor unforeseen circumstances disturbed his mind. The moment he had decided on the crime, he said to himself: ‘Grant that Bertha has been murdered; thanks to my precautions, they think that I have been killed too; Laurence, with whom I elope, writes a letter in which she announces her suicide; I have money, what must I do?’ The problem, it seems to me, is fairly put in this way.”
“Perfectly so,” approved M. Plantat.

===Chinese===

再来看看他的心智底色。咱们深知他性格中的致命软肋：绵软无力、懦弱动摇，唯有被逼至绝境死角时才会孤注一掷。咱们亲眼目睹过他面对决断退避三舍，一味试图拖延推诿。他极易被虚妄幻想所蒙蔽，总是将心中所愿想当然地当作既成事实。简而言之，他是个彻头彻尾的懦夫。那么他当下的处境又是如何呢？他亲手戕害了发妻，满以为已成功伪造了自己葬身火海的假象，拐带了一名名门闺秀私奔出逃，怀里还揣着逼近甚至足足1000000法郎的巨额现银。既然这一客观处境、连同此人的性格与心智底色皆已板上钉钉，难道我们不能凭借缜密的逻辑推演，根据其已知的言行轨迹，推断出他在特定局面下究竟会作何抉择吗？我认为完全行得通，而且我希望当场向您证明这一点。”
勒柯克先生站起身来，按照他一贯的习惯在屋里踱来踱去。
“咱们且来瞧瞧，”他继续剖析道，“若要推断出一个过往背景、性格特征与心智结构皆为我所熟知之人的行径举止，我应当如何着手。首先，我必须彻底剥离掉我自身的个性人格，设身处地化作对方。我将他的意志凌驾于我自身的意志之上。我不再是一名警署刑侦密探，而是蜕变成为那个特定的男人，无论他是何方神圣。比方说在眼下这个案子里，倘若我是特雷莫雷尔，我十分清楚自己会采取何种手段，我必定会布下一套足以让普天之下所有刑警全盘失嗅的迷魂阵。然而眼下我必须忘掉勒柯克先生，将自己彻头彻尾化作埃克托·德·特雷莫雷尔。一个下作无耻到霸占挚友发妻、眼睁睁看着那女人在自己眼皮底下给丈夫下毒的恶徒，究竟会如何权衡利弊？咱们早已知晓，特雷莫雷尔在横下一条心犯下这桩血案之前，曾经历过极其漫长的动摇犹疑。事态发展的必然逻辑——蠢人们惯常称之为命中注定——推着他一步步走向深渊。可以肯定的是，他曾从每一个角度审视过这场谋杀，反复推演过其后果，并竭力寻觅逃脱法网制裁的生路。他的一切行动皆是蓄谋已久，既非迫于燃眉之急，亦未被任何突发变故打乱阵脚。就在他决意动手的瞬间，他在心底自言自语道：‘就算贝尔塔命丧黄泉；多亏我事先布置周全，外界皆以为我也一同遇害；与我私奔的洛朗斯亲笔写下绝笔信宣告自尽；我怀揣万贯家财，接下来究竟该何去何从？’在我看来，问题的核心症结便这般直截了当地摆在眼前了。”
“精辟绝伦，”普朗塔先生赞许道。

===Original===

“Naturally, Trémorel would choose from among all the methods of flight of which he had ever heard, or which he could imagine, that which seemed to him the surest and most prompt. Did he meditate leaving the country? That is more than probable. Only, as he was not quite out of his senses, he saw that it was most difficult, in a foreign country, to put justice off the track. If a man flies from France to escape punishment, he acts absurdly. Fancy a man and woman wandering about a country of whose language they are ignorant; they attract attention at once, are observed, talked about, followed. They do not make a purchase which is not remarked; they cannot make any movement without exciting curiosity. The further they go the greater their danger. If they choose to cross the ocean and go to free America, they must go aboard a vessel; and the moment they do that they may be considered as good as lost. You might bet twenty to one they would find, on landing on the other side, a detective on the pier armed with a warrant to arrest them. I would engage to find a Frenchman in eight days, even in London, unless he spoke pure enough English to pass for a citizen of the United Kingdom. Such were Trémorel’s reflections. He recollected a thousand futile attempts, a hundred surprising adventures, narrated by the papers; and it is certain that he gave up the idea of going abroad.”
“It’s clear,” cried M. Plantat, “perfectly plain and precise. We must look for the fugitives in France.”
“Yes,” replied M. Lecoq. “Now let’s find out where and how people can hide themselves in France. Would it be in the provinces? Evidently not. In Bordeaux, one of our largest cities, people stare at a man who is not a Bordelais. The shopkeepers on the quays say to their neighbors: ‘Eh! do you know that man?’ There are two cities, however, where a man may pass unnoticed﻿—Marseilles and Lyons; but both of these are distant, and to reach them a long journey must be risked﻿—and nothing is so dangerous as the railway since the telegraph was established. One can fly quickly, it’s true; but on entering a railway carriage a man shuts himself in, and until he gets out of it he remains under the thumb of the police. Trémorel knows all this as well as we do. We will put all the large towns, including Lyons and Marseilles, out of the question.”
“In short, it’s impossible to hide in the provinces.”

===Chinese===

“很自然，特雷莫雷尔必定会在他所耳闻目睹或绞尽脑汁构想出的全盘潜逃路线中，遴选出一条在他看来最稳妥、最迅捷的退路。他是否曾动过远走高飞逃往国外的念头？极有可能。只不过，只要他脑子尚未彻底疯癫，他就必然会意识到，在异国他乡想要彻底甩掉司法追捕乃是难如登天。一个人若是企图靠逃离法国来躲避惩处，其行径无异于自投罗网。不妨设想一下：一男一女在一个语言不通的陌生国度游荡颠沛；他们顷刻间便会引人侧目，遭人窥探、议论与盯梢。他们采买任何一件细微物件皆会惹人怀疑，举手投足皆会激起旁人的好奇探究。他们逃得越远，面临的凶险便呈倍数递增。倘若他们胆敢横渡大洋远赴自由的美利坚，就必须登上海轮客船；而从他们踏上甲板的那一刹那起，便可视为已沦为瓮中之鳖。你可以拿20比1同人打赌，当客轮靠泊大洋彼岸时，码头上早就候着一名手持拘捕通缉令的刑警密探了。我敢打包票，哪怕是在伦敦城里，只要一个法国人说不出地道得足以冒充大不列颠臣民的纯正英语，不出8天我必定能将他揪出来。埃克托心底想必也盘算过这一层。他脑海中浮现出报端连篇累牍刊载过的1000次徒劳挣扎、100桩离奇败露的覆辙；因而可以肯定的是，他早已彻底掐灭了潜逃出国的念头。”
“全说通了，”普朗塔先生拍案叫绝道，“入木三分，丝丝入扣。这么说来，咱们必须在法兰西本土搜寻这对亡命之徒了。”
“正是，”勒柯克先生答道，“眼下咱们便来推敲推敲，在法国究竟在何处、以何种手段才能隐匿形迹。是在各省外埠吗？显然绝非如此。在波尔多——咱们屈指可数的大都市之一——倘若一个外乡人走在街上，满大街的人都会对他行注目礼。码头上的商铺掌柜会私下咬耳朵问邻舍：‘哎！你认得那生面孔是谁吗？’不过，倒确实有两座足以隐入人海而不引人瞩目的巨埠大城——马赛与里昂；然而这两地路途遥远，前往彼处必须冒着长途跋涉的巨大凶险——而自从电报铺设开来之后，天下再没有什么比乘坐火车铁路更凶险的逃亡行径了。乘火车逃跑固然风驰电掣；然而一旦踏进车厢，人便等于把自己反锁在了铁盒子里，在走下车厢之前，生死全攥在警署的手掌心里。特雷莫雷尔对这些门道同你我一样心知肚明。因此，包括里昂与马赛在内的外埠大城，全都可以彻底排除在外。”
“简而言之，想要在各省外埠隐匿根本是痴心妄想。”

===Original===

“Excuse me﻿—there is one means; that is, simply to buy a modest little place at a distance from towns and railways, and to go and reside on it under a false name. But this excellent project is quite above Trémorel’s capacity, and requires preparatory steps which he could not risk, watched as he was by his wife. The field of investigation is thus much narrowed. Putting aside foreign parts, the provinces, the cities, the country, Paris remains. It is in Paris that we must look for Trémorel.”
M. Lecoq spoke with the certainty and positiveness of a mathematical professor; the old justice of the peace listened, as do the professor’s scholars. But he was already accustomed to the detective’s surprising clearness, and was no longer astonished. During the four-and-twenty hours that he had been witnessing M. Lecoq’s calculations and gropings, he had seized the process and almost appropriated it to himself. He found this method of reasoning very simple, and could now explain to himself certain exploits of the police which had hitherto seemed to him miraculous. But M. Lecoq’s “narrow field” of observation appeared still immense.
“Paris is a large place,” observed the old justice.
M. Lecoq smiled loftily.
“Perhaps so; but it is mine. All Paris is under the eye of the police, just as an ant is under that of the naturalist with his microscope. How is it, you may ask, that Paris still holds so many professional rogues? Ah, that is because we are hampered by legal forms. The law compels us to use only polite weapons against those to whom all weapons are serviceable. The courts tie our hands. The rogues are clever, but be sure that our cleverness is much greater than theirs.”
“But,” interrupted M. Plantat, “Trémorel is now outside the law; we have the warrant.”
“What matters it? Does the warrant give me the right to search any house in which I may have reason to suppose he is hiding himself? No. If I should go to the house of one of Hector’s old friends he would kick me out of doors. You must know that in France the police have to contend not only with the rogues, but also with the honest people.”
M. Lecoq always waxed warm on this subject; he felt a strong resentment against the injustice practised on his profession. Fortunately, at the moment when he was most excited, the black ball suddenly caught his eye.
“The devil!” exclaimed he, “I was forgetting Hector.”
M.

===Chinese===

“且慢——倒也并非全无门路；那便是在远离市镇与铁路的偏远乡野，悄悄置办下一处不起眼的小小田庄，隐姓埋名迁居过去苟活。然而这套高明谋划早已远远超出了特雷莫雷尔的心智段位，而且需要大费周章的前期铺垫周旋，在发妻严密监视盯梢之下他根本不敢冒险走这步棋。如此一来，搜寻排查的范围便大幅收窄了。在排除了异国他乡、外省各州、外埠大城以及僻壤乡村之后，剩下的唯有一处——巴黎。咱们唯有在巴黎，才能把特雷莫雷尔揪出来。”
勒柯克先生这番话掷地有声、言之凿凿，宛如数学教授在推演公理定理一般；老治安法官毕恭毕敬倾听着，一如教授座下求知若渴的学子。不过，他早已领教过这位名侦探惊世骇俗的敏锐清晰，心中已不再感到诧异。在亲眼见证勒柯克先生精细推演与摸索探查的这24小时里，他已然心领神会掌握了这套逻辑门径，几乎能将其收为己用。他发觉这种推导法其实顺理成章，如今终于能参透警署过往那些在他看来宛如神迹般的破案传奇了。然而，在勒柯克口中所谓的“狭窄范围”，在他看来依然浩瀚如烟海。
“巴黎可是一座无边无际的汪洋大海啊，”老法官感慨道。
勒柯克先生傲然一笑。
“话虽如此；但这里是我的天下。整个巴黎全在警署的鹰眼监视之下，正如显微镜下被博物学家凝视的一只蝼蚁一般。您或许会心生疑窦：既然如此，巴黎为何至今依然藏匿着如此众多的职业恶棍惯匪？哈，那纯粹是因为咱们手脚被冰冷的司法条文给捆绑住了。法律严令咱们在面对无所不用其极的恶徒时，只能使用体面克制的斯文武器。法庭束缚住了咱们的手脚。恶棍固然狡黠，但请坚信，咱们的机变手段远胜过他们百倍。”
“可是，”普朗塔先生打断道，“特雷莫雷尔眼下已沦为不受法律庇护的在逃要犯；咱们手头已握有拘捕通缉令。”
“那又顶什么用？难道通缉令赋予了我随意搜查我怀疑藏匿重犯的任何私宅民居的特权吗？绝无可能。倘若我胆敢贸然闯进埃克托某个旧相识的府邸搜查，对方非一脚把我踹出门外不可。您必须明白，在法兰西，警署不仅要同恶棍斗智斗勇，还得时刻提防体面绅士的百般刁难掣肘。”
每逢触及这个话头，勒柯克先生总会义愤填膺大动肝火；他对自身行当所遭受的不公待遇深怀怨怼。好在就在他情绪最为激昂的关口，穿衣镜旁那只黑天鹅绒球冷不丁撞进了他的眼帘。
“该死！”他失声惊呼道，“我差点把埃克托给抛到脑后了。”

===Original===

Plantat, though listening patiently to his companion’s indignant utterances, could not help thinking of the murderer.
“You said that we must look for Trémorel in Paris,” he remarked.
“And I said truly,” responded M. Lecoq in a calmer tone. “I have come to the conclusion that here, perhaps within two streets of us, perhaps in the next house, the fugitives are hid. But let’s go on with our calculation of probabilities. Hector knows Paris too well to hope to conceal himself even for a week in a hotel or lodging-house; he knows these are too sharply watched by the police. He had plenty of time before him, and so arranged to hire apartments in some convenient house.”
“He came to Paris three or four times some weeks ago.”
“Then there’s no longer any doubt about it. He hired some apartments under a false name, paid in advance, and today he is comfortably ensconced in his new residence.”
M. Plantat seemed to feel extremely distressed at this.
“I know it only too well, Monsieur Lecoq,” said he, sadly. “You must be right. But is not the wretch thus securely hidden from us? Must we wait till some accident reveals him to us? Can you search one by one all the houses in Paris?”
The detective’s nose wriggled under his gold spectacles, and the justice of the peace, who observed it, and took it for a good sign, felt all his hopes reviving in him.
“I’ve cudgelled my brain in vain﻿—” he began.
“Pardon me,” interrupted M. Lecoq. “Having hired apartments, Trémorel naturally set about furnishing them.”
“Evidently.”
“Of course he would furnish them sumptuously, both because he is fond of luxury and has plenty of money, and because he couldn’t carry a young girl from a luxurious home to a garret. I’d wager that they have as fine a drawing-room as that at Valfeuillu.”
“Alas! How can that help us?”
“*Peste!* It helps us much, my dear friend, as you shall see. Hector, as he wished for a good deal of expensive furniture, did not have recourse to a broker; nor had he time to go to the Faubourg St. Antoine. Therefore, he simply went to an upholsterer.”
“Some fashionable upholsterer﻿—”
“No, he would have risked being recognized. It is clear that he assumed a false name, the same in which he had hired his rooms. He chose some shrewd and humble upholsterer, ordered his goods, made sure that they would be delivered on a certain day, and paid for them.”
M.

===Chinese===

普朗塔先生尽管耐着性子倾听着同伴满腹牢骚的愤懑控诉，心头却时刻未敢放下那个杀人凶犯。
“您方才言之凿凿，说咱们必须在巴黎搜寻特雷莫雷尔的踪迹，”他提醒道。
“我说得一点不错，”勒柯克先生平复了心绪，语气重新变得沉着笃定，“我断定逃犯眼下就潜藏在此处，或许就在距离咱们隔着2条街的街区，甚至就在隔壁隔壁的屋舍里。不过咱们且继续依照概率往下推演。埃克托对巴黎太熟悉了，深知自己绝不可能在任何一家客栈旅舍里安生躲过哪怕一个礼拜；他清楚那些落脚客栈全在警署密探的火眼金睛盯梢之下。他此前有大把从容充裕的光阴，因而定然早就着手在某栋便利妥当的宅邸里赁下了一套隐秘寓所。”
“几周之前，他确实曾接连赶赴巴黎跑了3到4趟。”
“既然如此，便再无半分疑虑了。他定然是借用化名假名赁下了寓所，预先垫付了全额租金，如今正舒舒服服安顿在他的新巢穴里享清福呢。”
普朗塔先生闻言，脸上掠过一抹极其痛苦的愁云。
“我何尝不知，勒柯克先生，”他凄然说道，“您说的必然分毫不差。可如此一来，那恶徒岂不是彻底遁入暗处脱离了咱们的视线？难道咱们唯有坐等某桩意外变故从天而降将他暴露不成？难道您能把全巴黎的千家万户挨家挨户搜个底朝天吗？”
侦探的金丝眼镜底下的鼻翼微微抽搐了两下；看在眼里的老治安法官深知这是破案在望的吉兆，心头熄灭的希望顿时重新死灰复燃。
“我苦思冥想良久终是一筹莫展——”他刚开口道。
“请恕我打断，”勒柯克先生插言道，“既然赁下了寓所，特雷莫雷尔接下来自然便要着手添置家具铺陈陈设。”
“那是自然。”
“他添置的陈设必定奢华考究，一来他生性穷奢极欲且腰缠万贯，二来他绝不可能把一个在锦衣玉食中娇生惯养的名门闺秀拐带进一间破败阁楼。我敢打赌，他们如今起居的客厅，奢华程度绝不逊色于瓦尔菲伊庄园的分毫。”
“唉！可这又能对破案有何裨益呢？”
“见鬼！这可帮了咱们天大的忙啊，我的好朋友，您稍后便知分晓。埃克托既然一心渴盼置办一大批昂贵考究的家具，就绝不会去旧货贩子那儿淘二手货；他更抽不出闲暇千里迢迢赶往圣安托万郊区的木器行。因此，他必然是径直找了一家高档家具装潢铺。”
“某家名噪一时的高档铺子——”
“不，那样做随时有被人认出真容的凶险。显而易见，他必定套用了租房时的假名假姓。他挑选了一家老板精明市侩、门面低调不显山露水的装潢行，订购了全套行头，再三叮嘱务必在特定日期如约送货上门，并且当场结清了全额现款。”

===Original===

Plantat could not repress a joyful exclamation; he began to see M. Lecoq’s drift.
“This merchant,” pursued the latter, “must have retained his rich customer in his memory, this customer who did not beat him down, and paid cash. If he saw him again, he would recognize him.”
“What an idea!” cried M. Plantat, delighted. “Let’s get photographs and portraits of Trémorel as quick as we can﻿—let’s send a man to Orcival for them.”
M. Lecoq smiled shrewdly and proceeded:
“Keep yourself easy; I have done what was necessary. I slipped three of the count’s cartes-de-visite in my pocket yesterday during the inquest. This morning I took down, out of the directory, the names of all the upholsterers in Paris, and made three lists of them. At this moment three of my men, each with a list and a photograph, are going from upholsterer to upholsterer showing them the picture and asking them if they recognize it as the portrait of one of their customers. If one of them answers ‘yes,’ we’ve got our man.”
“And we will get him!” cried the old man, pale with emotion.
“Not yet; don’t shout victory too soon. It is possible that Hector was prudent enough not to go to the upholsterer’s himself. In this case we are beaten in that direction. But no, he was not so sly as that﻿—”
M. Lecoq checked himself. Janouille, for the third time, opened the door, and said, in a deep bass voice:
“Breakfast is ready.”
Janouille was a remarkable cook; M. Plantat had ample experience of the fact when he began upon her dishes. But he was not hungry, and could not force himself to eat; he could not think of anything but a plan which he had to propose to his host, and he had that oppressive feeling which is experienced when one is about to do something which has been decided on with hesitation and regret. The detective, who, like all men of great activity, was a great eater, vainly essayed to entertain his guest, and filled his glass with the choicest Château Margaux; the old man sat silent and sad, and only responded by monosyllables. He tried to speak out and to struggle against the hesitation he felt. He did not think, when he came, that he should have this reluctance; he had said to himself that he would go in and explain himself. Did he fear to be ridiculed? No. His passion was above the fear of sarcasm or irony. And what did he risk? Nothing. Had not M.

===Chinese===

普朗塔先生忍不住发出一声欢喜的惊叹；他终于摸透了勒柯克先生的深谋远虑。
“这位掌柜，”侦探继续剖析道，“对于这样一位买东西从不讨价还价、甩出大把现银结账的豪阔贵客，必定记忆犹新。只要再次瞧见他的模样，断无认不出来的道理。”
“何等神机妙算！”普朗塔先生欢欣雀跃道，“咱们得火速弄到特雷莫雷尔的相片与画像——咱们赶紧差人快马加鞭赶赴奥尔西瓦尔取来。”
勒柯克先生狡黠一笑，接着说道：
“放宽心吧；我早已把该办的打点停当了。昨天在命案勘验的当口，我顺手把伯爵的3张名片小照揣进了衣兜里。今天一大早，我翻阅工商行号黄页名录，抄录下了全巴黎所有家具装潢商行的字号名册，誊抄成了3份清单。此时此刻，我手下的3名弟兄，每人怀揣一份商行名单与一张伯爵相片，正穿梭在各大装潢行之间挨家挨户登门排查，出示相片盘问掌柜伙计是否认得这是自家的大主顾。只要其中任何一家掌柜给出肯定的答复，咱们便将此贼彻底锁定了。”
“咱们定能将他手到擒来！”老人因情绪激荡而脸色煞白，高声疾呼道。
“且慢；切莫过早高奏凯歌。特雷莫雷尔或许足够老奸巨猾，并未亲自登门挑选家具。倘若当真如此，这条线索便彻底扑空了。不过量他也没这般滴水不漏的深沉城府——”
勒柯克先生骤然刹住话头。雅努伊第3次推开房门，用粗沉沙哑的男低音通报答道：
“早膳备齐了。”
雅努伊确实是一位手艺非凡的烹饪好手；普朗塔先生刚一动筷便深切领教了这一点。然而他全无胃口，无论如何也勉强咽不下饭菜；他满脑子全是自己意欲向房主人摊牌和盘托出的全套谋划，心头被那种唯有在迫不得已做出艰难悔恨抉择前夕才会感受到的沉重窒息感所笼罩。这位名侦探正如所有精力旺盛之辈一样向来食量惊人，他极力想要款待周全自己的客人，频频为对方斟满最名贵的玛歌酒庄顶级佳酿；然而老人只是凄楚默坐，应答翻来覆去不过是单音节词。他竭力想要敞开心扉，同心头那份退缩犹疑作斗争。在动身赴约之前，他未曾料到自己竟会这般退缩怯懦；他曾下定决心登门后便坦白交代一切。难道他是唯恐遭人嗤笑揶揄？绝非如此。他心头的那份狂热深情早已凌驾于对任何冷嘲热讽的畏怯之上。况且他又会冒何等凶险呢？毫无凶险可言。难道勒柯克先生不是

===Original===

Lecoq already divined the secret thoughts he dared not impart to him, and read his heart from the first? He was reflecting thus when the doorbell rang. Janouille went to the door, and speedily returned with the announcement that Goulard begged to speak with M. Lecoq, and asked if she should admit him.
“Certainly.”
The chains clanked and the locks scraped, and presently Goulard made his appearance. He had donned his best clothes, with spotless linen, and a very high collar. He was respectful, and stood as stiffly as a well-drilled grenadier before his sergeant.
“What the deuce brought you here?” said M. Lecoq, sternly. “And who dared to give you my address?”
“Monsieur,” said Goulard, visibly intimidated by his reception, “please excuse me; I was sent by Doctor Gendron with this letter for Monsieur Plantat.”
“Oh,” cried M. Plantat, “I asked the doctor, last evening, to let me know the result of the autopsy, and not knowing where I should put up, took the liberty of giving your address.”
M. Lecoq took the letter and handed it to his guest. “Read it, read it,” said the latter. “There is nothing in it to conceal.”
“All right; but come into the other room. Janouille, give this man some breakfast. Make yourself at home, Goulard, and empty a bottle to my health.”
When the door of the other room was closed, M. Lecoq broke the seal of the letter, and read:

> “My Dear Plantat:
> “You asked me for a word, so I scratch off a line or two which I shall send to our sorcerer’s﻿—”

“Oh, ho,” cried M. Lecoq. “Monsieur Gendron is too good, too flattering, really!”
No matter, the compliment touched his heart. He resumed the letter:

> “At three this morning we exhumed poor Sauvresy’s body. I certainly deplore the frightful circumstances of this worthy man’s death as much as anyone; but on the other hand, I cannot help rejoicing at this excellent opportunity to test the efficacy of my sensitive paper﻿—”

“Confound these men of science,” cried the indignant Plantat. “They are all alike!”
“Why so? I can very well comprehend the doctor’s involuntary sensations. Am I not ravished when I encounter a fine crime?”
And without waiting for his guest’s reply, he continued reading the letter:

===Chinese===

早在一开局便看穿了他不敢吐露的心思隐秘、将他的心肝肺腑彻底读透了吗？他正沉浸在这番纷乱思绪中，门铃声骤然大作。雅努伊前去应门，片刻工夫折返回来通报，说古拉尔恳求面见勒柯克先生，询问是否放他入内。
“准他进来。”
链条叮当铿锵作响，锁簧嘎吱转动，不多时古拉尔的身影便出现在了屋内。他换上了自己最体面的行头，衬衫雪白，领口竖得老高。他毕恭毕敬垂手而立，站姿挺拔僵硬宛如接受军士检阅的正规掷弹兵。
“你小子到底吃饱了撑的跑这儿来干嘛？”勒柯克先生面带愠色严厉喝问道，“是谁吃了豹子胆敢把我的住址泄露给你的？”
“长官大人，”古拉尔显然被这阵当头棒喝吓得不轻，战战兢兢分辩道，“请您千万恕罪；我是奉让德龙医生之命，特意给普朗塔先生送这封亲笔手信来的。”
“噢，”普朗塔先生连忙出面解释道，“昨晚我曾托付医生一旦验尸有了确凿结果便立刻知会我一声，因不知自己今早会下榻何处，便冒昧留下了您的寓所地址。”
勒柯克先生接过信件递给客人。“念吧，当面念念吧，”老治安法官说道，“里头绝无任何需要瞒人的隐秘。”
“那好；咱们到外间书房去。雅努伊，给这位弟兄端上些早饭充饥。古拉尔，别拘束放开肚皮吃，喝上一整瓶好酒替我的健康干一杯。”
待到书房大门阖拢之后，勒柯克先生拆开火漆封印，朗声诵读起来：

> “我亲爱的普朗塔：
> “您既然托我留个话，我便草草划拉两三行字，着人送到咱们那位活神仙府上去——”

“哈哈，”勒柯克先生失声笑道，“让德龙先生未免太抬举我、太赏脸奉承我了，真教人受宠若惊！”
不论怎样，这声赞誉着实搔到了他的痒处。他继续念道：

> “今晨3点整，我们起掘了可怜的索夫勒齐的遗体。对于这位正人君子的惨死横祸，我的悲痛惋惜之情绝不亚于在座任何一人；但在另一方面，能逮住这等千载难逢的绝佳良机来检验我那套感光试纸的神奇功效，我心头又忍不住欢欣鼓舞——”

“这帮搞学问的家伙真该挨千刀，”义愤填膺的普朗塔先生忍不住破口大骂道，“全是一个模子刻出来的冷血狂！”
“何出此言？我完全能体谅医生这种情不自禁的狂喜。每逢撞上一桩精彩绝伦的惊天大案，难道我自己不也一样如痴如醉吗？”
未等客人答话，他便径直往下诵读信文：

===Original===

> “The experiments promised to be all the more conclusive as aconitine is one of those drugs which conceal themselves most obstinately from analysis. I proceed thus: After heating the suspected substances in twice their weight of alcohol, I drop the liquid gently into a vase with edges a little elevated, at the bottom of which is a piece of paper on which I have placed my tests. If my paper retains its color, there is no poison; if it changes, the poison is there. In this case my paper was of a light yellow color, and if we were not mistaken, it ought either to become covered with brown spots, or completely brown. I explained this experiment beforehand to the judge of instruction and the experts who were assisting me. Ah, my friend, what a success I had! When the first drops of alcohol fell, the paper at once became a dark brown; your suspicions are thus proved to be quite correct. The substances which I submitted to the test were liberally saturated with aconitine. I never obtained more decisive results in my laboratory. I expect that my conclusions will be disputed in court; but I have means of verifying them, so that I shall surely confound all the chemists who oppose me. I think, my dear friend, that you will not be indifferent to the satisfaction I feel﻿—”

===Chinese===

> “鉴于乌头碱乃是各类毒物中在化验分析时隐藏最深、最难定性的品种之一，这项试验的结果因而显得愈发具有决定性意义。我的具体操作规程如下：将受检可疑样本浸泡在相当于其两倍重量的纯酒精中加热萃取，随后将提取出的清液轻柔滴入一只边缘微翘的浅口器皿中，器皿底部预先铺平了一张浸润了我独家检测试剂的特制试纸。倘若试纸维持原本色泽不变，便代表全无毒物反应；倘若试纸色泽发生变异，便确凿无疑证实毒物潜伏其中。在眼下这桩检视中，我的试纸原呈淡黄色；倘若咱们先前的推断准确无误，试纸理应浮现出点点棕褐色斑渍，抑或通体彻底转为深褐色。在动手操演之前，我曾将全套实验原理向预审法官大人及从旁襄助的各位专家同行作了详尽阐述。啊，我的挚友，那是何等辉煌的空前胜利啊！当头一滴酒精溶液方一触碰试纸，整张纸片顷刻间化作了一片浓黑墨褐；由此一来，您的全盘怀疑已然彻底坐实为无可辩驳的铁证！送检受试的可疑脏器样本中，大量充斥着高浓度的致命乌头碱。即便在我的私人实验室里，我也从未获取过如此无可置疑的决定性明证。我预料法庭开庭质证之日，我的推论结论势必会招致辩方专家的激烈攻讦攻诘；但我手头握有确凿无疑的复核验证手段，届时定能将所有胆敢跳出来唱反调的化学家驳得体无完肤、哑口无言。我想，我亲爱的老友，您对我此刻心头的莫大快慰，定然不会无动于衷吧——”

===Original===

M. Plantat lost patience.
“This is unheard-of!” cried he. “Incredible! Would you say, now, that this poison which he found in Sauvresy’s body was stolen from his own laboratory? Why, that body is nothing more to him than ‘suspected matter!’ And he already imagines himself discussing the merits of his sensitive paper in court!”
“He has reason to look for antagonists in court.”
“And meanwhile he makes his experiments, and analyzes with the coolest blood in the world; he continues his abominable cooking, boiling and filtering, and preparing his arguments﻿—!”
M. Lecoq did not share in his friend’s indignation; he was not sorry at the prospect of a bitter struggle in court, and he imagined a great scientific duel, like that between Orfila and Raspail, the provincial and Parisian chemists.
“If Trémorel has the face to deny his part in Sauvresy’s murder,” said he, “we shall have a superb trial of it.”
This word “trial” put an end to M. Plantat’s long hesitation.
“We mustn’t have any trial,” cried he.
The old man’s violence, from one who was usually so calm and self-possessed, seemed to amaze M. Lecoq.
“Ah ha,” thought he, “I’m going to know all.” He added aloud:
“What, no trial?”
M. Plantat had turned whiter than a sheet; he was trembling, and his voice was hoarse, as if broken by sobs.
“I would give my fortune,” resumed he, “to avoid a trial﻿—every centime of it, though it doesn’t amount to much. But how can we secure this wretch Trémorel from a conviction? What subterfuge shall we invent? You alone, my friend, can advise me in the frightful extremity to which you see me reduced, and aid me to accomplish what I wish. If there is any way in the world, you will find it and save me﻿—”
“But, my﻿—”
“Pardon﻿—hear me, and you will comprehend me. I am going to be frank with you, as I would be with myself; and you will see the reason of my hesitation, my silence, in short, of all my conduct since the discovery of the crime.”
“I am listening.”
“It’s a sad history, Lecoq. I had reached an age at which a man’s career is, as they say, finished, when I suddenly lost my wife and my two sons, my whole joy, my whole hope in this world. I found myself alone in life, more lost than the shipwrecked man in the midst of the sea, without a plank to sustain me. I was a soulless body, when chance brought me to settle down at Orcival.

===Chinese===

普朗塔先生终于忍无可忍了。
“简直骇人听闻！”他失声怒斥道，“荒谬绝伦！照这么说来，他在索夫勒齐体内化验出的毒药，难道不是从他自己的实验室里失窃的吗？在他眼里，挚友冰冷的遗骸充其量不过成了‘受检可疑样本’！而他眼下居然已经自鸣得意地在脑子里预演起在法庭辩论上如何捍卫他那张宝贝试纸的无上荣耀了！”
“他预料到法庭上必有交锋对手，倒也并非无的放矢。”
“可在此期间他却像个没事人一样，冷酷得连骨髓都透着冰渣，心安理得做着他的试验、搞着他的化验分析；他继续摆弄着他那一套令人作呕的瓶瓶罐罐、熬煮过滤，处心积虑筹备着他的辩论雄辞——！”
勒柯克先生对同伴的义愤填膺并不感同身受；一想到法庭上即将上演的唇枪舌剑死磕硬碰，他甚至心生向往，脑海中浮现出宛如当年奥尔菲拉与拉斯帕伊之间那场名震天下、代表外省与巴黎化学权威的世纪科学大决斗。
“倘若特雷莫雷尔厚颜无耻矢口否认自己谋害索夫勒齐的罪孽，”他说道，“咱们势必将迎来一场载入史册的绝妙大审判。”
这“审判”二字，犹如一记重锤彻底砸碎了普朗塔先生内心深处盘桓良久的迟疑退缩。
“咱们绝不能让这桩案子付诸公审审判！”他嘶声厉喊道。
老人突如其来的失态狂暴，令向来见惯了他端庄从容、波澜不惊的勒柯克先生大惊失色。
“哈哈，”侦探心下暗忖，“我终于要探知全盘底细了。”他随即朗声反问道：
“什么，不付诸审判？”
普朗塔先生的脸色瞬间惨白得如同一张白纸；他浑身剧烈颤抖，嗓音粗粝沙哑，仿佛被涌上喉头的呜咽抽泣硬生生碾碎了一般。
“为了免去一场公审，”他接着说道，“我甘愿倾家荡产奉献出我名下的全盘家资——哪怕是一个子儿也不剩，尽管那些家当也值不得几个钱。然而，咱们究竟该如何保全特雷莫雷尔这个恶徒免遭定罪制裁呢？咱们能编造出何等瞒天过海的托词诡计？我的挚友，唯有您一人，能在我深陷这般生不如死的绝境死地中为我指点迷津，助我成全心愿。倘若普天之下尚存一丝生路门径，您定能替我寻得并将我拉出苦海——”
“可是，我的——”
“请原谅——且听我把话说完，您自会明察我的苦衷。我即将向您掏心掏肺，正如我面对自己的灵魂般毫无保留；随后您便会彻底洞悉我何以如此犹疑迟决、何以守口如瓶，简而言之，彻底读懂自打命案东窗事发以来我全盘言行的真正根由。”
“洗耳恭听。”
“勒柯克，这是一段痛彻心扉的惨淡往事。当我行将步入世人所谓功成身退的晚年光景时，造化弄人，我竟在转瞬之间痛失了发妻与两个儿子——我在人世间的全部欢愉、全盘指望在顷刻间化作了泡影。我孑然一身沦落于茫茫尘世，比狂风暴雨中沉沦大海中央、连一根救命浮木都攀附不上的落难海难者还要无助迷惘。我犹如一具行尸走肉，直到机缘巧合之下，我才在奥尔西瓦尔定居扎下根来。

===Original===

There I saw Laurence; she was just fifteen, and never lived there a creature who united in herself so much intelligence, grace, innocence, and beauty. Courtois became my friend, and soon Laurence was like a daughter to me. I doubtless loved her then, but I did not confess it to myself, for I did not read my heart clearly. She was so young, and I had gray hairs! I persuaded myself that my love for her was like that of a father, and it was as a father that she cherished me. Ah, I passed many a delicious hour listening to her gentle prattle and her innocent confidences; I was happy when I saw her skipping about in my garden, picking the roses I had reared for her, and laying waste my parterres; and I said to myself that existence is a precious gift from God. My dream then was to follow her through life. I fancied her wedded to some good man who made her happy, while I remained the friend of the wife, after having been the confidant of the maiden. I took good care of my fortune, which is considerable, because I thought of her children, and wished to hoard up treasures for them. Poor, poor Laurence!”
M. Lecoq fidgeted in his chair, rubbed his face with his handkerchief, and seemed ill at ease. He was really much more touched than he wished to appear.
“One day,” pursued the old man, “my friend Courtois spoke to me of her marriage with Trémorel; then I measured the depth of my love. I felt terrible agonies which it is impossible to describe; it was like a long-smothered fire which suddenly breaks forth and devours everything. To be old, and to love a child! I thought I was going crazy; I tried to reason, to upbraid myself, but it was of no avail. What can reason or irony do against passion? I kept silent and suffered. To crown all, Laurence selected me as her confidant﻿—what torture! She came to me to talk of Hector; she admired in him all that seemed to her superior to other men, so that none could be compared with him. She was enchanted with his bold horseback riding, and thought everything he said sublime.”
“Did you know what a wretch Trémorel was?”
“Alas, I did not yet know it. What was this man who lived at Valfeuillu to me? But from the day that I learned that he was going to deprive me of my most precious treasure, I began to study him.

===Chinese===

就是在那里，我初次见到了洛朗斯；当时她年方15岁，天下从未有过任何一个生灵，能将如此非凡的聪慧灵秀、风姿绰约、天真无邪与绝代风华集于一身。库尔图瓦与我结成了莫逆之交，不多时，洛朗斯在我眼中便宛如亲生闺女一般。毋庸讳言，彼时我便已对她情根深种，但我绝不敢向自己的内心坦白认领，只因我当时尚未看清自己的心扉。她正值豆蔻年华，而我已是两鬓如霜！我千方百计说服自己，我对她的深情纯属慈父之爱，而她对我亦如对待严父般敬重依恋。啊，倾听着她银铃般娇憨的絮语呢喃、倾听着她毫无城府的闺阁密语，我不知度过了多少销魂蚀骨的甘美光阴；每逢瞧见她在我的花园里欢快雀跃穿梭跳跃，采摘我专程为她精心培育的名贵玫瑰，将我的花坛践踏得一片狼藉，我心头便洋溢着无边的幸福；我暗自感喟造化生灵乃是上帝赐予人间的无上恩典。彼时我毕生的绮梦，无非是能在漫漫人生长路上默默庇护她一生。我憧憬着有朝一日她能寻得一位敦厚良善的如意郎君，缔结良缘永浴爱河；而我则在充当了这怀春少女的知心长辈之后，继续充当这位贤妻良母的挚友至交。我悉心打理积攒着自己的家当产业——那是一笔为数相当可观的万贯家资——全因我心中时刻挂念着她未来的子嗣儿女，渴望为她的血脉聚敛起一座金山银山。可怜的、可怜至极的洛朗斯啊！”
勒柯克先生在座椅上如坐针毡般挪动着身躯，掏出方巾擦拭着面颊，显得极不自在。他内心遭受的强烈触动，远超他表面上试图伪装出的平静漠然。
“终于有一天，”老人继续倾诉道，“我的挚友库尔图瓦向我提起了她与特雷莫雷尔商议婚嫁之事；那一刻，我才真正丈量出了自己心底那份深渊般绝望爱意的深浅。我感受到了无法用言语描摹的酷刑煎熬；那宛如深埋地底、压抑良久的岩浆地火，在一瞬间喷涌爆发，将我周身的一切焚烧殆尽。老朽残躯，竟无可救药地爱上了一个豆蔻少女！我一度以为自己快要发疯癫狂了；我试图诉诸理智，千百次痛斥鞭笞自己的荒唐无耻，然而一切皆属徒劳。面对排山倒海的狂乱激情，理智与讥嘲又能顶什么用？我唯有咬紧牙关，在死寂中默默承受这无休止的凌迟。更具毁灭性的是，洛朗斯偏偏挑中了我充当她的知心倾诉对象——那是何等生不如死的诛心折磨啊！她频频跑来向我大谈特谈埃克托的一切；在她眼里，那男人身上的一切皆凌驾于凡夫俗子之上，举目四海再无一人堪与比肩。她为他纵马驰骋的矫健身姿神魂颠倒，将他嘴里吐出的每一句混账话奉为金科玉律。”
“您当时可知晓特雷莫雷尔究竟是个何等下作无耻的恶徒？”
“唉，当时我尚蒙在鼓里。那个蛰居在瓦尔菲伊的贵胄于我何干？然而自打得知他即将从我怀中夺走我生命中最无价的至宝那一刻起，我便开始暗中对他展开了掘地三尺的详尽摸底。

===Original===

I should have been somewhat consoled if I had found him worthy of her; so I dogged him, as you, Monsieur Lecoq, cling to the criminal whom you are pursuing. I went often to Paris to learn what I could of his past life; I became a detective, and went about questioning everybody who had known him, and the more I heard of him the more I despised him. It was thus that I found out his interviews with Jenny and his relations with Bertha.”
“Why didn’t you divulge them?”
“Honor commanded silence. Had I a right to dishonor my friend and ruin his happiness and life, because of this ridiculous, hopeless love? I kept my own counsel after speaking to Courtois about Jenny, at which he only laughed. When I hinted something against Hector to Laurence, she almost ceased coming to see me.”
“Ah! I shouldn’t have had either your patience or your generosity.”
“Because you are not as old as I, Monsieur Lecoq. Oh, I cruelly hated this Trémorel! I said to myself, when I saw three women of such different characters smitten with him, ‘what is there in him to be so loved?’ ”
“Yes,” answered M. Lecoq, responding to a secret thought, “women often err; they don’t judge men as we do.”
“Many a time,” resumed the justice of the peace, “I thought of provoking him to fight with me, that I might kill him; but then Laurence would not have looked at me any more. However, I should perhaps have spoken at last, had not Sauvresy fallen ill and died. I knew that he had made his wife and Trémorel swear to marry each other; I knew that a terrible reason forced them to keep their oath; and I thought Laurence saved. Alas, on the contrary she was lost! One evening, as I was passing the mayor’s house, I saw a man getting over the wall into the garden; it was Trémorel. I recognized him perfectly. I was beside myself with rage, and swore that I would wait and murder him. I did wait, but he did not come out that night.”
M. Plantat hid his face in his hands; his heart bled at the recollection of that night of anguish, the whole of which he had passed in waiting for a man in order to kill him. M. Lecoq trembled with indignation.
“This Trémorel,” cried he, “is the most abominable of scoundrels. There is no excuse for his infamies and crimes. And yet you want to save him from trial, the galleys, the scaffold which await him.”
The old man paused a moment before replying.

===Chinese===

倘若我查明他配得上洛朗斯，我心头或许还能捞得几分慰藉；于是我如影随形死死咬住他的尾巴，正如您，勒柯克先生，死死咬住您正在追踪缉拿的凶犯一般。我频繁奔波于巴黎，竭尽所能打探他过往的荒唐底细；我自己化身成了一名刑侦密探，四处奔走盘问每一个曾与他相识的熟人，而我听到的污糟勾当越多，我便越发对他嗤之以鼻、深恶痛绝。正是在此期间，我探查到了他同珍妮的幽会私通，以及他同贝尔塔之间暗通款曲的苟且私情。”
“那您当时为何不公之于众呢？”
“体面名节勒令我守口如瓶。难道仅仅为了我那见不得光、荒唐透顶的绝望痴恋，我便有权当众玷污挚友的名誉、亲手毁掉他满门的幸福与生计吗？我曾向库尔图瓦含蓄敲打提起过珍妮其人，岂料他不过是一笑了之；碰壁之后我便彻底三缄其口了。每逢我试图向洛朗斯隐晦暗示埃克托的人品卑劣，她便几乎同我彻底绝交、不再登门走动了。”
“哈！换作我，绝无您这般坚忍的耐性与博大的宽仁。”
“那是因为您尚年轻，勒柯克先生，未曾经历过我这般年岁的沧桑。噢，我对这个特雷莫雷尔简直恨之入骨！每逢目睹3位性格迥异的女子皆对他死心塌地神魂颠倒，我常常忍不住自问：‘这厮身上究竟有何等魔力，竟能博得天下女子这般倾心相许？’”
“确实，”勒柯克先生顺着他心底的隐秘思绪回应道，“女子往往盲目犯浑；她们评判男人的标尺，同你我完全南辕北辙。”
“不知有多少回，”治安法官接着倾诉道，“我曾动过找借口向他提出决斗、亲手将他一枪毙命的念头；可如此一来，洛朗斯此生便绝不会再正眼瞧我一眼了。然而，倘若不是索夫勒齐暴毙夭亡，我或许终究会撕破脸皮揭开画皮。我知道索夫勒齐生前逼迫发妻与特雷莫雷尔立下结发夫妻的重誓；我知道有一桩骇人听闻的绝密缘由逼得他们不得不履行婚约；我本以为洛朗斯终于彻底化险为夷得救了。唉，岂料恰恰相反，她自此彻底堕入了万劫不复的深渊！有一天傍晚，当我路过镇长宅邸门前时，冷不丁瞧见一个黑影翻越院墙潜入了花园；那正是特雷莫雷尔。我绝不可能认错人。那一刻我怒火攻心几近疯癫，咬牙切齿对天发誓定要在此死守到底亲手宰了他。我确实彻夜死守不退，然而那一夜他却自始至终未曾走出大门一步。”
普朗塔先生双手掩面；回想起那个漫长而充满绞心痛苦的喋血长夜，回想起自己为了手刃情敌在黑暗中空耗了一整夜的绝望狂乱，他的心在滴血。勒柯克先生听得义愤填膺，浑身战栗。
“这个特雷莫雷尔，”侦探厉声斥道，“堪称普天之下最丧尽天良的魔鬼。他的卑劣罪孽无论如何皆死有余辜。然而您眼下竟然想要保全他免遭法庭公审，免受苦役流放，免登原本早已为他备好的断头台！”
老人默然良久，未置可否。

===Original===

Of the thoughts which now crowded tumultuously in his mind, he did not know which to utter first. Words seemed powerless to betray his sensations; he wanted to express all that he felt in a single sentence.
“What matters Trémorel to me?” said he at last. “Do you think I care about him? I don’t care whether he lives or dies, whether he succeeds in flying or ends his life some morning in the Place Roquette.”
“Then why have you such a horror of a trial?”
“Because﻿—”
“Are you a friend to his family, and anxious to preserve the great name which he has covered with mud and devoted to infamy?”
“No, but I am anxious for Laurence, my friend; the thought of *her* never leaves me.”
“But she is not his accomplice; she is totally ignorant﻿—there’s no doubt of it﻿—that he has killed his wife.”
